satisfy the conditions of
Lemma

5.7
.

It is clear that
\mathcal{R}(u)
is nonempty, because
u\in\mathcal{U}
. It is immediate to verify that

\mathcal{R}(u)
is convex as well. We now check that
\mathcal{R}(u)
is closed in
E
. Let
\big((u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be any sequence in
\mathcal{R}(u)
that converges in
E
to some

(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in E
; we would like to show that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
.
First, since
(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\to(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})
in
E
, we have
u_{n}^{(1)}\to u^{(1)}
in
L^{2}(I)
and
u_{n}^{(2)}\to u^{(2)}
in
H^{1}(I)
and hence in
L^{2}(I)
, so that

\displaystyle\|u-(u^{(1)}+u^{(2)})\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|(u-(u_{n}^{(1)}+u_{n}^{(2)}))+(u_{n}^{(1)}-u^{(1)})+(u_{n}^{(2)}-u^{(2)})\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|u-(u_{n}^{(1)}+u_{n}^{(2)})\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|u_{n}^{(1)}-u^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}+\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|u_{n}^{(2)}-u^{(2)}\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\qquad=0\,,

where the last equality used the fact that
u=u_{n}^{(1)}+u_{n}^{(2)}
a. e. for every
n

(since
(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
) and the two convergence observations above. Thus

u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
and hence almost everywhere. Moreover, by

Lemma

A.1
,
u^{(1)}
is nondecreasing and
u^{(2)}
is nonincreasing.
Finally, since
\int_{I}u_{n}^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
for each
n
and
u_{n}^{(2)}\to u^{(2)}
in

L^{2}(I)
, it follows that
\int_{I}u^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
as well. We conclude that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
, and thus
\mathcal{R}(u)
is closed.

Now, clearly
\mathcal{D}^{-}
is proper since it is finitely valued by definition. It is also continuous
(and thus lower semicontinuous) because for any
(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\to(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})
in
E
, we have
u_{n}^{(2)}\to u^{(2)}
in
H^{1}(I)
and hence
\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\to\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x}u^{(2)}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, so
\mathcal{D}^{-}(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\to\mathcal{D}^{-}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})
. It is also immediate to
check that that
\mathcal{D}^{-}
is convex.

Finally, we need to show that
\mathcal{D}^{-}
is coercive in the sense of

Lemma

5.7
. Let
\big((u_{n}^{(1)}u_{n}^{(2)})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
sequence in
\mathcal{R}(u)
such that
\left\|(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)})\right\|_{E}^{2}=\|u_{n}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}\to\infty
. We claim that
\|\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
. Suppose for a contradiction that this false. There are two
cases. First, suppose
\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}(I)}\to\infty
. Then necessarily

\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
, but since each
u_{n}^{(2)}
has mean zero, the
Poincaré-Wirtinger inequality gives that

\|\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\geq\frac{1}{C}\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}

for some constant
C>0
. Thus
\|\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
, a
contradiction. In the second case,
\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}(I)}
remains bounded, so we must have

\|u_{n}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
. But since
u=u_{n}^{(1)}+u_{n}^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
, the
reverse triangle inequality implies that
\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\geq\|u_{n}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}-\|u\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
, so
\|u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
. Then again
Poincaré-Wirtinger implies that
\|\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\infty
, so the claim
holds. But since
\mathcal{D}^{-}\left(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{x}u_{n}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
, we have
\mathcal{D}^{-}\left(u_{n}^{(1)},u_{n}^{(2)}\right)\to+\infty
, so

\mathcal{D}^{-}
is coercive. Thus
\mathcal{D}^{-}
achieves its minimum on
\mathcal{R}(u)
by

Lemma

5.7
, so
\mathcal{R}^{*}(u)
is nonempty.
∎

Proof of uniqueness.

We show uniqueness using strict convexity. Suppose
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)}),(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}^{*}(u)
, and suppose for a contradiction that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\neq(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
in
E
(in the notation from the previous part of the proof; note that equality in

E
is equivalent to equality as tuples in
L^{2}(I)\times H^{1}(I)
).

We first claim that
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}\neq\partial_{x}v^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I
). Indeed, suppose

\partial_{x}u^{(2)}=\partial_{x}v^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
. Note that
u^{(2)}\neq v^{(2)}
in

L^{2}(I)
, because otherwise we would have
u^{(2)}=v^{(2)}
in
H^{1}(I)
and
u^{(1)}=u-u^{(2)}=u-v^{(2)}=v^{(1)}
in
L^{2}(I)
, and thus
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})=(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
in
E
, a contradiction. Now, since
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}=\partial_{x}v^{(2)}
and

u^{(2)}\neq v^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
, we conclude that
u^{(2)}=v^{(2)}+C
in
L^{2}(I)
for
some constant
C\neq 0
. But this contradicts the fact that
\int_{I}u^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}v^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
, which holds by the definition of
\mathcal{R}(u)
. Thus
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}\neq\partial_{x}v^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
.

Define the function
f:L^{2}(I)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by
f(w)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\|w\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
,
which is strictly convex, and note that
\mathcal{D}^{-}(r^{(1)},r^{(2)})=f(\partial_{x}r^{(2)})
for
all
r\in\mathcal{R}(u)
. Now, the element
(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})+\frac{1}{2}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
is in
\mathcal{R}(u)
by convexity of that set, and
satisfies
\mathcal{D}^{-}(z^{(1)},z^{(2)})=f\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}u^{(2)}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}v^{(2)}\right)<\frac{1}{2}f(\partial_{x}u^{(2)})+\frac{1}{2}f(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}^{-}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}^{-}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
,
contradicting the fact that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)}),(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}^{*}(u)
. Thus

(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})=(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
in
E
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 5.7

(See e. g.
[
Bre11
, Corollary 3.23]
)
.

Let
E
be a reflexive Banach space and let
C\subseteq E
be nonempty, closed and convex.
Suppose
f:C\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is convex, proper, lower semicontinuous, and coercive in
the sense that for every sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C
with
\|x_{n}\|_{E}\to\infty
, we
have
f(x_{n})\to+\infty
. Then there exists
x^{*}\in C
satisfying
f(x^{*})=\inf_{x\in C}f(x)
.

Owing to
Proposition

5.6
, we may define for each
u\in\mathcal{U}
a canonical
representation and directed Dirichlet energy (by a slight abuse of notation):

Definition 5.8

(Canonical representation)
.

For each
u\in\mathcal{U}
, the
canonical representation
of
u
is the unique element of

\mathcal{R}^{*}(u)
, denoted
(u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow})
. We define the
directed Dirichlet energy
of

u
by
\mathcal{D}^{-}(u)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{D}^{-}(u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow})
.

The following definition, which extends the directed Dirichlet energy functional to all of
L^{2}(I)

by assigning
+\infty
to functions outside of
\mathcal{U}
, will enable us to find and study a solution to
our PDE using tools from the theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows. Our main
reference for this theory is
[
Bre73
]
.

Definition 5.9

(Energy functional)
.

Define the functional
\mathcal{E}^{-}:L^{2}(I)\to[0,+\infty]
by

\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}^{-}(u)&\text{if }u\in\mathcal{U}\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proposition 5.10
.

The functional
\mathcal{E}^{-}:L^{2}(I)\to[0,+\infty]
is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

Properness is trivial, since e. g.
0\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}(0)=0
. Convexity also follows
easily from the convexity of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{D}^{-}
.

It remains to show lower semicontinuity, and since
L^{2}(I)
is a metric space, it suffices to
show sequential lower semicontinuity. Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
, and let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
sequence in
L^{2}(I)
converging to
u
in
L^{2}(I)
. We must show that

\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{n})\,.

(9)

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subsequence

(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{n_{k}})=A<+\infty
. By
extracting a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{n_{k}})<+\infty
, and thus

u_{n_{k}}\in\mathcal{U}
, for every
k
.

We claim that
\big((u_{n_{k}}{\uparrow},u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})\big)_{k}
is bounded as a sequence
in
L^{2}(I)\oplus H^{1}(I)
. First, since
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{n_{k}})=A
, we have that

\left(\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)_{k}
is bounded. Then, since every

u_{n_{k}}\in\mathcal{U}
and hence
\int_{I}u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
, the Poincaré-Wirtinger
inequality implies that

\int_{I}(u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

for some
C>0
, and hence
(u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})_{k}
is bounded in
H^{1}(I)
. Now, since
u_{n_{k}}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
, we have that
\left(\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\right)_{k}
is bounded, and since

\left(\|u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\right)_{k}
is bounded as a consequence of boundedness in

H^{1}(I)
, it follows that
\left(\|u_{n_{k}}{\uparrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\right)_{k}=\left(\|u_{n_{k}}-u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\right)_{k}
is also bounded, that is,
(u_{n_{k}}{\uparrow})_{k}
is bounded
in
L^{2}(I)
. Hence
\big((u_{n_{k}}{\uparrow},u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})\big)_{k}
is bounded in
L^{2}(I)\oplus H^{1}(I)
as claimed.

Since
L^{2}(I)\oplus H^{1}(I)
is a Hilbert space, we conclude that
\big((u_{n_{k}}{\uparrow},u_{n_{k}}{\downarrow})\big)_{k}
has a weakly convergent subsequence, which we denote by
\big((u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\uparrow},u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow})\big)_{m}
. Then there exist
v^{(1)}\in L^{2}(I)
and

v^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)
such that
u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\uparrow}\rightharpoonup v^{(1)}
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
and

u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\rightharpoonup v^{(2)}
weakly in
H^{1}(I)
. So letting
v\vcentcolon=v^{(1)}+v^{(2)}
, we conclude that
u_{n_{k_{m}}}\rightharpoonup v
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
; indeed, for every
f\in L^{2}(I)
, we have

\left\langle u_{n_{k_{m}}},f\right\rangle=\left\langle u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\uparrow}+u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow},f\right\rangle=\left\langle u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\uparrow},f\right\rangle+\left\langle u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow},f\right\rangle\to\left\langle v^{(1)},f\right\rangle+\left\langle v^{(2)},f\right\rangle=\left\langle v,f\right\rangle\,.

We now claim that
v\in\mathcal{U}
. Indeed, by
Lemma

A.1
,
v^{(1)}
is
nondecreasing and
v^{(2)}
is nonincreasing, and moreover
\int_{I}v^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
by
weak convergence; hence
(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(v)
. Then, since
u_{n_{k_{m}}}\rightharpoonup v

weakly in
L^{2}(I)
and
u_{n_{k_{m}}}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
, we conclude that
u=v
, so
u\in\mathcal{U}

with
(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
. In particular, this means that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

Now, the fact that
u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\rightharpoonup v^{(2)}
weakly in
H^{1}(I)
implies that

\|v^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}(I)}\leq\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\|_{H^{1}(I)}
. Moreover,
since
H^{1}(I)
embeds compactly into
L^{2}(I)
by the Rellich-Kondrachov theorem (see e. g.
[
Bre11
, Theorem 9.16]
), we have that
u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\to v^{(2)}
in
L^{2}(I)
, so

\|u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\to\|v^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
. Therefore we obtain

\displaystyle\liminf_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{n_{k_{m}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\liminf_{m\to\infty}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\liminf_{m\to\infty}\left(\|u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}-\|u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|u_{n_{k_{m}}}{\downarrow}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\|v^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\|v^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}-\|v^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,,

and thus (
9
) holds.
∎

Definition 5.11

(Static Neumann problem)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
and
z\in L^{2}(I)
. We say that
u,z
form a
weak solution
to the

static Neumann problem

\begin{cases}z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}&\text{in }I\\
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0&\text{on }\{0,1\}\end{cases}

(10)

if for all
\phi\in H^{1}(I)
we have

\int_{I}z\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}\phi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

(11)

Remark 5.12
.

The equation
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
in (
10
) is also
called the Poisson equation, while
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
is called the
(homogeneous) Neumann boundary condition.

Definition 5.13

(Nice evolution function)
.

Let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
and
\bm{u^{\prime}}:(0,+\infty)\to L^{2}(I)
. We say that

\bm{u}
is a
nice evolution function
(with weak derivative
\bm{u^{\prime}}
) if
1)
\bm{u}(t)=\bm{u}(0)+\int_{(0,t)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s
for all
t>0
;
2)
\bm{u^{\prime}}_{(\delta,+\infty)}\in L^{\infty}(\delta,+\infty;L^{2}(I))
for all
\delta>0
,
where
\bm{u^{\prime}}_{J}
denotes the restriction of
\bm{u^{\prime}}
to domain
J\subseteq(0,+\infty)
; and
3)
\bm{u^{\prime}}_{(0,\delta)}\in L^{2}(0,\delta;L^{2}(I))
for all
\delta>0
.

Remark 5.14
.

Definition

5.13
captures functions that are Lipschitz away from zero, and
absolutely continuous with square-integrable weak derivative near zero.

Definition 5.15

(Neumann evolution problem)
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
be a nice evolution function. We
say
\bm{u}
is a
weak solution
to the
Neumann evolution problem
with initial
state (or initial data)
u_{0}
if 1)
\bm{u}(0)=u_{0}
; 2)
\bm{u}(t)\in\mathcal{U}
for all
t>0
;
and 3)
\bm{u}(t),\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem for a. e.
t>0
.

Before introducing the gradient flow problem, we need some notation. Let
\varphi:L^{2}(I)\to[0,+\infty]
be a convex, proper, lower semicontinuous function. We write
D(\varphi)\vcentcolon=\{u\in L^{2}(I):\varphi(u)<+\infty\}
for the
domain
of
\varphi
. For any
u\in L^{2}(I)
, we
write define the
subdifferential
of
\varphi
at
u
as

\partial\varphi(u)\vcentcolon=\{z\in L^{2}(I):\forall v\in L^{2}(I)\,.\,\varphi(v)\geq\varphi(u)+\left\langle z,v-u\right\rangle\}\,,

and we write
D(\partial\varphi)\vcentcolon=\{u\in L^{2}(I):\partial\varphi(u)\neq\emptyset\}

for the
domain
of
\partial\varphi
. Using the fact that
\varphi
is proper, it is easy to
check that
D(\partial\varphi)\subseteq D(\varphi)
. It is standard that
\partial\varphi
is a

maximal monotone operator
; we will not use (the definition of) this property explicitly, but
rather rely on the theory of such operators as presented in
[
Bre73
]
.

We can now define the gradient flow problem:

Definition 5.16

(Gradient flow problem)
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\bm{u}
be a nice evolution function. We say
\bm{u}
is a

solution
to the
gradient flow problem
with initial state (or initial data)
u_{0}

if 1)
\bm{u}(0)=u_{0}
; 2)
\bm{u}(t)\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
for all
t>0
; and
3)
\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))
for a. e.
t>0
.

Note that in both
Definitions

5.15
and
5.16
, the pointwise condition at
t=0

makes sense by the requirement that
\bm{u}
be continuous.

The theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows immediately yields that the gradient
flow problem has a unique solution, as follows:

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
. Then there exists a unique solution
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
to
the gradient flow problem. Moreover, for all
t>0
we have

\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{0})-\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))=\int_{(0,t)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\,.

For
u_{0},v_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
, the corresponding solutions
\bm{u},\bm{v}
have
\|\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|u_{0}-v_{0}\|_{L^{2}(I)}
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

This is a direct application of
[
Bre73
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 and Proposition 3.1]
.
∎

Directed heat semigroup.

As described in
[
Bre73
]
, the nonexpansive property of solutions
\bm{u}
allows us to define an
operator
P_{t}:L^{2}(I)\to L^{2}(I)
such that
P_{t}u=\bm{u}(t)
for each initial state
u\in\mathcal{U}

with corresponding solution
\bm{u}
, and with
P_{t}u
defined by continuous extension when
u\in L^{2}(I)\setminus\mathcal{U}
(recall that
\mathcal{U}
is dense in
L^{2}(I)
). Then for every
u\in L^{2}(I)
and
t>0
it holds that
P_{t}u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
, and
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
forms a nonexpansive
semigroup in
L^{2}(I)
, i. e.

1.

P_{t+s}=P_{t}P_{s}
for all
t,s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, with
P_{0}
the identity;

2.

P_{t}u\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
as
t\downarrow 0
, for all
u\in L^{2}(I)
; and

3.

\|P_{t}u-P_{t}v\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|u-v\|_{L^{2}(I)}
for all
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

The last property also implies that, for each
t\geq 0
,
P_{t}:L^{2}(I)\to L^{2}(I)
is continuous. We
say that
P_{t}
is the semigroup
generated
by
-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}
.

It turns out that the solution to the gradient flow problem is also a weak solution to the Neumann
evolution problem, as the following results show.

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
and
z\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Then
u,z
form a weak solution
to the static Neumann problem.

Proof.

Note that we have
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})\subseteq D(\mathcal{E}^{-})=\mathcal{U}
. Let
\phi\in H^{1}(I)
; we
will prove that (
11
) holds. Let
\alpha\neq 0
, let
\psi_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\alpha\phi
and let
v_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=u+\psi_{\alpha}
. Since
-z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
, we
have

\mathcal{E}^{-}(v_{\alpha})\geq\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle-z,v_{\alpha}-u\right\rangle=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle-z,\psi_{\alpha}\right\rangle\,.

(12)

Now, the fact that
u\in\mathcal{U}
implies that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=\mathcal{D}^{-}(u)
. We now claim that
v_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{U}
as well, which will imply that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(v_{\alpha})=\mathcal{D}^{-}(v_{\alpha})
. In fact, we
establish the following:

Claim 5.19
.

We have
v_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{U}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{D}^{-}(v_{\alpha})\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}+\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

Proof.

We define a nondecreasing function
v^{(1)}\in L^{2}(I)
and a nonincreasing function

v^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)
as follows: for each
x\in I
,

\displaystyle v^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=u{\uparrow}(x)+\psi_{\alpha}(0)+\int_{(0,x)}\big(\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(y)-\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(y)\rvert\big)^{+}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle v^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=u{\downarrow}(x)+\int_{(0,x)}\big(\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(y)\land\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(y)\rvert\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,.

It is clear that
v^{(1)},v^{(2)}\in L^{2}(I)
with
v^{(1)}
nondecreasing. Since

u{\downarrow}\in H^{1}(I)
by definition of
\mathcal{R}(u)
, while
\psi_{\alpha}\in H^{1}(I)
because

\phi\in H^{1}(I)
, we also obtain that
v^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)
. To see that
v^{(2)}
is
nonincreasing, note that for a. e.
x\in I
we have

\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)=\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)+\big(\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x)\land\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\rvert\big)\leq 0\,.

Observe also that
v^{(1)}+v^{(2)}=u+\psi_{\alpha}=v_{\alpha}
. Finally, by
translating
v^{(1)}
and
v^{(2)}
by a constant if necessary, we can ensure that
\int_{I}v^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
without invalidating the other properties. Thus
v_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{U}
.

To show the second part of the claim, we note that for a. e.
x\in I
,

\Big|\underbrace{\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)}_{\leq 0}\Big|=\Big|\underbrace{\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)}_{\leq 0}+\underbrace{\big(\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x)\land\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\rvert\big)}_{\leq\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\rvert}\Big|\leq\Big|\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)+\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x)\Big|\,,

where the inequality follows from inspecting the cases
\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x)\leq\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\rvert
and
\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x)>\lvert\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\rvert
. We conclude that

\mathcal{D}^{-}(v_{\alpha})\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)+\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha}(x))^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

Now, putting together (
12
) and the claim, we get

\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{I}z\psi_{\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}(v_{\alpha})\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}+\partial_{x}\psi_{\alpha})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Simplifying and substituting
\psi_{\alpha}=\alpha\phi
, we obtain

\alpha\int_{I}z\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\geq-\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\alpha\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}\phi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Taking the limits
\alpha\to 0^{+}
and
\alpha\to 0^{-}
, we conclude that

\int_{I}z\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}\phi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

which is (
11
) as needed.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and suppose
\bm{u}
is a solution to the gradient flow problem. Then
\bm{u}

is also a weak solution to the Neumann evolution problem.

Proof.

The condition
\bm{u}(0)=u_{0}
holds by definition of solution to the gradient flow problem,
and for all
t>0
we have
\bm{u}(t)\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})\subseteq D(\mathcal{E}^{-})=\mathcal{U}
. Finally,
for a. e.
t>0
we have
\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))
, which by

Proposition

5.18
implies that
\bm{u}(t),\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
form a
solution to the static Neumann problem. Hence
\bm{u}
is a weak solution to the Neumann
evolution problem.
∎

5.3
Exponential decay of directed Dirichlet energy

The following elliptic regularity result is standard, and essentially shows that weak solutions to
the static Neumann problem are in fact strong solutions whose regularity is two degrees higher than
that of
z
; in particular, even if we only have
z\in L^{2}(I)
, we gain one degree of regularity by
obtaining
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
when we only assumed
u{\downarrow}\in H^{1}(I)
. Recall that
H^{0}(I)

is the same as
L^{2}(I)
.

Lemma 5.21

(Elliptic regularity; see
[
Mik78
, Chapter IV Section 2, Theorems 3 and 4]
)
.

Let
k\geq 0
be an integer and let
u\in\mathcal{U},z\in H^{k}(I)
form a weak solution to the static
Neumann problem

\begin{cases}z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}&\text{in }I\\
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0&\text{on }\{0,1\}\,.\end{cases}

Then
u{\downarrow}\in H^{k+2}(I)
, and moreover
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
and
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
a. e. in
I
.

We now give our energy decay result.

Proposition 5.22
.

There exists a constant
K>0
such that the following holds. Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Then for all
t>0
,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{t}u)\leq e^{-Kt}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,.

Proof.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\bm{u}(t)=P_{t}u
be the corresponding solution to the gradient flow
problem. Recall that
Proposition

5.17
gives, for all
t>0
,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\int_{(0,t)}-\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\,.

It follows that
t\mapsto\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))
is absolutely continuous on every interval
[0,T]

with weak derivative
\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))=-\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
. Moreover for
a. e.
t>0
we have that
\bm{u}(t),\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
form a weak solution to the static Neumann
problem, and thus
\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}
a. e. in
I
and

\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
by
Lemma

5.21
. Therefore
the Poincaré inequality (for zero-on-the-boundary functions) yields

\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))=\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}C\|\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}=\frac{C}{2}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}

for some constant
C>0
. It follows that for a. e.
t>0
, we have

\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))=-\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\leq-\frac{2}{C}\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))\,,

which implies that for all
t>0
,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))\leq e^{-2t/C}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,.\qed

The exponential decay of
t\mapsto\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{t}u)
allows us to find a Cauchy sequence in
(P_{t}u)_{t\geq 0}
, and thus establish its strong convergence to some limit in
L^{2}(I)
. Later on, we will say
more about this limit by reasoning about the weak convergence of
P_{t}u
(to the same limit).

Lemma 5.23

(Cauchy sequence)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
, and let
(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be given by
u_{k}\vcentcolon=P_{k}u
. Then
(u_{k})_{k}
is
Cauchy as a sequence in
L^{2}(I)
. As a consequence,
u_{k}\to u^{*}
in
L^{2}(I)
for some
u^{*}\in L^{2}(I)
.

Proof.

The existence of a strong limit from the Cauchy property follows from the fact that
L^{2}(I)
is
a complete normed space. Let us now establish the Cauchy property. Fix any
n>m
in
\mathbb{N}
.
Then

\displaystyle\|u_{n}-u_{m}\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{(m,n)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}

(Absolute continuity)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=m}^{n-1}\int_{(j,j+1)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(Triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=m}^{n-1}\left(\int_{(j,j+1)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=m}^{n-1}\left(\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{j})-\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{j+1})\right)^{1/2}

(
Proposition

5.17
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=m}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u_{j})^{1/2}

(
\mathcal{E}^{-}
is nonnegative)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=m}^{\infty}\left(e^{-Kj}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\right)^{1/2}

(
Proposition

5.22
)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}\frac{e^{-Km/2}}{1-e^{-K/2}}

(Geometric series)

\displaystyle=A\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}e^{-Bm}

for constants
A,B>0
that only depend on the constant
K
from

Proposition

5.22
. It follows that, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
n>m>N
,

\|u_{n}-u_{m}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq A\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}e^{-Bm}\leq A\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}e^{-BN}<\varepsilon\,,

so
(u_{k})_{k}
is Cauchy.
∎

Proposition 5.24

(Strong convergence)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
. Then
P_{t}u
converges in
L^{2}(I)
to some
u^{*}\in L^{2}(I)
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
u^{*}
be such that
(P_{k}u)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
u^{*}
in
L^{2}(I)
as
k\to\infty
,
as given by
Lemma

5.23
. We claim that
P_{t}u\to u^{*}
. Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
\|P_{k}u-u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(I)}<\varepsilon
for all
k\geq N
, as given by the
convergence of the sequence
(P_{k}u)_{k}
. Then for any
t\geq N
, letting
j\vcentcolon=\lfloor t\rfloor
,
we have

\|P_{t}u-u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|P_{j}u-u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(I)}+\|P_{t}u-P_{j}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}

by the triangle inequality. We have
\|P_{j}u-u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(I)}<\varepsilon
by the choice of
N
,
and on the other hand,

\displaystyle\|P_{t}u-P_{j}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{(j,t)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}

(Absolute continuity)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(j,t)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(I)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

(Triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq(t-j)^{1/2}\left(\int_{(j,t)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{j}u)-\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{t}u)\right)^{1/2}

(
t-j\in[0,1)
,
Proposition

5.17
)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-Kj/2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\text{($\mathcal{E}^{-}\geq 0$, \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{prop:exponential-decay})}\,.

Thus by letting
N
be large enough, we can ensure that

\|P_{t}u-P_{j}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq e^{-Kj/2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}\leq e^{-KN/2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)^{1/2}<\varepsilon\,,

and hence
\|P_{t}u-u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(I)}<2\varepsilon
. Thus
P_{t}u\to u^{*}
in
L^{2}(I)
.
∎

5.4
Auxiliary results for studying regularity of solutions

Lemma 5.25

(Functions in
D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
are well-behaved)
.

Suppose
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
. Let
(a,b)\in I
be a nonempty interval and suppose that

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<0
for all
x\in(a,b)
. Then
u{\uparrow}
is constant in
(a,b)
.

Proof.

Let
z\in L^{2}(I)
be such that
-z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
, which is nonempty by hypothesis.
By
Proposition

5.18
and
Lemma

5.21
,
we conclude that
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
(which in particular justifies writing the condition
that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}<0
on
(a,b)
, which we take to mean in terms of the continuous
representative of
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\in H^{1}(I)
).

By the continuity of
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
and the boundary conditions
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(0)=\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(1)=0
(which follows from
Lemma

5.21
), we may
assume without loss of generality (by extending the interval
(a,b)
if necessary) that

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)=\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(b)=0
.

Let

Z\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\inf_{[a,b)}u{\uparrow}&\text{if }a>0\\
\frac{1}{b-a}\int_{(a,b)}u{\uparrow}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x&\text{if }a=0\,,\end{cases}

which is finite in the first case because the infimum must be no smaller than (say)

u{\uparrow}(\frac{a}{2})
, and in the second case because
u{\uparrow}\in L^{2}(I)
. Next, define
v\in L^{2}(I)
by

v(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}u(x)&\text{if }x\in(0,a)\cup[b,1)\\
u{\downarrow}(x)+Z&\text{if }x\in[a,b)\,.\end{cases}

We first observe that
v\in\mathcal{U}
, since
v=v^{(1)}+v^{(2)}
with
v^{(2)}\vcentcolon=u{\downarrow}
and
v^{(1)}\in L^{2}(I)
given by

v^{(1)}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}u{\uparrow}(x)&\text{if }x\in(0,a)\cup[b,1)\\
Z&\text{if }x\in[a,b)\,,\end{cases}

which is nondecreasing because
u{\uparrow}
is and by the definition of
Z
. Therefore
v\in D(\mathcal{E}^{-})
and

\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{i}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{D}^{-}(u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow})=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,.

On the other hand, we claim that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)\geq\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
as well. Indeed, let
(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(v)
be arbitrary. Construct
u^{(1)}\in L^{2}(I)
and
u^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)
by

u^{(2)}\vcentcolon=w^{(2)}
and

u^{(1)}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}w^{(1)}(x)&\text{if }x\in(0,a)\cup[b,1)\\
u(x)-u^{(2)}(x)&\text{if }x\in[a,b)\,,\end{cases}

That
u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
is clear by construction. To show that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
, it remains to show that
u^{(1)}
is nondecreasing. Certainly it is nondecreasing on

(0,a)\cup[b,1)
since
w^{(1)}
is nondecreasing. Note that for
x\in[a,b)
, we have

u^{(1)}(x)=u(x)-w^{(2)}(x)-w^{(1)}(x)+w^{(1)}(x)=u(x)-v(x)+w^{(1)}(x)=u{\uparrow}(x)+w^{(1)}(x)-Z\,.

Thus
u^{(1)}
is nondecreasing on
[a,b)
, since
u{\uparrow}
and
w^{(1)}
are. For
x\in(0,a)
and
y\in[a,b)
, which only applies when
a>0
, the inequality
u^{(1)}(x)\leq u^{(1)}(y)
follows from the nondecreasing monotonicity of
w^{(1)}
and the inequality

u{\uparrow}(y)\geq Z
, which holds by the (first case of the) definition of
Z
.

Now, we must show that
u^{(1)}(x)\leq u^{(1)}(y)
if
x\in[a,b)
and
y\in[b,1)
. In
fact, it suffices to consider
y\in(b,1)
, because if the inequality holds in all such cases,
then
u^{(1)}
can be made monotone on all of
I
by possibly changing its value at
b
, which
does not affect the a. e. equality
u=u^{(1)}+u^{(2)}
. Therefore let
x\in[a,b)
and

y\in(b,1)
, which in particular only applies when
b<1
. Recalling that every monotone
function has limits from the left and from the right at every point, the key observation is that

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to b^{+}}w^{(1)}(s)-\lim_{s\to b^{-}}w^{(1)}(s)

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{s\to b^{+}}v(s)-w^{(2)}(s)\right]-\left[\lim_{s\to b^{-}}v(s)-w^{(2)}(s)\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{s\to b^{+}}u{\uparrow}(s)+u{\downarrow}(s)\right]-w^{(2)}(b)-\left[\lim_{s\to b^{-}}u{\downarrow}(s)+Z\right]+w^{(2)}(b)

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\to b^{+}}u{\uparrow}(s)-Z\,,

where we used the fact that
(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(v)
in the first equality, the
definition of
v
and continuity of
w^{(2)}
in the second equality, and the continuity of

u{\downarrow}
in the third equality. Now, we have

\displaystyle u^{(1)}(x)\leq u^{(1)}(y)

\displaystyle\iff u{\uparrow}(x)+u{\downarrow}(x)-w^{(2)}(x)+Z-Z\leq w^{(1)}(y)

\displaystyle\iff u{\uparrow}(x)+w^{(1)}(x)-Z\leq w^{(1)}(y)

\displaystyle\impliedby u{\uparrow}(x)+w^{(1)}(x)-Z\leq\lim_{s\to b^{+}}w^{(1)}(s)

\displaystyle\iff u{\uparrow}(x)+w^{(1)}(x)-Z\leq\lim_{s\to b^{-}}w^{(1)}(s)+\lim_{s\to b^{+}}u{\uparrow}(s)-Z

\displaystyle\impliedby u{\uparrow}(x)\leq\lim_{s\to b^{+}}u{\uparrow}(s)\quad\text{and}\quad w^{(1)}(x)\leq\lim_{s\to b^{-}}w^{(1)}(s)\,,

which is true because
u{\uparrow}
and
w^{(1)}
are nondecreasing and
x<b
. This establishes
that
u^{(1)}
is nondecreasing and thus
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
. It follows that

\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\leq\mathcal{D}^{-}(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}w^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{D}^{-}(w^{(1)},w^{(2)})\,,

and thus
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\leq\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
as claimed. Thus
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
, which in particular
implies that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)=\mathcal{D}^{-}(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
and hence
(v{\uparrow},v{\downarrow})=(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})
.

By definition of subdifferential, the fact that
-z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
implies that

\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)\geq\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle-z,v-u\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\left\langle z,v-u\right\rangle\,.

Since
v=u
on
I\setminus[a,b]
and
v{\downarrow}=u{\downarrow}
, we conclude that

0\leq\left\langle z,v-u\right\rangle=\left\langle z,v-u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}=\left\langle z,Z-u{\uparrow}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}\,.

Now, since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
a. e. and
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)=\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(b)=0
, we have

\left\langle z,Z\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}=Z\left\langle\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow},1\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}=Z(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(b)-\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a))=0\,,

and hence

\left\langle z,u{\uparrow}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}\leq 0\,.

Now, suppose for a contradiction that
u{\uparrow}
is not constant in
(a,b)
. Then since

u{\uparrow}
is nondecreasing, there must exist
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
with
a<a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}<b
and
u{\uparrow}(a^{\prime})<u{\uparrow}(b^{\prime})
, i. e.
\delta\vcentcolon=u{\uparrow}(b^{\prime})-u{\uparrow}(a^{\prime})>0
. Let
\alpha\vcentcolon=-\sup_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
, and note that
\alpha>0
by the extreme value theorem
together with the continuity of
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
and the fact that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}<0
in
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
. Then
Lemma

5.27
applied to
f=u{\uparrow}
,
g=\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
and
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in J=(a,b)
implies that
\left\langle z,u{\uparrow}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}=\left\langle\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow},u{\uparrow}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(a,b)}\geq\alpha\delta>0
, which is
the desired contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.26
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be a finite, nonempty open interval. Let
f\in L^{\infty}(J)
be
nondecreasing and let
g\in H^{1}(J)
be such that
g<0
in
J
and
g=0
on
\partial J
,
i. e. the endpoints of
J
. Let
[a,b]\subset J
, let
\delta\vcentcolon=f(b)-f(a)
and let

\alpha\vcentcolon=-\sup_{(a,b)}g
. Then
\left\langle f,\partial_{x}g\right\rangle_{L^{2}(J)}\geq\alpha\delta
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
J=I
.
Now, for each sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, recall that
I_{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon)
and let
f_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(I_{\varepsilon})
be the mollification of
f
. Let

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}:I\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}f_{\varepsilon/2}(\varepsilon)&\text{if }x\in(0,\varepsilon)\\
f_{\varepsilon/2}(x)&\text{if }x\in[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]\\
f_{\varepsilon/2}(1-\varepsilon)&\text{if }x\in(1-\varepsilon,1)\,.\end{cases}

Note that each
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\in H^{1}(I)
, in particular because the piecewise definition is
continuous and
f_{\varepsilon/2}
is smooth on
[\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon]
. Also, each

f_{\varepsilon/2}
is nondecreasing and hence so is each
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}
. Moreover,
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to f
a. e. as
\varepsilon\to 0
since this is true of
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
. Finally,
we have
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to f
in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I)
since
f_{\varepsilon}\to f
in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
,
and since
\|f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}\leq\|f_{\varepsilon/2}\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}
, we have that
(f_{\varepsilon}^{*})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{2}(I)
and

Lemma

A.2
implies that
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\rightharpoonup f
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
as

\varepsilon\to 0
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be two sequences such that
a_{n}\uparrow a
,
b_{n}\downarrow b
, and moreover, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(a_{n})\to f(a_{n})
and

f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(b_{n})\to f(b_{n})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
; the existence of such sequences is
guaranteed by the fact that
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\to f
almost everywhere. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\delta_{n}\vcentcolon=f(b_{n})-f(a_{n})
and
\alpha_{n}\vcentcolon=-\sup_{(a_{n},b_{n})}g
. Note that

\delta_{n}\geq\delta
because
f
is nondecreasing while
a_{n}\leq a
and
b\leq b_{n}
, and that

\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
as
n\to\infty
by the continuity of
g
.

For each
\varepsilon>0
, integration by parts gives

\left\langle f_{\varepsilon}^{*},\partial_{x}g\right\rangle=\int_{I}f_{\varepsilon}^{*}(\partial_{x}g)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\left.f_{\varepsilon}^{*}g\right|_{0}^{1}-\int_{I}(\partial_{x}f_{\varepsilon}^{*})g\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Recall that
g=0
on
\{0,1\}
. Moreover, since
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}
is nondecreasing while
g<0

in
I
, the integrand in the RHS above is nonpositive. Hence we can only make the RHS smaller by
restricting the range of integration. Thus, fixing any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and using the definition of

\alpha_{n}
,

\left\langle f_{\varepsilon}^{*},\partial_{x}g\right\rangle\geq-\int_{(a_{n},b_{n})}(\partial_{x}f_{\varepsilon}^{*})g\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\geq-\int_{(a_{n},b_{n})}(\partial_{x}f_{\varepsilon}^{*})(-\alpha_{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\alpha_{n}\left.f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\right|_{a_{n}}^{b_{n}}\,.

Since
f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\rightharpoonup f
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
, we obtain

\left\langle f,\partial_{x}g\right\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle f_{\varepsilon}^{*},\partial_{x}g\right\rangle\geq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\alpha_{n}\left.f_{\varepsilon}^{*}\right|_{a_{n}}^{b_{n}}=\alpha_{n}\delta_{n}\geq\alpha_{n}\delta\,,

the second equality by the choice of sequences
(a_{n})_{n},(b_{n})_{n}
and definition of
\delta_{n}
.
We conclude that

\left\langle f,\partial_{x}g\right\rangle\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\alpha_{n}\delta=\alpha\delta\,.\qed

Lemma 5.27
.

The statement of
Lemma

5.26
still holds if we replace the condition
f\in L^{\infty}(J)
with
f\in L^{2}(J)
.

Proof.

Again let
J=I
without loss of generality. We proceed by an approximation argument. Let

(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly decreasing sequence satisfying
a_{1}=a
and
a_{n}\to 0
.
Similarly, let
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly increasing sequence satisfying
b_{1}=b
and

b_{n}\to 1
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
f_{n}\in L^{\infty}(I)
by

f_{n}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\inf_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}f&\text{if }x\in(0,a_{n})\\
f(x)&\text{if }x\in[a_{n},b_{n}]\\
\sup_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}f&\text{if }x\in(b_{n},1)\,.\end{cases}

Note that the infimum and supremum above are finite by virtue of the monotonicity of
f
and the
observation that each
[a_{n},b_{n}]\subset I
; thus we indeed have
f_{n}\in L^{\infty}(I)
.
Moreover, each
f_{n}
is nondecreasing and, since
a_{n}\leq a<b\leq b_{n}
, we have
f_{n}(a)=f(a)
and
f_{n}(b)=f(b)
.
Lemma

5.26
implies that
\left\langle f_{n},\partial_{x}g\right\rangle\geq\alpha\delta
for
\alpha
as in that statement and
\delta=f_{n}(b)-f_{n}(a)=f(b)-f(a)
. Finally, we have
f_{n}\to f
in
L^{2}(I)
; indeed, letting
c\vcentcolon=\frac{a+b}{2}
for convenience and using the monotonicity of
f
,

\displaystyle\|f-f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{(0,a_{n})}\left(f(x)-\inf_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}f\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{(b_{n},1)}\left(f(x)-\sup_{[a_{n},b_{n}]}f\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(0,a_{n})}\left(f(x)-f(c)\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{(b_{n},1)}\left(f(x)-f(c)\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,a_{n})}^{2}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(b_{n},1)}^{2}+(a_{n}+1-b_{n})f(c)^{2}\right]\to 0\,,

the last step by the continuity of the functions
x\to\|f\|_{L^{2}(0,x)}^{2}
and
x\to\|f\|_{L^{2}(x,1)}^{2}
and the fact that
a_{n}+(1-b_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Hence

\left\langle f_{n},\partial_{x}g\right\rangle\to\left\langle f,\partial_{x}g\right\rangle
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

The following auxiliary result confirms the natural expectation that, if
u
has a first derivative
and some degree of regularity, then its components
u{\uparrow}
and
u{\downarrow}
enjoy the same
regularity and, moreover, can be obtained by taking the positive and negative parts of the
derivative
\partial_{x}u
, respectively.

Proposition 5.28
.

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and let
u\in\mathcal{U}\cap W^{1,p}(I)
. Then
u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow}\in W^{1,p}(I)
and, moreover, we have
\partial_{x}u{\uparrow}=\partial_{x}^{+}u
and
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=\partial_{x}^{-}u
a. e. in
I
.

Proof.

Let
u^{(1)},u^{(2)}:I\to\mathbb{R}
be given by

u^{(1)}(y)\vcentcolon=u(0)+C+\int_{(0,y)}\partial_{x}^{+}u(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{and}\qquad u^{(2)}(y)\vcentcolon=-C+\int_{(0,y)}\partial_{x}^{-}u(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

for each
y\in I
, where
C\in\mathbb{R}
is implicitly defined so as to satisfy

\int_{I}u^{(2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0\,.

Then
u^{(1)}
is nondecreasing,
u^{(2)}
is nonincreasing, both are absolutely continuous
with
\partial_{x}u^{(1)}=\partial_{x}^{+}u
and
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}=\partial_{x}^{-}u
a. e. in

I
, and

(u^{(1)}+u^{(2)})(y)=u(0)+\int_{(0,y)}\left(\partial_{x}^{+}u(x)+\partial_{x}^{-}u(x)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=u(x)

for all
y\in I
by the absolute continuity of
u
. It is also clear that
u^{(1)},u^{(2)}\in L^{\infty}(I)\subset L^{p}(I)
, since they are pointwise upper bounded in magnitude by

\left\lvert u(0)\right\rvert+\left\lvert C\right\rvert+\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{1}(I)}<+\infty
. Moreover, since
u\in W^{1,p}(I)
, we have
\partial_{x}u\in L^{p}(I)
and hence
\partial_{x}^{+}u,\partial_{x}^{-}u\in L^{p}(I)
, yielding that
u^{(1)},u^{(2)}\in W^{1,p}(I)
.

The proof will be concluded if we show that
(u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow})=(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})
.
However, at this point we cannot even state that
(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
because we
have not established that
u^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)=W^{1,2}(I)
(unless
p\geq 2
, of course).
However, if we can show that any
(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
satisfies

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}v^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\geq}}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u^{(2)})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

(13)

then using the assumption that
u\in\mathcal{U}
and the definition of
u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow}
, we will
conclude that indeed
(u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow})=(u^{(1)},u^{(2)})
, as needed.

Let
(v^{(1)},v^{(2)})\in\mathcal{R}(u)
. We claim that
\left\lvert\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert
for a. e.
x\in I
, which will imply (
13
). Recall
that
v^{(2)}\in H^{1}(I)\subset W^{1,1}(I)
. Since
u=v^{(1)}+v^{(2)}
a. e. and
u\in W^{1,p}(I)\subset W^{1,1}(I)
, we conclude that
v^{(1)}\in W^{1,1}(I)
. Hence
u,u^{(1)},u^{(2)},v^{(1)},v^{(2)}
are all absolutely continuous. The fundamental theorem of calculus
for the Lebesgue integral implies that, almost everywhere in
I
, these functions are all
differentiable, and their classical and weak derivatives agree with
\partial_{x}u=\partial_{x}u^{(1)}+\partial_{x}u^{(2)}=\partial_{x}v^{(1)}+\partial_{x}v^{(2)}
. On any such point

x
, the monotonicity of
u^{(1)},u^{(2)},v^{(1)},v^{(2)}
implies that
\partial_{x}u^{(1)}(x),\partial_{x}v^{(1)}(x)\geq 0
and
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x),\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)\leq 0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\left\lvert\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)=-\partial_{x}u(x)+\partial_{x}v^{(1)}(x)=-\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)-\partial_{x}u^{(1)}(x)+\partial_{x}v^{(1)}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert-\partial_{x}u^{(1)}(x)+\partial_{x}v^{(1)}(x)\,.

Now, if
\partial_{x}u(x)\geq 0
, then
\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)=0
by definition, so

\left\lvert\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert
holds trivially. Otherwise, we
conversely have
\partial_{x}u^{(1)}(x)=0
while
\partial_{x}v^{(1)}\geq 0
, and hence, by
the above,

\left\lvert\partial_{x}v^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\partial_{x}u^{(2)}(x)\right\rvert\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The following standard facts will also be useful.

Fact 5.29

(See e. g.
[
EG15
, Theorem 4.4]
)
.

Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
f\in W^{1,p}(I)
. Then
\partial_{x}f=0
a. e. on
\{f=0\}
.

The following fact is an immediate application of the Sobolev embedding theorem:

Fact 5.30
.

Let
f\in H^{2}(I)
. Then
f\in C^{1,1/2}(\overline{I})
. In particular,
f
is continuously
differentiable.

Fact 5.31

(See e. g.
[
Bar76
, Chapter II, Corollary 2.1]
)
.

The set
D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
is a dense subset of
D(\mathcal{E}^{-})=\mathcal{U}
.

Observation 5.32
.

Since
\mathcal{U}
contains
H^{1}(I)
, which is dense in
L^{2}(I)
,
Fact

5.31
implies that

\overline{D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})}=\overline{D(\mathcal{E}^{-})}=\overline{\mathcal{U}}=L^{2}(I)\,.

5.5
Preservation of
H^{1}
regularity

We wish to show that if the initial state
u
is in
H^{1}(I)
, then
P_{t}u
remains in
H^{1}(I)
for
all times
t>0
. To that end, define
\varphi:L^{2}(I)\to[0,+\infty]
by

\varphi(u)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}&\text{if }u\in H^{1}(I)\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The theory of maximal monotone operators gives us a recipe to establish that
t\mapsto\varphi(P_{t}u)
is nonincreasing. The key ingredients are

Claims

5.33
and
5.34
.

Claim 5.33
.

The functional
\varphi
is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicontinuity. Since

L^{2}(I)
is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity. Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
L^{2}(I)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
. We need to show that

\varphi(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\varphi(u_{n})\,.

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subsequence

(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\varphi(u_{n_{k}})=A<+\infty
. By
extracting a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
\varphi(u_{n_{k}})<+\infty
, and thus

u_{n_{k}}\in H^{1}(I)
, for every
k
.

We claim that
(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
is bounded in
H^{1}(I)
. Indeed suppose this is not the case. Then
since
\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}=\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\|\partial_{x}u_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
and

\|\partial_{x}u_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
remains bounded due to the fact that
\varphi(u_{n_{k}})\to A
, we conclude that
\|u_{n_{k}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}
gets arbitrarily large as
k\to\infty
. But this
contradicts the fact that
u_{n_{k}}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
, so the claim holds.

It follows that we may extract a weakly convergent subsequence
(u_{n_{k_{\ell}}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
, and by uniqueness of weak limits, we obtain that
u\in H^{1}(I)
and
u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup u
weakly in
H^{1}(I)
. By weak lower semicontinuity the norm in
H^{1}(I)
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}=\|u\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}\leq\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\|u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{H^{1}(I)}^{2}=\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\|u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\|\partial_{x}u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\,.

Since
u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
, we have
\|u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\to\|u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
and hence

\varphi(u)=\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\leq-\|u\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\|u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+\|\partial_{x}u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}=\liminf_{\ell\to\infty}\varphi(u_{n_{k_{\ell}}})\,.\qed

Lemma 5.34
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
and let
z\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Then for all
\lambda>0
,

\varphi(u-\lambda z)\geq\varphi(u)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

5.18
,
z
is a weak solution to the static Neumann
problem, and by
Lemma

5.21
we have
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
with
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
and
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
in
L^{2}(I)
.

Note that the result holds trivially if
u-\lambda z\not\in H^{1}(I)
, in which case
\varphi(u-\lambda z)=+\infty
. Therefore assume that
u-\lambda z\in H^{1}(I)
. We consider four
cases:
u\in H^{1}(I)
;
u\in W^{1,1}(I)\setminus H^{1}(I)
, i. e.
u
is AC but not in
H^{1}(I)
;

u
is continuous but not AC; and
u
is not continuous.
8
8

8

As usual, phrases such as
“
u
is continuous” should be understood as “the object
u\in L^{2}(I)
has a continuous
representative”, and in particular the condition “
u
is continuous but not AC” makes sense
because the continuous representative, if it exists, is unique.

Case 1.

Suppose
u\in H^{1}(I)
. Note that in this case the assumption that
u-\lambda z\in H^{1}(I)
implies that
z\in H^{1}(I)
as well, so in particular
\partial_{x}u,\partial_{x}z\in L^{2}(I)
. Additionally,
Lemma

5.21
also yields
u{\downarrow}\in H^{3}(I)
. We have

\varphi(u-\lambda z)=\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x}(u-\lambda z)\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\varphi(u)+\lambda^{2}\|\partial_{x}z\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}-2\lambda\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Therefore it suffices to show that

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}0\,.

(14)

Recalling that
u=u{\uparrow}+u{\downarrow}
with
u{\uparrow},u{\downarrow}\in H^{1}(I)=W^{1,2}(I)
by

Proposition

5.28
, we have

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

with the second term in the RHS satisfying

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\left.(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})z\right|_{0}^{1}-\int_{I}(\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})z\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-\|\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\leq 0\,.

By
Proposition

5.28
,
\partial_{x}u{\uparrow}=\partial_{x}^{+}u
and
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=\partial_{x}^{-}u
in
L^{2}(I)
. Since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
in

L^{2}(I)
, we get

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}(\partial_{x}^{+}u)(\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

We claim that the quantity above is zero. Indeed, fixing any representative of
\partial_{x}u\in L^{2}(I)
, let
S\vcentcolon=\{x\in I:\partial_{x}u>0\}
. First, we have
(\partial_{x}^{+}u)(\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}^{-}u)=\partial_{x}^{+}u=0
on
I\setminus S
. Second, we have

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=\partial_{x}^{-}u=0
on
S
, so applying
Fact

5.29

twice (recall that
u{\downarrow}\in H^{3}(I)
) gives that
\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
a. e. in
S
, thus establishing the claim. Thus
(
14
) indeed holds, which concludes the proof in Case 1.

Case 2.

Suppose
u\in W^{1,1}(I)\setminus H^{1}(I)
. We will derive a
contradiction, showing that this case cannot happen. Note that, since
u-\lambda z\in H^{1}(I)

by assumption, we conclude that
z\in W^{1,1}(I)\setminus H^{1}(I)
, and in particular
u,z

are AC with
\partial_{x}u,\partial_{x}z\in L^{1}(I)
. As in the previous case, we have

\displaystyle\varphi(u-\lambda z)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x}(u-\lambda z)\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(15)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x}u-\lambda\partial_{x}z\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(16)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\left((\partial_{x}u)^{2}-2\lambda(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)+\lambda^{2}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

(17)

We claim that the function
(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
. First, by

Proposition

5.28
we have
u{\uparrow}\in W^{1,1}(I)
(while
u{\downarrow}\in H^{1}(I)

since
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
), as well as
\partial_{x}u{\uparrow}=\partial_{x}^{+}u
and
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=\partial_{x}^{-}u
a. e. in
I
. Hence

\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)+(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{I}\Big[\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert+\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

We claim that
(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z),(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
. First, we again have that
(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)=(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})=0
a. e. as in the previous case, where
in particular we are allowed to apply
Fact

5.29
twice to
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
because
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\in W^{2,1}(I)
by virtue of the fact that
\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=z\in W^{1,1}(I)
in the current case. Hence
(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
. Second, note that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
is AC and hence
bounded, while
\partial_{x}z\in L^{1}(I)
since
z
is AC. Therefore

\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\|\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}\|\partial_{x}z\|_{L^{1}(I)}<+\infty\,,

and hence
(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
. Hence
\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert+\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\in L^{1}(I)
and

\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\uparrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{I}\left\lvert(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})(\partial_{x}z)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x<+\infty\,,

so
(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
as claimed. Now, since
\varphi(u-\lambda z)<+\infty
by assumption, (
17
) shows that
(\partial_{x}u)^{2}-2\lambda(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)+\lambda^{2}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}\in L^{1}(I)
, while we have just
established that
2\lambda(\partial_{x}u)(\partial_{x}z)\in L^{1}(I)
. We conclude that

(\partial_{x}u)^{2}+\lambda^{2}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}\in L^{1}(I)
, i. e.

\int_{I}\left((\partial_{x}u)^{2}+\lambda^{2}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x<+\infty\,.

On the other hand, the fact that
u,z\in W^{1,1}(I)\setminus H^{1}(I)
implies that

\int_{I}(\partial_{x}u)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=+\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{I}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=+\infty\,,

which is the desired contradiction. This concludes the proof in Case 2.

Case 3.

Suppose
u
is continuous but not AC (and hence the same is true of

u{\uparrow}
). Then by the definition of absolute continuity, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such
that, for all
\delta>0
, there exists a set of pairwise disjoint intervals
\left((a_{i},b_{i})\right)_{i\in[k]}
in
I
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(b_{i}-a_{i})<\delta
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert>\varepsilon
.

We claim that, moreover, the sequences
\left((a_{i},b_{i})\right)_{i\in[k]}
above can always
be taken to satisfy
u(a_{i})<u(b_{i})
for every
i\in[k]
. Indeed, let
\varepsilon>0
be as in
the paragraph above, let
\delta>0
, and let
\left((a_{i},b_{i})\right)_{i\in[k]}
be the
corresponding sequence. Let
S\vcentcolon=\{i\in[k]:u(a_{i})\geq u(b_{i})\}
. Then for each
i\in S
, we have

\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert=u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})=\underbrace{\Big[u{\downarrow}(a_{i})-u{\downarrow}(b_{i})\Big]}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{\Big[u{\uparrow}(a_{i})-u{\uparrow}(b_{i})\Big]}_{\leq 0}\leq\left\lvert u{\downarrow}(a_{i})-u{\downarrow}(b_{i})\right\rvert\,.

Now, since
u{\downarrow}
is AC, let
\delta>0
be small enough so that

\sum_{i\in S}\left\lvert u{\downarrow}(a_{i})-u{\downarrow}(b_{i})\right\rvert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,.

It follows that

\varepsilon<\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i\in S}\left\lvert u{\downarrow}(a_{i})-u{\downarrow}(b_{i})\right\rvert+\sum_{i\in[k]\setminus S}\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\sum_{i\in[k]\setminus S}\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert\,,

and hence

\sum_{i\in[k]\setminus S}\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,,

and of course
u(a_{i})<u(b_{i})
for each
i\in[k]\setminus S
and
\sum_{i\in[k]\setminus S}(b_{i}-a_{i})<\delta
. This establishes the claim.

Let
v\vcentcolon=u-\lambda z
for convenience, and fix any sequence
\left((a_{i},b_{i})\right)_{i\in[k]}
of pairwise disjoint intervals satisfying
u(a_{i})<u(b_{i})
for each
i\in[k]
. Using
Lemma

5.35
, we map each interval
(a_{i},b_{i})
into an interval

(a^{\prime}_{i},b^{\prime}_{i})\subseteq(a_{i},b_{i})
such that

\left\lvert v(a^{\prime}_{i})-v(b^{\prime}_{i})\right\rvert\geq\frac{\left\lvert u(a_{i})-u(b_{i})\right\rvert}{2}\,.

This implies that
v
is not AC and hence
\varphi(v)=+\infty
, thus concluding the proof in
this case.

Case 4.

Suppose
u
is not continuous. By
Lemma

5.36
,
u-\lambda z
is not continuous, which implies that
u-\lambda z\not\in H^{1}(I)
and
\varphi(u-\lambda z)=+\infty
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
, suppose
u
is continuous, and let
z\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
.
Let
\lambda>0
and let
v\vcentcolon=u-\lambda z
. Let
0<a<b<1
and suppose
u(a)<u(b)
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
with
a\leq a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}\leq b
such that

v(b^{\prime})-v(a^{\prime})\geq\left\lvert u(a)-u(b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon\,.

Proof.

Recall that, by
Proposition

5.18
,
u,z
form a weak solution to
the static Neumann problem, and by
Lemma

5.21
we have
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
with
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
and
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}

in
L^{2}(I)
. In particular,
u{\downarrow}
is continuously differentiable by

Fact

5.30
.

We first observe that it cannot be the case that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<0
for all
x\in(a,b)
, since otherwise
Lemma

5.25
would imply that
u{\uparrow}
is
constant in
(a,b)
, which by continuity would imply that
u{\uparrow}(a)=u{\uparrow}(b)
and hence

u(a)\geq u(b)
, a contradiction.

We first construct
a^{\prime}\in(a,b)
. In particular, we wish
a^{\prime}
to satisfy

u(a^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}u(a)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad z(a^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\geq}}0\,.

(18)

Let
x^{*}\in[a,b)
be given by

x^{*}\vcentcolon=\inf\{x\in(a,b):\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)=0\}\,,

which is well-defined by the observation above and the fact that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\leq 0
in
all of
I
(since
u{\downarrow}
is nonincreasing and continuously differentiable). By the
continuity of
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
, we have that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x^{*})=0
. Since

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)\leq 0
, it must be the case that either
a=x^{*}
or

\mathcal{L}\left\{x\in(a,x^{*}):\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\geq 0\right\}>0\,.

(19)

We consider each case separately. First, suppose
a=x^{*}
. We claim that for all
\delta>0
,
there exists
x\in(a,a+\delta)
such that
z(x)\geq 0
. Suppose for a contradiction that this
is not the case, and fix
\delta>0
such that for all
x\in(a,a+\delta)
,
z(x)<0
. Then
since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
almost everywhere, we conclude that
\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}<0
a. e. in
(a,a+\delta)
. Hence
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<0
for all

x\in(a,a+\delta)
, contradicting the assumption that
a=x^{*}
given the definition of

x^{*}
. Thus the claim holds. Now, using the continuity of
u
, choose
\delta>0
small enough
and choose
a^{\prime}\in(a,a+\delta)
so that
z(a^{\prime})\geq 0
and moreover
u(a^{\prime})\leq u(a)+\varepsilon/2
. This choice of
a^{\prime}
satisfies (
18
).

Second, suppose
a<x^{*}
and (
19
) holds. Since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
almost everywhere, choose
a^{\prime}\in(a,x^{*})
such that
z(a^{\prime})\geq 0
, which
is possible by (
19
). Now, since
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}<0
for all
x\in(a,a^{\prime})\subset(a,x^{*})
by the choice of
x^{*}
,
Lemma

5.25
implies
that
u{\uparrow}
is constant in
(a,a^{\prime})
. By the continuity of
u{\uparrow}
, we conclude that

u{\uparrow}(a)=u{\uparrow}(a^{\prime})
and hence, since
u{\downarrow}
is nonincreasing, we have

u(a^{\prime})=u{\uparrow}(a^{\prime})+u{\downarrow}(a^{\prime})\leq u{\uparrow}(a)+u{\downarrow}(a)=u(a)\,,

and again (
18
) is satisfied. This concludes the choice of
a^{\prime}
.

Now, we may assume without loss of generality that
\varepsilon<\left\lvert u(a)-u(b)\right\rvert/2
. Therefore
our choice of
a^{\prime}
yields an interval
(a^{\prime},b)
which, using (
18
) and
recalling that
u(a)<u(b)
, satisfies
u(a^{\prime})<u(b)
. Therefore, repeating a symmetric version
of the argument above yields a choice of
b^{\prime}\in(a^{\prime},b)
satisfying

u(b^{\prime})\geq u(b)-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad z(b^{\prime})\leq 0\,.

(20)

Combining (
18
) and (
20
), we conclude that

v(b^{\prime})-v(a^{\prime})=\Big[u(b^{\prime})-u(a^{\prime})\Big]-\lambda\underbrace{\Big[z(b^{\prime})-z(a^{\prime})\Big]}_{\leq 0}\geq u(b)-u(a)-\varepsilon=\left\lvert u(a)-u(b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon\,.\qed

Lemma 5.36
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
, suppose
u
is not continuous, and let
z\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Let
\lambda>0
. Then
u-\lambda z
is not continuous.

Proof.

Recall that, by
Proposition

5.18
,
u,z
form a weak solution to
the static Neumann problem, and by
Lemma

5.21
we have
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
with
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
and
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}

in
L^{2}(I)
. In particular,
u{\downarrow}
is continuously differentiable by

Fact

5.30
.

Since
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
is continuous while
u
is not continuous, we conclude that

u{\uparrow}
is not continuous and, since it is monotone, it contains only jump discontinuities.
Let
x_{0}\in I
be a point of jump discontinuity of
u{\uparrow}
, i. e. a point such that
L_{-}<L_{+}

where

L_{-}\vcentcolon=\lim_{x\to x_{0}^{-}}u{\uparrow}(x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad L_{+}\vcentcolon=\lim_{x\to x_{0}^{+}}u{\uparrow}(x)\,.

We first claim that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x_{0})=0
. Indeed, it is clear that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\leq 0
since
u{\downarrow}
is nonincreasing and continuously differentiable. If we had

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x_{0})<0
, then by continuity
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
would be strictly
negative in a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
, in which case
Lemma

5.25
would
imply that
u{\uparrow}
is constant in a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
, contradicting the fact that
L_{-}<L_{+}
. Hence the claim holds.

We now claim that, for all
\delta>0
, there exists
x\in(x_{0},x_{0}+\delta)
such that
z(x)\leq 0
. Suppose for a contradiction that there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
x\in(x_{0},x_{0}+\delta)
,
z(x)>0
. Then, since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
a. e. and

\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x_{0})=0
, we conclude that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}>0
in
(x_{0},x_{0}+\delta)
, contradicting the fact that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\leq 0
. Hence the claim holds.
By the same reasoning, we conclude that for all
\delta>0
there exists
x\in(x_{0}-\delta,x_{0})
such that
z(x)\geq 0
.

Thus, for all
\delta>0
there exist points
x_{-}\in(x_{0}-\delta,x_{0}),x_{+}\in(x_{0},x_{0}+\delta)
such that
z(x_{+})-z(x_{-})\leq 0
and hence

\displaystyle(u-\lambda z)(x_{+})-(u-\lambda z)(x_{-})

\displaystyle=\Big[u{\uparrow}(x_{+})-u{\uparrow}(x_{-})\Big]+\Big[u{\downarrow}(x_{+})-u{\downarrow}(x_{-})\Big]-\lambda\Big[z(x_{+})-z(x_{-})\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\Big[u{\uparrow}(x_{+})-u{\uparrow}(x_{-})\Big]+\Big[u{\downarrow}(x_{+})-u{\downarrow}(x_{-})\Big]\,.

Since
u{\uparrow}\to L_{+}
as
x\downarrow x_{0}
and
u{\uparrow}\to L_{-}
as
x\uparrow x_{0}
and

u{\uparrow}
is nondecreasing, we have
u{\uparrow}(x_{+})-u{\uparrow}(x_{-})\geq L_{+}-L_{-}
. By the
continuity of
u{\downarrow}
, we can let
\delta>0
be small enough so that
u{\downarrow}(x_{+})-u{\downarrow}(x_{-})\geq-\frac{L_{+}-L_{-}}{2}
. We conclude that, for all sufficiently small
\delta>0
, there exist points
x_{-}\in(x_{0}-\delta,x_{0}),x_{+}\in(x_{0},x_{0}+\delta)
such that

(u-\lambda z)(x_{+})-(u-\lambda z)(x_{-})\geq\frac{L_{+}-L_{-}}{2}\,,

and thus
u-\lambda z
is not continuous.
∎

Lemma 5.37

(Specialization of
[
Bre73
, Theorem 4.4]
)
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and let
\tau:H\to[0,+\infty]
be a convex, proper, and lower
semicontinuous functional such that
\tau(\mathrm{Proj}_{\overline{D(A)}}x)\leq\tau(x)
for
all
x\in H
. Let
A:H\to 2^{H}
be a maximal monotone operator and let
S_{t}
be the semigroup
generated by
-A
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\tau((I+\lambda A)^{-1}x)\leq\tau(x)
for all
x\in H
and
\lambda>0
; and

2.

\tau(S_{t}x)\leq\tau(x)
for all
x\in\overline{D(A)}
and
t\geq 0
.

In the statement above,
(I+\lambda A)^{-1}:H\to D(A)
is the
resolvent
of
A
(also
denoted by
J_{\lambda}
, see e. g.
[
Eva10
, p. 563]
).

Proposition 5.38

(
\varphi
-monotonicity of solutions)
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
be the solution to the gradient
flow problem with initial data
u_{0}
. Then for all
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}<+\infty
, we have

\varphi(\bm{u}(t_{1}))\geq\varphi(\bm{u}(t_{2}))
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Lemma

5.37
. Indeed, letting
H\vcentcolon=L^{2}(I)
and
A\vcentcolon=\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}
(which is maximal monotone as remarked earlier), we first
observe that indeed
\varphi(\mathrm{Proj}_{\overline{D(A)}}f)\leq\varphi(f)
for all
f\in L^{2}(I)
, since by
Observation

5.32
we have
\mathrm{Proj}_{\overline{D(A)}}f=f
.
Thus it suffices to show that for all
f\in L^{2}(I)
and all
\lambda>0
,

\varphi((I+\lambda A)^{-1}f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}\varphi(f)\,.

But this is equivalent to showing that, for all
u\in D(A)
,
-z\in A(u)
and
\lambda>0
,

\varphi(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}\varphi(u-\lambda z)\,,

which is precisely
Lemma

5.34
.
∎

Corollary 5.39

(Preservation of
H^{1}
regularity)
.

Suppose
u_{0}\in H^{1}(I)
, and let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
be the solution to the
gradient flow problem with initial data
u_{0}
. Then
\bm{u}(t)\in H^{1}(I)
for all
t>0
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

5.38
and the definition of

\varphi
.
∎

5.6
Preservation of Lipschitz regularity

It will also be useful to control the Lipschitz regularity of solutions. At a high level, we follow
a similar strategy as in
Section

5.5
. Recall that
W^{1,\infty}(I)
is
equivalent to the space of Lipschitz real-valued functions on
I
, up to identification of almost
everywhere equal functions. Define
\psi:L^{2}(I)\to[0,+\infty]
by

\psi(u)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\|\partial_{x}u\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}&\text{if }u\in W^{1,\infty}(I)\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Fact 5.40
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
and let
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Then
\psi(u)\leq M
if and only if
u=f

a. e. for some
M
-Lipschitz function
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, in this case
f
is the
(unique) continuous representative of
u
and
\psi(u)
is the Lipschitz constant of
f
.

Claim 5.41
.

The functional
\psi
is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof.

Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicontinuity. Since

L^{2}(I)
is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity. Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
L^{2}(I)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
. We need to show that

\psi(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\leq}}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\psi(u_{n})\,.

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subsequence

(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\psi(u_{n_{k}})=M<+\infty
. By
extracting a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
\psi(u_{n_{k}})<+\infty
, and thus

u_{n_{k}}\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
, for every
k
. For simplicity and using
Fact

5.40
,
fix for each
n_{k}
the continuous representative of
u_{n_{k}}
, which we denote by the same name.
Then each
u_{n_{k}}
is
M_{k}
-Lipschitz with
M_{k}\to M
.

Since
u_{n_{k}}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
, it is standard that we may extract a subsequence that
converges to
u
pointwise almost everywhere. Denote this further subsequence again by

(u_{n_{k}})_{k}
, and let
N\subset I
be a measure zero set such that
u_{n_{k}}\to u
pointwise
in
I\setminus N
. Then for each
x\neq y
in
I\setminus N
, we have

\left\lvert u(x)-u(y)\right\rvert=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\lvert u_{n_{k}}(x)-u_{n_{k}}(y)\right\rvert\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}M_{k}|x-y|=M|x-y|\,.

By
Lemma

A.3
,
u
is a. e. equal to an
M
-Lipschitz function,
so
\psi(u)\leq M
by
Fact

5.40
, as needed.
∎

Definition 5.42
.

For any
w:I\to\mathbb{R}
and distinct
x,y\in I
, let

\slope_{w}(x,y)\vcentcolon=\frac{w(y)-w(x)}{y-x}\,.

Lemma 5.43
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
and let
z\in-\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Then for all
\lambda>0
,

\psi(u-\lambda z)\geq\psi(u)
.

Proof.

We follow the proof outline from
Lemma

5.34
, but some of the technical
details are different. As in that proof, we have
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
with
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
on
\{0,1\}
and
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
in
L^{2}(I)
.

We may assume that
u-\lambda z\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
, since otherwise
\psi(u-\lambda z)=+\infty
and there is nothing to prove. In particular, this establishes that
u-\lambda z
is
continuous.

When
u
is not continuous,
Lemma

5.36
yields that
u-\lambda z
is not
continuous, a contradiction. Therefore we may assume that
u
is continuous. Since both
u
and

u-\lambda z
are continuous, we conclude that
z
is also continuous. Let
v\vcentcolon=u-\lambda z
. By
Fact

5.40
, it suffices to show that for every
(a,b)\subset I
and
all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\subset I
such that

\left\lvert\slope_{v}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right\rvert\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{\geq}}\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon\,.

(21)

If
u(a)<u(b)
, then the interval
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\subseteq(a,b)
given by

Lemma

5.35
with parameter
\varepsilon(b-a)
satisfies (
21
);
and if
u(a)=u(b)
, then any interval will do. Therefore suppose
u(a)>u(b)
, so in
particular
\slope_{u}(a,b)<0
.

Note that a sufficient condition for (
21
) is that
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon
with
u(a^{\prime})>u(b^{\prime})
,
z(a^{\prime})\leq 0
and
z(b^{\prime})\geq 0
,
since in that case,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\slope_{v}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lvert(u-\lambda z)(b^{\prime})-(u-\lambda z)(a^{\prime})\right\rvert}{b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}}=\Big|\underbrace{u(b^{\prime})-u(a^{\prime})}_{<0}-\lambda\underbrace{(z(b^{\prime})-z(a^{\prime}))}_{\geq 0}\Big|\left(\frac{1}{b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left\lvert u(b^{\prime})-u(a^{\prime})\right\rvert}{b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}}=\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon\,,

which is (
21
). We now find
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
satisfying the aforementioned
conditions.

For each point
x\in I
, say
x
is
left-favourable
if every neighbourhood
(x-\delta,x+\delta)
contains a point
x^{\prime}
such that
z(x^{\prime})\leq 0
. Similarly, say
x
is

right-favourable
if every neighbourhood
(x-\delta,x+\delta)
contains a point
x^{\prime}
such
that
z(x^{\prime})\geq 0
.

We claim that there exist points
a^{*}<b^{*}
in
I
such that
a^{*}
is left-favourable,
b^{*}
is
right-favourable, and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b^{*})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
with
u(a^{*})>u(b^{*})
. Let us first show that this claim yields the desired points
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, and then
proceed to prove the claim. Suppose we have
a^{*}
and
b^{*}
as claimed. Then by the continuity
of
u
, we may fix sufficiently small
\delta>0
and find points
a^{\prime}\in(a^{*}-\delta,a^{*}+\delta)
and
b^{\prime}\in(b^{*}-\delta,b^{*}+\delta)
such that
a^{\prime}<b^{\prime}
,
z(a^{\prime})\leq 0
,
z(b^{\prime})\geq 0
,
u(a^{\prime})>u(b^{\prime})
, and, for
\alpha\vcentcolon=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\frac{b^{*}-a^{*}}{u(a^{*})-u(b^{*})}
and
\beta\vcentcolon=\frac{\varepsilon(b^{*}-a^{*})}{2}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{u(a^{\prime})-u(b^{\prime})}{b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}}\geq\frac{u(a^{*})-u(b^{*})-\beta}{(b^{*}-a^{*})(1+\alpha)}\geq\frac{u(a^{*})-u(b^{*})}{b^{*}-a^{*}}(1-\alpha)-\frac{\varepsilon/2}{1+\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{u(a^{*})-u(b^{*})}{b^{*}-a^{*}}-\varepsilon=\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b^{*})\right\rvert-\varepsilon\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert-\varepsilon\,,

as desired, where we used the inequality
\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\geq 1-\alpha
. We now establish
the existence of
a^{*}
and
b^{*}
.

We first find
a^{*}
. If
a
is left-favourable, then choose
a^{*}=a
; note that, of course, we
have
u(a^{*})>u(b)
and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
. Otherwise,
a
is
not left-favourable, which gives some
\delta>0
such that
z(x)>0
for every
x\in(a-\delta,a+\delta)
, and hence
\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}>0
a. e. in
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
. We now consider two cases. Recall that
\slope_{u}(a,b)<0
.

First, suppose
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)\geq\slope_{u}(a,b)
. Note that it cannot be the case
that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)>\slope_{u}(a,b)
for all
x\in(a,b)
, since otherwise we would
have

u(b)=u{\uparrow}(b)+u{\downarrow}(b)\geq u{\uparrow}(a)+u{\downarrow}(a)+\int_{(a,b)}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x>u(a)+(b-a)\slope_{u}(a,b)=u(b)\,,

a contradiction. Therefore we may let

a^{*}\vcentcolon=\inf\left\{x\in(a,b):\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\leq\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\}\,.

By the continuity of
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
(recall that
u{\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
is continuously
differentiable), we conclude that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a^{*})=\slope_{u}(a,b)
. We also observe
that we must have
a^{*}>a
since, as noted above, we have
\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)>0
a. e. in some neighbourhood
(a-\delta,a+\delta)
, which implies that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)>\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)\geq\slope_{u}(a,b)
for all
x\in(a,a+\delta)
. We
claim that
a^{*}
is left-favourable. Indeed, otherwise there would be some
\delta>0
such
that
z(x)>0
for all
x\in(a^{*}-\delta,a^{*}+\delta)
, and since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
a. e. we would conclude that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a^{*})=\slope_{u}(a,b)
for
x\in(a^{*}-\delta,a^{*})
, contradicting the choice of
a^{*}
. Hence we have
found a left-favourable
a^{*}
in this case; we claim that
a^{*}
also satisfies
u(a^{*})>u(b)

and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
. The first inequality holds since

\displaystyle u(a^{*})-u(b)

\displaystyle=u{\downarrow}(a^{*})+u{\uparrow}(a^{*})-\big[u(a)+(b-a)\slope_{u}(a,b)\big]

\displaystyle\geq u{\downarrow}(a)+\int_{(a,a^{*})}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+u{\uparrow}(a)-u{\uparrow}(a)-u{\downarrow}(a)-(b-a)\slope_{u}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\int_{(a,a^{*})}\left(\underbrace{\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)}_{>\slope_{u}(a,b)}-\slope_{u}(a,b)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-(b-a^{*})\underbrace{\slope_{u}(a,b)}_{<0}

\displaystyle>0\,,

and the second inequality holds since

\displaystyle(b-a^{*})(b-a)\Big(\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert-\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=(b-a^{*})(b-a)\left(\frac{u(a^{*})-u(b)}{b-a^{*}}-\frac{u(a)-u(b)}{b-a}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(u(a^{*})-u(b)\Big)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Bigg(u{\uparrow}(a^{*})+u{\downarrow}(a)+\int_{(a,a^{*})}\underbrace{\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)}_{>\slope_{u}(a,b)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-u(b)\Bigg)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad>\Big(u{\uparrow}(a)+u{\downarrow}(a)+(a^{*}-a)\slope_{u}(a,b)-u(b)\Big)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(u(a)+(a^{*}-a)\frac{u(b)-u(a)}{b-a}-u(b)\Big)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)\Big((b-a)-(b-a^{*})-(a^{*}-a)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=0\,.

Therefore in the first case we have found a left-favourable
a^{*}\in(a,b)
such that
u(a^{*})>u(b)
and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
.

Second, suppose
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a)<\slope_{u}(a,b)
. We proceed similarly, but with
points to the left of
a
instead. Namely, it cannot be the case that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<\slope_{u}(a,b)
for all
x\in(0,a)
, since by continuity this would imply that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(0)\leq\slope_{u}(a,b)<0
, a contradiction. Hence we may define

a^{*}\vcentcolon=\sup\left\{x\in(0,a):\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)\geq\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\}\,.

By continuity, we conclude that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a^{*})=\slope_{u}(a,b)
, which also implies
that
a^{*}<a
. We claim that
a^{*}
is left-favourable. Indeed, if it was not, then for some

\delta>0
we would have
z(x)>0
for all
x\in(a^{*}-\delta,a^{*}+\delta)
, and since
z=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
a. e. we would conclude that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)>\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(a^{*})=\slope_{u}(a,b)
for all
x\in(a^{*},a^{*}+\delta)
, contradicting the
choice of
a^{*}
. We now claim that
a^{*}
also satisfies
u(a^{*})>u(b)
and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
.

To prove the first inequality, we first observe that
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)<\slope_{u}(a,b)<0
for all
x\in(a^{*},a)
, which by
Lemma

5.25
implies that
u{\uparrow}

is constant in
(a^{*},a)
. Since
u{\uparrow}
is continuous (because
u
and
u{\downarrow}
are), we
conclude that
u{\uparrow}(a^{*})=u{\uparrow}(a)
. Therefore we have

u(a^{*})-u(a)=u{\downarrow}(a^{*})-u{\downarrow}(a)\geq 0

since
u{\downarrow}
is nonincreasing, and hence
u(a^{*})\geq u(a)>u(b)
. As for the second
inequality, we have

\displaystyle(b-a^{*})(b-a)\Big(\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert-\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=(b-a^{*})(b-a)\left(\frac{u(a^{*})-u(b)}{b-a^{*}}-\frac{u(a)-u(b)}{b-a}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(u(a^{*})-u(b)\Big)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})
