\displaystyle\qquad=\Bigg(u{\uparrow}(a)+u{\downarrow}(a)-\int_{(a^{*},a)}\underbrace{\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}(x)}_{<\slope_{u}(a,b)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-u(b)\Bigg)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad>\Bigg(u(a)-(a-a^{*})\frac{u(b)-u(a)}{b-a}-u(b)\Bigg)(b-a)-\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)(b-a^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(u(a)-u(b)\Big)\Big((b-a)-(b-a^{*})+(a-a^{*})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=0\,.

Therefore in any case we have found a left-favourable
a^{*}\in(0,b)
such that
u(a^{*})>u(b)

and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a,b)\right\rvert
.

Repeating an analogous argument for the right endpoint of the interval
(a^{*},b)
, we find a
right-favourable
b^{*}\in(a^{*},1)
such that
u(a^{*})>u(b^{*})
and
\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b^{*})\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert\slope_{u}(a^{*},b)\right\rvert
, which concludes the proof as explained above.
∎

Proposition 5.44

(
\psi
-monotonicity of solutions)
.

Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
be the solution to the gradient
flow problem with initial data
u_{0}
. Then for all
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}<+\infty
, we have

\psi(\bm{u}(t_{1}))\geq\psi(\bm{u}(t_{2}))
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Proposition

5.38
, this follows from

Lemmas

5.43
and
5.37
.
∎

Corollary 5.45

(Preservation of Lipschitz regularity)
.

Suppose
u_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
, and let
\bm{u}\in C([0,+\infty);L^{2}(I))
be the solution
to the gradient flow problem with initial data
u_{0}
. Then
\bm{u}(t)\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
for
all
t>0
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

5.44
and the definition of
\psi
.
∎

5.7
Nonexpansiveness and order preservation

Our goal in this section is to establish that the semigroup
P_{t}
is nonexpansive and order
preserving. These properties will help us show that applying
P_{t}
to line restrictions in the
multidimensional setting behaves as expected by “making progress” toward monotonicity with each
application.

In the context of PDEs, one desirable way to show that a property is preserved through time is to
differentiate in time, and then pass the derivative inside the integral to exploit the definition of
the PDE. However, we need to establish some technical results before we can justify such
calculations.

We start with the following lemma, slightly adapted from
[
CT80
]
, which reveals a close
connection between order preservation and nonexpansiveness (here, in the supremum norm) of
operators. Hence our strategy will be to establish order preservation, and conclude
nonexpansiveness.

Lemma 5.46
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
be a finite measure space, and let
1\leq p\leq+\infty
. Let

L^{\infty}(\Omega)\subset C\subset L^{p}(\Omega)
have the property that for all
f\in C
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
f+r\in C
. Let
T:C\to L^{p}(\Omega)
be continuous as a map from
L^{p}(\Omega)

to
L^{p}(\Omega)
(the continuity requirement may be dropped if
p=\infty
) satisfying

T(f+r)=T(f)+r\text{ a.\kern 1.02228pte.\ }

(22)

for every
f\in C
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

For all
f,g\in C
, if
f\leq g
a. e. then
Tf\leq Tg
a. e. .

(b)

For all
f,g\in C
,
(Tf-Tg)^{+}\leq\esssup(f-g)^{+}
a. e. .

(c)

For all
f,g\in C
,
\left\lvert Tf-Tg\right\rvert\leq\esssup\left\lvert f-g\right\rvert
a. e. .

Proof.

We show
(a)

\implies

(b)

\implies

(c)

\implies

(a)
. First, assume
(a)
holds, and let
f,g\in C
. Suppose
r\vcentcolon=\esssup(f-g)^{+}<+\infty
, since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Note that
g+r\geq f

and
g+r\geq g
both a. e. . Thus by
(a)
and (
22
),
we have

Tg+(Tf-Tg)^{+}=Tf\lor Tg\leq T(g+r)=Tg+r\text{ a.\kern 1.00006pte.\ }\,,

so
(Tf-Tg)^{+}\leq r
a. e. as needed. Next, suppose
(b)
holds, and let
f,g\in C
. Let
r_{1}\vcentcolon=\esssup(f-g)^{+}
and
r_{2}\vcentcolon=\esssup(g-f)^{+}
; note that
\esssup\left\lvert f-g\right\rvert=\max\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
, so we may assume that
r_{1},r_{2}<+\infty
, since otherwise
there is nothing to show. Then indeed, using
(b)
,

\left\lvert Tf-Tg\right\rvert=\max\left\{(Tf-Tg)^{+},(Tg-Tf)^{+}\right\}\leq\max\{r_{1},r_{2}\}=\esssup\left\lvert f-g\right\rvert\text{ a.\kern 1.00006pte.\ ,}

as needed. Finally, suppose
(c)
holds, and let
f,g\in C
satisfy
f\leq g

a. e. . First, suppose
f,g\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. Let
r\vcentcolon=\esssup(g-f)
, so that
0\leq r<+\infty
by assumption. Then, by
(c)
and (
22
),

\displaystyle\esssup\{Tf-Tg+r\}

\displaystyle=\esssup\{T(f+r)-Tg\}\leq\esssup\left\lvert T(f+r)-Tg\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\esssup\left\lvert f-g+r\right\rvert\leq r\,,

so
Tf\leq Tg
a. e. as needed.

Now, for general
f,g\in C
with
f\leq g
a. e. , we may approximate them by sequences

(f_{n})_{n},(g_{n})_{n}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)\subset C
such that
f_{n}\to f
and
g_{n}\to g
in

L^{p}(\Omega)
and moreover
f_{n}\leq g_{n}
a. e. for each
n
; indeed, letting
f_{n}\vcentcolon=\max\{-n,\min\{n,f\}\}
and likewise for
g_{n}
, it is immediate that
f_{n},g_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
f_{n}\leq g_{n}
a. e. , and the convergences
f_{n}\to f
and
g_{n}\to g
in

L^{p}(\Omega)
follow from the dominated convergence theorem: since
\left\lvert f-f_{n}\right\rvert^{p}\to 0

pointwise and
\left\lvert f-f_{n}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert f\right\rvert
pointwise for each
n
, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\left\lvert f_{n}-f\right\rvert^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=0
.

The previous case yields
Tf_{n}\leq Tg_{n}
a. e. for each
n
, and we claim that the continuity
of
T
implies that
Tf\leq Tg
a. e. as well. Indeed, we have
Tf_{n}\to Tf
an
Tg_{n}\to Tg

in
L^{p}(\Omega)
and thus

\displaystyle\left\|(Tf-Tg)^{+}-(Tf_{n}-Tg_{n})^{+}\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|(Tf-Tg)-(Tf_{n}-Tg_{n})\right\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Tf-Tf_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|Tg-Tg_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\to 0\,,

which means that
(Tf_{n}-Tg_{n})^{+}\to(Tf-Tg)^{+}
in
L^{p}(\Omega)
. But since
(Tf_{n}-Tg_{n})^{+}=0
a. e. and hence in
L^{p}(\Omega)
for each
n
, we conclude that
(Tf-Tg)^{+}=0
a. e. ,
i. e.
Tf\leq Tg
a. e. as needed.
∎

The following lemma is a specific formulation of the well-known Leibniz rule for differentiating
under the integral sign. It is a specialization of the version stated in
[
Che
]
, and can be
proved by a standard argument using the Fubini-Tonelli theorem.

Lemma 5.47

(Differentiating under the integral sign)
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
be a measure space and let
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
. Let
f:[a,b]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a jointly measurable function satisfying the following:

1.

f(t,\cdot)\in L^{1}(\Omega)
for a. e.
t\in(a,b)
.

2.

f(\cdot,x)
is AC for a. e.
x\in\Omega
. (Its a. e. defined partial derivative
is denoted by
\partial_{t}f
as usual.)

3.

It holds that

\int_{(a,b)}\int_{\Omega}\left\lvert\partial_{t}f(t,x)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t<+\infty\,.

Then the function
t\mapsto\int_{\Omega}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
is AC and

\partial_{t}\int_{\Omega}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\qquad\text{for a.\kern 1.02228pte.\ $t\in(a,b)$.}

The following fact is a standard consequence of the definition of absolute continuity:

Fact 5.48
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval. If
f,g:J\to\mathbb{R}
are AC, then
f\lor g
is AC.

Fact 5.49

(See e. g.
[
EG15
, Theorem 4.4]
)
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval. Let
1\leq p<\infty
and let
f\in W^{1,p}(J)
.
Then
f^{+}\in W^{1,p}(J)
and
\partial_{x}(f^{+})=\chi_{\{f>0\}}\partial_{x}f
a. e. in
J
.

We also use the following standard formulation of a chain rule for Sobolev functions. This version
follows e. g. from
[
EG15
, Theorem 4.4]
, which is stated for globally Lipschitz functions
F
, by
using the fact that the image of
f
on
J
is bounded, so that the local Lipschitz condition
suffices (e. g. extend
F
linearly outside the image of
f
to obtain a
C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, globally
Lipschitz function
\tilde{F}
).

Fact 5.50
.

Let
J\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval. Let
1\leq p<\infty
, let
f\in W^{1,p}(J)
, and
let
F\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
be locally Lipschitz. Then
F\circ f\in W^{1,p}(J)
and
\partial_{x}(F\circ f)(x)=F^{\prime}(f(x))\partial_{x}f(x)
for a. e.
x\in J
.

The following lemma essentially says that, given an element of a Bochner space, we can get a handle
on a concrete jointly measurable function “representing” that element in a precise sense. This
makes concrete the intuitive expectation that a solution
\bm{u}
to our PDEs, which maps each point
in time to an element of
L^{2}(I)
, should also give us a specific value at each point in time and
space “
\bm{u}(t,x)
”.

Lemma 5.51
.

[
DS58
, Theorem 17, p. 198]

Let
(S,\Sigma_{S},\mu)
and
(T,\Sigma_{T},\lambda)
be measure spaces which are either both
finite or both positive and
\sigma
-finite, and let
(R,\Sigma_{R},\rho)
be their product. Let

1\leq p\leq\infty
and let
F
be a
\mu
-integrable function on
S
to
L^{p}(T,\Sigma_{T},\lambda,\mathcal{X})
where
\mathcal{X}
is a real or complex Banach space. Then there is a
\rho
-measurable
function
f
on
R
to
\mathcal{X}
, which is uniquely determined except for a set of
\rho
-measure
zero, such that
f(s,\cdot)=F(s)
for
\mu
-almost all
s
in
S
. Moreover
f(\cdot,t)
is

\mu
-integrable on
S
for
\lambda
-almost all
t
and the integral
\int_{S}f(s,t)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)
, as a function of
t
is equal to the element
\int_{S}F(s)\mu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s)
of
L^{p}(T,\Sigma_{T},\lambda,\mathcal{X})
.

Corollary 5.52
.

Let
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
be a compact interval endowed with the Lebesgue measure, and let
F\in L^{1}(a,b;L^{2}(I))
. Then there exists a jointly measurable function
f^{*}:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

1.

f^{*}(s,\cdot)=F(s)
in
L^{2}(I)
for a. e.
s\in(a,b)
.

2.

f^{*}(\cdot,x)\in L^{1}(a,b)
for all
x\in I
.

3.

For all
s\in[a,b]
, the functions
x\mapsto\int_{(a,s)}f^{*}(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
and

\int_{(a,s)}F(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
are equal in
L^{2}(I)
.

Proof.

Let condition
\tilde{2}
denote condition 2 with “all
x\in I
” replaced by “a. e.
x\in I
”​​. We first apply
Lemma

5.51
with
S=[a,b]
and
T=I
, both endowed with the
Lebesgue measure, to obtain a function
f
satisfying conditions 1 and
\tilde{2}
, as well
condition 3 for
s=b
.

We now show that condition 3 also holds for other values of
s\in[a,b)
using the a. e. uniqueness given by
Lemma

5.51
. For any
s\in[a,b)
, we may apply that lemma with

\tilde{S}=[a,s]
instead to obtain a function
\tilde{f}
satisfying
x\mapsto\int_{(a,s)}{\tilde{f}}(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{(a,s)}F(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
in
L^{2}(I)
. But since both
g=\tilde{f}

and
g=f
satisfy that
g(r,\cdot)=F(r)
in
L^{2}(I)
for a. e.
r\in(a,s)
,

Lemma

5.51
implies that
\tilde{f}=f
except for a subset of
[a,s]\times I
of joint
measure zero. We conclude that, for a. e.
x\in I
, we have
\tilde{f}(\cdot,x)=f(\cdot,x)

a. e. in
(a,s)
and hence
\int_{(a,s)}f(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{(a,s)}{\tilde{f}}(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
. It follows that
x\mapsto\int_{(a,s)}f(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=x\mapsto\int_{(a,s)}{\tilde{f}}(r,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{(a,s)}F(r)
in
L^{2}(I)
, as claimed.

The final step is to construct a jointly measurable function
f^{*}:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying condition 2 rather than just
\tilde{2}
, while preserving conditions 1 and 3. Let
N\subset I
be a measure zero set such that
f(\cdot,x)\in L^{1}(a,b)
for all
x\in I\setminus N
. Define
f^{*}
by

f^{*}(s,x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}f(s,x)&\text{if }x\in I\setminus N\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We note that
f^{*}
is jointly measurable; indeed, this follows from the facts that
f=f^{*}
on

[a,b]\times(I\setminus N)
and that the set
[a,b]\times(I\setminus N)
is jointly
measurable. By construction,
f^{*}
satisfies condition 2, and it is clear that
f^{*}
also
satisfies conditions 1 and 3 since
f
does and
N
is a null set.
∎

Lemma 5.53
.

Let
0\leq a<b<+\infty
, and let
\bm{u}\in C([a,b];L^{2}(I))
be the restriction to domain

[a,b]
of any solution to the gradient flow problem, with
\bm{u^{\prime}}:[a,b]\to L^{2}(I)
its
weak derivative restricted in the same way. Then there exists a jointly measurable function

\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

1.

\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)=\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
in
L^{2}(I)
for a. e.
t\in(a,b)
.

2.

\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(\cdot,x)\in L^{1}(a,b)
for all
x\in I
.

3.

For all
t\in[a,b]
, the functions
x\mapsto\int_{(a,t)}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s
and
\int_{(a,t)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s
are equal in
L^{2}(I)
.

Moreover, fixing any representative of
\bm{u}(a)\in L^{2}(I)
, the function
\bm{\tilde{u}}:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)\vcentcolon=\bm{u}(a)(x)+\int_{(a,t)}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

is jointly measurable and satisfies

4.

For each
t\in[a,b]
,
\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,\cdot)=\bm{u}(t)
in
L^{2}(I)
.

5.

For each
x\in I
,
\bm{\tilde{u}}(\cdot,x)
is absolutely continuous with weak
derivative
\partial_{t}\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)=\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)
.

Proof.

Apply
Corollary

5.52
to
\bm{u^{\prime}}
, which is integrable since it is the
weak derivative of
\bm{u}
, to obtain jointly measurable
\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying properties 1–3. We now verify that
\bm{\tilde{u}}
satisfies properties 4 and
5. Note that these two properties then imply that
\bm{\tilde{u}}
is a Carathéodory function and
hence jointly measurable (see e. g.
[
AB06
, Lemma 4.51]
).

For each
t\in[a,b]
, the definition of
\bm{u}
(in particular its absolute continuity)
implies that

\bm{u}(t)-\bm{u}(a)=\left[\bm{u}(0)+\int_{(0,t)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right]-\left[\bm{u}(0)+\int_{(0,a)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right]=\int_{(a,t)}\bm{u^{\prime}}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

in
L^{2}(I)
. Property 3 implies that, for a. e.
x\in I
,

\bm{u}(t)(x)=\bm{u}(a)(x)+\int_{(a,t)}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)\,,

which is property 4. Finally, property 5 is an immediate consequence of the definition of

\bm{\tilde{u}}
.
∎

We are now prepared to differentiate in time in order to establish that
P_{t}
is order preserving.

Proposition 5.54
.

Let
u_{0},v_{0}\in H^{1}(I)
, and let
\bm{u},\bm{v}
be the solutions to the gradient flow
problem with initial data
u_{0},v_{0}
respectively. Then the function
\Delta:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
given by

\Delta(t)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\left[(\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t))^{+}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

is nonincreasing.

Proof.

Let
0\leq a<b<+\infty
, so that it suffices to show that
\Delta(a)\geq\Delta(b)
. Apply

Lemma

5.53
to
\bm{u}
and
\bm{v}
to obtain functions
\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}
,

\bm{\tilde{u}}
,
\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}
, and
\bm{\tilde{v}}
with the properties stated in that lemma.
Define the function
f:[a,b]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
by

f(t,x)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\left[(\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x))^{+}\right]^{2}\,.

Note that
f
is jointly measurable, since both
\bm{\tilde{u}}
and
\bm{\tilde{v}}
are. We also
obtain that, for each
t\in[a,b]
,

\Delta(t)=\int_{I}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

We now verify that
f
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

5.47
. Let us verify the first
condition. Since
\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,\cdot),\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(I)
, it follows that

f(t,\cdot)\in L^{1}(I)
, as desired.

We now verify the second condition. We already have that, for all
x\in I
,
\bm{\tilde{u}}(\cdot,x)
and
\bm{\tilde{v}}(\cdot,x)
are AC.
Fact

5.49

implies that, for all
x\in I
, the function
t\mapsto(\bm{\tilde{u}}(\cdot,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(\cdot,x))^{+}
is AC. But the function
y\mapsto y^{2}
is locally Lipschitz, so

Fact

5.50
implies that
f(\cdot,x)
is AC for each
x
, so the
second condition is satisfied. Moreover, using

Facts

5.50
and
5.49
, its weak derivative is

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,x)

\displaystyle=\left[(\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x))^{+}\right]\partial_{t}\left[(\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x))^{+}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[(\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x))^{+}\right]\chi_{\{\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)>\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\}}\left[\partial_{t}\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\partial_{t}\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\right]

\displaystyle=\chi_{\{\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)>\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\}}\left[\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\right]\left[\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}(t,x)\right]\,.

We claim that
\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\left\lvert\partial_{t}f(t,x)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t<+\infty
.
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\left\lvert\partial_{t}f(t,x)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\Big|\chi_{\{\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)>\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\}}\left[\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\right]\left[\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}(t,x)\right]\Big|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}(\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x))^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}(\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}(t,x))^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,.

We claim that each of the four terms above is finite. First, using Tonelli’s theorem, the
definition of
\bm{\tilde{u}}
and Jensen’s inequality, and letting
L\vcentcolon=b-a
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\int_{(a,b)}\left(\bm{u}(a)(x)+\int_{(a,t)}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{I}\int_{(a,b)}\bm{u}(a)(x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+2L\int_{I}\int_{(a,b)}\int_{(a,t)}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{u}(a)(x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+2L\int_{(a,b)}\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=2L\|\bm{u}(a)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}+2L^{2}\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(s,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\,,

where in the last line we write
\|\bm{u}(a)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}
, which is finite, since
\bm{u}(a)\in L^{2}(I)
. Hence, since the argument for the
\bm{v}
terms proceeds identically, all we need
to show is that

\int_{(a,b)}\int_{I}\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{<}}+\infty\,.

Since
\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,\cdot)=\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
in
L^{2}(I)
for a. e.
t\in(a,b)
, this is
equivalent to showing that

\int_{(a,b)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{<}}+\infty\,.

(23)

Recall that, by
Definition

5.13
,
\bm{u^{\prime}}\in L^{2}(0,b;L^{2}(I))
, so in
particular
\bm{u^{\prime}}\in L^{2}(a,b;L^{2}(I))
, which by definition implies
(
23
). Hence the claim holds and the third condition of

Lemma

5.47
is satisfied.

Therefore,
Lemma

5.47
implies that
\Delta(\cdot)=\int_{I}f(\cdot,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
is AC
and, for a. e.
t\in(a,b)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta(t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\int_{I}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\partial_{t}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\chi_{\{\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)>\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\}}\left[\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}}(t,x)\right]\left[\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)-\bm{\tilde{v}^{\prime}}(t,x)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(Shown above)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\chi_{\{\bm{u}(t)>\bm{v}(t)\}}\left[\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t)\right]\left[\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)-\bm{v^{\prime}}(t)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(By
Lemma

5.53
)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\left[\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t)\right]^{+}\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\left[\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}-\bm{v}(t){\downarrow}\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(By
Lemma

5.21
).

By
Corollary

5.39
,
\bm{u}(t),\bm{v}(t)\in H^{1}(I)
and hence are AC,
and by
Fact

5.49
,
\left[\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t)\right]^{+}
is AC.
Therefore we can integrate by parts and, using
Fact

5.49
and the
boundary condition
\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}=\partial_{x}\bm{v}(t){\downarrow}=0
on

\{0,1\}
from
Lemma

5.21
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta(t)

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}\chi_{\{\bm{u}(t)>\bm{v}(t)\}}\left[\partial_{x}(\bm{u}(t)-\bm{v}(t))\right]\left[\partial_{x}(\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}-\bm{u}(t){\downarrow})\right]

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}\chi_{\{\bm{u}(t)>\bm{v}(t)\}}\left[\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t)-\partial_{x}\bm{v}(t)\right]\left[\partial_{x}^{-}\bm{u}(t)-\partial_{x}^{-}\bm{u}(t)\right]

(By
Proposition

5.28
).

But for any numbers
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, it is the case that
\alpha\geq\beta\implies(\alpha\land 0)\geq(\beta\land 0)
and
\alpha\leq\beta\implies(\alpha\land 0)\leq(\beta\land 0)
. Hence the integrand above is pointwise nonnegative, and we conclude that
\partial_{t}\Delta(t)\leq 0
for a. e.
t\in(a,b)
. Hence
\Delta(a)\geq\Delta(b)
, as needed.
∎

Corollary 5.55

(“Directed nonexpansiveness” of
P_{t}
)
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
. Then for all
t>0
,

\int_{I}\left[(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)^{+}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{I}\left[(u-v)^{+}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Proposition

5.54
when
u,v\in H^{1}(I)
,
and the general case follows by approximating
u
and
v
by
H^{1}(I)
functions and using the
continuity of the map
w\mapsto P_{t}w
from
L^{2}(I)
to
L^{2}(I)
.
∎

Corollary 5.56

(
P_{t}
is order preserving)
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
, and suppose
u\leq v
a. e. . Then
P_{t}u\leq P_{t}v
a. e. for all
t>0
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Corollary

5.55
.
∎

Lemma 5.57
.

Every nondecreasing
u\in L^{2}(I)
is a stationary point of
P_{t}
, i. e.
P_{t}u=u
for all
t
.

Proof.

By definition,
u=u{\uparrow}+u{\downarrow}
with
u{\uparrow}=u
and
u{\downarrow}=0
, so
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=0
. Thus for every
v\in L^{2}(I)
,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)\geq 0=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle 0,v-u\right\rangle\,,

so
0\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Thus
\bm{u}(t)=u
is the solution to the gradient flow problem
with initial state
u
.
∎

Observation 5.58
.

Corollary

5.56
in particular implies that if
u\geq a
a. e. for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
, then
P_{t}u\geq P_{t}a=a
a. e. for all
t>0
, the equality by

Lemma

5.57
.

We also observe below that
P_{t}
is degree one positively homogeneous and additive when one argument
is a constant function, as the following results show.

Lemma 5.59
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
v\vcentcolon=u+\beta
. Then
v\in\mathcal{U}
with
v{\uparrow}=u{\uparrow}+\beta
and
v{\downarrow}=u{\downarrow}
, and thus
\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)=\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the definition of
v{\uparrow},v{\downarrow}
.
∎

Lemma 5.60
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
\alpha>0
, and
v\vcentcolon=\alpha u
. Then
v\in\mathcal{U}
with
v{\uparrow}=\alpha u{\uparrow}
and
v{\downarrow}=\alpha u{\downarrow}
, and thus
\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)=\alpha^{2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the definitions of
v{\uparrow},v{\downarrow}
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}
.
∎

Lemma 5.61
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
v\vcentcolon=u+\beta
. Then
\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to prove that
\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\subseteq\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
. Let
z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
. Then for all
w\in\mathcal{U}
, using
Lemma

5.59
twice and the
definition of subdifferential,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(w)=\mathcal{E}^{-}(w-\beta)\geq\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle z,w-\beta-u\right\rangle=\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)+\left\langle z,w-v\right\rangle\,,

and hence
z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.62
.

Let
u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
,
\alpha>0
, and
v\vcentcolon=\alpha u
. Then
\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)=\alpha\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to prove that
\alpha\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\subseteq\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
.
Let
z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
; we claim that
\alpha z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
. Indeed for all
w\in\mathcal{U}
, using
Lemma

5.60
twice and the definition of subdifferential,

\mathcal{E}^{-}(w)=\alpha^{2}\mathcal{E}^{-}\left(\tfrac{1}{\alpha}w\right)\geq\alpha^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)+\left\langle z,\tfrac{1}{\alpha}w-u\right\rangle\right)=\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)+\left\langle\alpha z,w-v\right\rangle\,,

and hence
\alpha z\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(v)
as desired.
∎

Proposition 5.63

(Effect of certain affine transformations on
P_{t}
)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
,
\alpha>0
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
P_{t}(\alpha u+\beta)=\alpha P_{t}u+\beta
.

Proof.

We suffices to prove the statement for
u\in\mathcal{U}
; the general case then follows by
approximating
\mathcal{U}
by
H^{1}(I)
functions and the continuity of
P_{t}
from
L^{2}(I)
to
L^{2}(I)
.
For
v\vcentcolon=u+\beta
, we observe that
\bm{v}(t)\vcentcolon=\bm{u}(t)+\beta
with

\bm{v^{\prime}}(t)\vcentcolon=\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
is a solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state

v
, since for each
t>0
for which
-\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))
,

Lemma

5.61
implies that
-\bm{v^{\prime}}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{v}(t))
.

Similarly, let
w\vcentcolon=\alpha u
. Then
\bm{w}(t)\vcentcolon=\alpha\bm{u}(t)
with
\bm{w^{\prime}}(t)\vcentcolon=\alpha\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
is the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state
w
,
since for each
t>0
for which
-\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(\bm{u}(t))
,

Lemma

5.62
implies that
-\bm{w^{\prime}}(t)\in\partial\mathcal{E}^{-}(w)
.
∎

5.8
Convergence to monotone equilibrium

Since the directed Dirichlet energy
\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{t}u)
decays over time and
P_{t}u
converges to some
limit as
t\to\infty
(by
Proposition

5.24
), we expect this limit to be a monotone
function. Let us establish this fact and other properties of that limit.

Lemma 5.64
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
satisfy
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=0
. Then
u
is nondecreasing.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=0
, we have
\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=0
a. e. in
I
. Hence
u{\downarrow}
is a
constant function, while
u{\uparrow}
is nondecreasing by definition.
∎

Proposition 5.65
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
. Then there exists a nondecreasing
u^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
, unique as an element of

L^{2}(I)
, such that
P_{t}u\to u^{*}
in
L^{2}(I)
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}^{-}
achieves its minimum (namely
0
, on e. g. constant functions) and
P_{t}u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})
for all
t>0
,
[
AC84
, Theorem 2, p. 160]
implies that there exists a
minimizer
u^{*}
of
\mathcal{E}^{-}
such that
P_{t}u\rightharpoonup u^{*}
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
. This means that

u^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u^{*})=0
, so by
Lemma

5.64

u^{*}
is
nondecreasing. By
Proposition

5.24
(which we may apply because, fixing any
t_{0}>0
, we have
P_{t_{0}}u\in D(\partial\mathcal{E}^{-})\subseteq D(\mathcal{E}^{-})=\mathcal{U}
),
P_{t}u
also converges
strongly in
L^{2}(I)
, and it is standard that the weak and strong limits agree and that this
limit is unique.
∎

Therefore the following definition is justified:

Definition 5.66

(Monotone equilibrium)
.

Let
P_{\infty}:L^{2}(I)\to\mathcal{U}
be the operator mapping each
u\in L^{2}(I)
to the unique (as an
element of
L^{2}(I)
) nondecreasing
u^{*}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
P_{t}u\to u^{*}
in
L^{2}(I)
as
t\to\infty
. We call
P_{\infty}u
the
monotone equilibrium
of
u
.

We now pass to the limit
P_{\infty}
some useful properties of
P_{t}
.

Proposition 5.67

(“Directed nonexpansiveness” of
P_{\infty}
)
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
. Then

\int_{I}\left[(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)^{+}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{I}\left[(u-v)^{+}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

This follows by a standard limit argument as follows. Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
. By

Corollary

5.55
,
\|(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|(u-v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}
for all
t>0
. By definition of
P_{\infty}
, we have
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u

and
P_{t}v\to P_{\infty}v
in
L^{2}(I)
as
t\to\infty
. Using the triangle inequality, we
obtain

\displaystyle\|(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)^{+}-(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq\|(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)-(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)\|_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{t}u-P_{\infty}u\|_{L^{(}I)}+\|P_{t}v-P_{\infty}v\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to 0\,,

so
(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)^{+}\to(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)^{+}
in
L^{2}(I)
as
t\to\infty
. Since

\|(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|(u-v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}
for all
t>0
, we conclude that

\|(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|(u-v)^{+}\|_{L^{2}(I)}
, which gives the
conclusion.
∎

Corollary 5.68

(
P_{\infty}
is order preserving)
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
, and suppose
u\leq v
a. e. . Then
P_{\infty}u\leq P_{\infty}v
a. e. .

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Proposition

5.67
.
∎

Proposition 5.69
.

P_{\infty}
is nonexpansive, and therefore continuous, as an
L^{2}(I)\to L^{2}(I)
map.

Proof.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
. In the proof of
Proposition

5.67
, we showed
that
\|(P_{t}u-P_{t}v)-(P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v)\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to 0
, which implies that
\|P_{t}u-P_{t}v\|_{L^{2}(I)}\to\|P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v\|_{L^{2}(I)}
. But
\|P_{t}u-P_{t}v\|_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\|u-v\|_{L^{2}(I)}
for all
t>0
since
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a nonexpansive semigroup, so
the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 5.70
.

For every nondecreasing
u\in L^{2}(I)
, we have
P_{\infty}u=u
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

5.57
along with the convergence
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u
in
L^{2}(I)
.
∎

Strong convergence to the monotone equilibrium, together with the preservation of regularity results
from
Section

5.5
, allows us to obtain regularity of the monotone
equilibrium as well:

Proposition 5.71

(
H^{1}
regularity of the monotone equilibrium)
.

Let
u\in H^{1}(I)
. Then
P_{\infty}u\in H^{1}(I)
with
\varphi(P_{\infty}u)\leq\varphi(u)
.

Proof.

Recall the functional
\varphi
from
Section

5.5
. Since
u\in H^{1}(I)
,
\varphi(u)<+\infty
. By
Proposition

5.38
, we conclude that

\varphi(P_{t}u)\leq\varphi(u)<+\infty
for all
t>0
. Since
\varphi
is lower
semicontinuous by
Claim

5.33
and
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u
in
L^{2}(I)
by

Proposition

5.24
, we conclude that
\varphi(P_{\infty}u)\leq\varphi(u)<+\infty
. Hence
P_{\infty}u\in H^{1}(I)
.
∎

Proposition 5.72

(Lipschitz regularity of the monotone equilibrium)
.

Let
u\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
. Then
P_{\infty}u\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
with
\psi(P_{\infty}u)\leq\psi(u)
.

Proof.

Recall the functional
\psi
from
Section

5.6
. Since
u\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
,
\psi(u)<+\infty
. By
Proposition

5.44
, we conclude that

\psi(P_{t}u)\leq\psi(u)<+\infty
for all
t>0
. Since
\psi
is lower semicontinuous by

Claim

5.41
and
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u
in
L^{2}(I)
by

Proposition

5.24
, we conclude that
\psi(P_{\infty}u)\leq\psi(u)<+\infty
.
Hence
P_{\infty}u\in W^{1,\infty}(I)
.
∎

We observe that
P_{\infty}
also behaves nicely under the appropriate class of affine
transformations.

Proposition 5.73

(Effect of certain affine transformations on
P_{\infty}
)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
,
\alpha>0
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
P_{\infty}(\alpha u+\beta)=\alpha P_{\infty}u+\beta
.

Proof.

Since
P_{t}v\to P_{\infty}v
as
t\to\infty
for each
v\in L^{2}(I)
, applying

Proposition

5.63
yields

P_{\infty}(\alpha u+\beta)=\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{t}(\alpha u+\beta)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left[\alpha P_{t}u+\beta\right]=\alpha\left[\lim_{t\to\infty}P_{t}u\right]+\beta=\alpha P_{\infty}u+\beta\,.\qed

We can finally conclude, via
Lemma

5.46
, that
P_{\infty}
is nonexpansive in the

L^{\infty}
norm.

Proposition 5.74

(
P_{\infty}
is nonexpansive in
L^{\infty}
norm)
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(I)
. Then
\left\lvert P_{\infty}u-P_{\infty}v\right\rvert\leq\esssup\left\lvert u-v\right\rvert
a. e. in

I
.

Proof.

We verify the conditions of
Lemma

5.46
with
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
the set
I

endowed with the Lebesgue measure,
p=2
,
C=L^{2}(I)
, and
T=P_{\infty}
. It is clear that
for all
f\in L^{2}(I)
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
f+r\in L^{2}(I)
. By
Proposition

5.69
,

P_{\infty}
is a continuous
L^{2}(I)\to L^{2}(I)
map. Moreover, for all
f\in L^{2}(I)
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the condition
P_{\infty}(f+r)=P_{\infty}f+r
holds by

Proposition

5.73
. Finally, for all
f,g\in L^{2}(I)
with
f\leq g
a. e. ,
we have that
P_{\infty}f\leq P_{\infty}g
a. e. by
Corollary

5.68
. The
conclusion follows.
∎

6
Directed transport-energy inequality

In this section, we establish a connection between the PDE studied above and the Wasserstein
distance between the initial state
u
and its monotone equilibrium
P_{\infty}u
, via the dynamical
approach embodied by the
Benamou-Brenier formula
, and then
tensorize
this result from
one dimension to
[0,1]^{d}
. For optimal transport concepts, we follow the presentation and formalism
of
[
San15
]
, and also refer to
[
AGS05
,
Vil09
]
.

This section is organized as follows. After preliminary considerations in

Section

6.1
,
Section

6.2
shows that the PDE
results from
Section

5
imply a result about optimal transport
(Wasserstein) distance, namely a one-dimensional
transport-energy inequality
, thereby
implementing the ideas described in
Section

1.2.3
of the
proof overview. The idea is to show that our solution to the directed heat equation

\partial_{t}u=\partial_{x}\partial_{x}^{-}u

satisfies the so-called
continuity equation

\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}+\partial_{x}(\varrho_{t}v_{t})=0\,,

which prescribes that “particles” (distributed according to measure
\varrho_{t}
) move according to
the velocity field
v_{t}
; in our setting, we have
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}=u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and
v_{t}=-\frac{\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}}{u}
(
Proposition

6.8
), so that indeed
\partial_{x}(\varrho_{t}v_{t})=-\partial_{x}\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}=-\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}
. For functions
u

bounded close to
1
, we can informally disregard the denominator in
v_{t}
and imagine, for
simplicity, that
v_{t}\approx-\partial_{x}u{\downarrow}
. Then thanks to the exponential decay of

\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int(\partial_{x}u{\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

from the previous section, we conclude that the right-hand side of the Benamou-Brenier formula

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\min\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\varrho_{t})}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t:\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}+\partial_{x}(\varrho_{t}v_{t})=0,\varrho_{0}=\mu,\varrho_{1}=\nu\right\}

from
Proposition

6.6
is roughly upper bounded by
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
, where (informally) the
measures
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu=(P_{\infty}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
correspond to the initial
state and monotone equilibrium of the directed heat equation, as probability measures
9
9

9

More
formally, we decompose the evolution from time
t=0
to time
t\to\infty
into intervals of
unit time length, but by the exponential energy decay, this summation is dominated by the
initial term
\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)
.
​​. Therefore we conclude in
Section

1.2.3
the
transport-energy inequality in one dimension,

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\mu_{\infty})\lesssim\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,.

Finally, the continuity equation also lets us conclude natural and useful properties of solutions of
the directed heat equation, such as mass conservation
(
Corollaries

6.9
and
6.10
) and the property that solutions can only
ever lose mass in any given prefix of the interval
[0,1]
(
Corollary

6.14
).

The next goal is to establish the
tensorized
(multidimensional) transport-energy inequality,
thereby implementing the ideas described in
Section

1.2.4
of the proof
overview. In
Section

6.3
, we develop the definitions and technical
tools required to show our “Pythagorean composition” result
(
Section

1.2.4
), which yields a recipe for proving multidimensional
transport-energy inequalities (with respect to the “directed Wasserstein distance”) by induction
(
Lemma

6.33
).

Finally,
Section

6.4
ties together the tensorization recipe described above with
the one-dimensional results specific to our PDE from the previous sections. We show that, by taking
an initial state
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with corresponding measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, applying
the directed heat semigroup one coordinate at a time, and putting together the one-dimensional
transport-energy inequalities at each of these steps via properties of our PDE such as order
preservation and nonexpansiveness (which enable the induction to go through), we indeed obtain a

monotone
final state
f^{*}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with corresponding measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}=f^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
for which the multidimensional transport-energy inequality applies
(
Section

1.2.4
):

W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})\lesssim\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\nabla^{-}f\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

6.1
Preliminaries for optimal transport

We start by introducing notation and definitions relevant to the theory of optimal transport.

Projections.

Say
X\times Y
is a product space and
z=(x,y)
is an element in this
space. Then the projection operator onto the first coordinate, denoted interchangeably by
\pi_{1}
or

\pi_{x}
depending on the context, is given by
\pi_{1}(z)=\pi_{x}(z)=x
. Similarly,
\pi_{2}(z)=\pi_{y}(z)=y
. We extend this definition in the natural way to projections from larger product
spaces onto smaller product spaces. For example, given the product space
X_{1}\times\dotsm\times X_{d}
and index set
I\subset[d]
,
I=\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\}
, the operator
\pi_{I}
projects any
element in this space down to an element of
X_{i_{1}}\times\dotsm\times X_{i_{n}}
. We also use the
shorthand
\pi_{-I}\vcentcolon=\pi_{[d]\setminus I}
.

Now, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Borel set and let
I\subseteq[d]
. For a point
x\in\Omega
, we write the projections
x_{I}\vcentcolon=\pi_{I}(x)
and
x_{-I}\vcentcolon=\pi_{-I}(x)
, and we
also write
x=(x_{I},x_{-I})
. Then, for the set
\Omega
, we write the projections
\Omega_{I}\vcentcolon=\{x_{I}:x\in\Omega\}
and
\Omega_{-I}\vcentcolon=\{x_{-I}:x\in\Omega\}
. For small
sets
I
or
[d]\setminus I
, we also use shorthand notation such as
x_{i}\vcentcolon=x_{\{i\}}
and

\Omega_{-i-j}\vcentcolon=\Omega_{-\{i,j\}}
.

Pushforward measure.

Given measurable spaces
(X,\Sigma_{X})
and
(Y,\Sigma_{Y})
,
measure
\mu
on
X
, and measurable map
T:X\to Y
, the
pushforward measure

{T}_{\#}{\mu}
on
Y
is the measure satisfying

\displaystyle({T}_{\#}{\mu})(B)

\displaystyle=\mu(T^{-1}(B))

for all
B\in\Sigma_{Y}
, or equivalently,

\displaystyle\int_{Y}\phi(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}({T}_{\#}{\mu})(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\phi(T(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)

for all measurable
\phi:Y\to\mathbb{R}
.

For a measure
\gamma
on product space
X\times Y
, we say that
{(\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}
and

{(\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}
, which are measures on
X
and
Y
respectively, are the first and
second
marginals
of
\gamma
, respectively.

If
(\Omega_{3},\Sigma_{3})
is another measurable space and
S:Y\to Z
a measurable map, then it
holds that
{(S\circ T)}_{\#}{\mu}={S}_{\#}{({T}_{\#}{\mu})}
.

Transport plans.

For two probability spaces
(\Omega_{1},\Sigma_{1},\mu_{1})
and
(\Omega_{2},\Sigma_{2},\mu_{2})
, we write

\Pi(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
for the set of
couplings
, or
transport plans
, between
\mu_{1}
and

\mu_{2}
, namely probability measures
\gamma
on the product space
\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
whose
first and second marginals are
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, respectively.

Space of probability measures.

For any Borel set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
P(\Omega)
denote the space of all (Borel)
probability measures on
\Omega
. We endow
P(\Omega)
with the weak topology, which is the topology
of weak convergence with respect to bounded continuous functionals. Namely, we say
\mu_{n}
converges
weakly to
\mu
in
P(\Omega)
, and write
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
, if
\int_{\Omega}\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}\to\int_{\Omega}\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
for all bounded continuous
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
.

Wasserstein distances.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
bounded
Borel set and let
p\in[1,+\infty)
. Let
\mu,\nu\in P(\Omega)
. Given transport plan
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
, we define the
cost

C_{p}(\gamma)
by

C_{p}(\gamma)\vcentcolon=\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}|x-y|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)\right)^{1/p}\,.

We then define the
p
-Wasserstein
distance between
\mu
and
\nu
by

W_{p}(\mu,\nu)\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}C_{p}(\gamma)\,,

and we also often refer to the quantity
W_{p}^{p}(\mu,\nu)\vcentcolon=W_{p}(\mu,\nu)^{p}
. It is standard
that
W_{p}(\cdot,\cdot)
is a distance metric on
P(\Omega)
.

Fact 6.1

(Wasserstein distance metrizes weak convergence; see e. g.
[
San15
, Theorems 5.10 and 5.11]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set, and let
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then for a
sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n}
in
P(\Omega)
and
\mu\in P(\Omega)
, we have
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
if and
only if
W_{p}(\mu_{n},\mu)\to 0
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set, and let
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then for
sequences
(\mu_{n})_{n}
and
(\nu_{n})_{n}
in
P(\Omega)
with
\mu_{n}\rightharpoonup\mu
and
\nu_{n}\rightharpoonup\nu
for
\mu,\nu\in P(\Omega)
, we have
W_{p}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\to W_{p}(\mu,\nu)
. In other
words, the
p
-Wasserstein distance is continuous in (the weak topology on)
P(\Omega)
.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality, we have

W_{p}(\mu,\nu)-W_{p}(\mu,\mu_{n})-W_{p}(\nu_{n},\nu)\leq W_{p}(\mu_{n},\nu_{n})\leq W_{p}(\mu_{n},\mu)+W_{p}(\mu,\nu)+W_{p}(\nu,\nu_{n})\,.

By
Fact

6.1
, the LHS and RHS converge to
W_{p}(\mu,\nu)
as
n\to\infty
, so
the conclusion follows.
∎

6.2
Optimal transport via Benamou-Brenier

Let
\{\varrho_{t}:t\in[0,T]\}
be a family of measures on
\overline{I}
and
\{v_{t}:t\in[0,T]\}
be a family of
velocity fields
such that
v_{t}\in L^{1}(\varrho_{t})
for each
t
. We
start by defining what it means for the family
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})
to solve the
continuity
equation

\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}+\partial_{x}(\varrho_{t}v_{t})=0\,.

Definition 6.3

(Weak solution; see
[
San15
, Section 4.1.2]
)
.

Let
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})
be a family of measure/velocity field pairs indexed by
t\in[0,T]

such that
v_{t}\in L^{1}(\varrho)
for each
t
. We say that
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})
is a
weak
solution to the continuity equation
if, for every test function
\psi\in C^{1}(\overline{I})
,
the function
t\mapsto\int_{\overline{I}}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}
is absolutely continuous in
t

and, for a. e.
t
, we have

\partial_{t}\int_{\overline{I}}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}=\int_{\overline{I}}(\partial_{x}\psi)v_{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}\,.

(24)

In this case, we call
\varrho_{0}
and
\varrho_{T}
the
initial
and
final states
of
the solution, respectively (this makes sense because the above implies that
t\mapsto\varrho_{t}
is continuous for the weak convergence of measures).

Remark 6.4
.

We will only work with absolutely continuous measures
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}=u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
,
u\in L^{2}(I)
.

Remark 6.5
.

Applying (
24
) with a constant test function
\psi
shows that every
solution to the continuity equation is
mass conserving
. In particular,

Proposition

6.8
below implies that this is true of the semigroup
P_{t}
; see

Corollary

6.9
.

The continuity equation is connected to Wasserstein distance via the
Benamou-Brenier formula
:

Proposition 6.6

(Benamou-Brenier formula; see e. g.
[
San15
, Theorem 5.28]
)
.

Let
\mu,\nu
be probability measures on
\overline{I}
. Then

W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\min\left\{\int_{0}^{1}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\varrho_{t})}^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t:\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}+\partial_{x}(\varrho_{t}v_{t})=0,\varrho_{0}=\mu,\varrho_{1}=\nu\right\}\,,

where the constraint above means that
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a weak solution to the
continuity equation with initial state
\mu
and final state
\nu
.

We now show our solution to the directed heat equation yields a solution to the continuity equation.
This allows us to conclude several nice properties of this solution, such as mass conservation, as
well as apply the Benamou-Brenier formula to obtain a
transport-energy inequality
.

Condition 6.7
.

u\in\mathcal{U}
is a. e. positive and bounded away from zero, and satisfies
\int_{I}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=1
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
satisfy
Condition

6.7
. Let
\bm{u}(t)
be the solution to the
gradient flow problem with initial state
u
, and
\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)
its weak derivative for each
t\geq 0
. Let
T>0
and define the measure/velocity field family
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
by

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}\vcentcolon=\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\quad\text{and}\quad v_{t}\vcentcolon=-\frac{\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}}{\bm{u}(t)}

for each
t\in[0,T]
. Then
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})
is a weak solution to the continuity equation
with initial state
\bm{u}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and final state
\bm{u}(T)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

Proof.

We first note that, by
Observation

5.58
, each
\bm{u}(t)
is a. e. positive and bounded away from zero, uniformly in
t
. In particular, this justifies the
denominator in the definition of
v_{t}
. Furthermore, recall that
\bm{u}(t)\in\mathcal{U}
for all
t\geq 0
by
Corollaries

5.20
and
5.15
, so the
numerator in the definition of
v_{t}
is defined up to sets of measure zero for each
t
. (In
fact, by

Definitions

5.16
,
5.18
and
5.21
we
have
\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}\in H^{2}(I)
for a. e.
t>0
, in which case the numerator is even
pointwise well-defined.)

We now verify that
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})
satisfies
Definition

6.3
. The initial and
final states are as claimed by construction. Let
\psi\in C^{1}(\overline{I})
. Let
\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}},\bm{\tilde{u}}:[0,T]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
be obtained by applying

Lemma

5.53
to
\bm{u}
with time domain
[0,T]
. Define the (jointly
measurable) function
f:[0,T]\times I\to\mathbb{R}
by

f(t,x)\vcentcolon=\psi(x)\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)\,.

We claim that
f
satisfies the properties of
Lemma

5.47
. It is clear that
f(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(I)\subset L^{1}(I)
for each
t\in[0,T]
since
\bm{u}(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(I)

while
\psi
is bounded, so the first property is satisfied. Also, for each
x\in I
we have
that
\bm{\tilde{u}}(\cdot,x)
is AC and hence so is
f(\cdot,x)
, so the second property is
satisfied. Finally, by properties 1 and 5 of
Lemma

5.53
and the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\int_{(0,T)}\int_{I}\left\lvert\partial_{t}f(t,x)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{(0,T)}\int_{I}\left\lvert\psi(x)\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)\right\rvert\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(I)}\int_{(0,T)}\|\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\|_{L^{2}(I)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t<+\infty\,,

the last inequality since
\psi\in C^{1}(\overline{I})
while
\bm{u^{\prime}}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(I))
by

Definition

5.13
. Hence the third property is satisfied and

Lemma

5.47
applies. Thus the function mapping each
t\in[0,T]
to

\int_{\overline{I}}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}=\int_{I}\psi\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\psi(x)\bm{\tilde{u}}(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

is absolutely continuous and, for a. e.
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\int_{\overline{I}}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\partial_{t}f(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\psi(x)\bm{\tilde{u}^{\prime}}(t,x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(Property 5 of
Lemma

5.53
)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\psi\bm{u^{\prime}}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(Property 1 of
Lemma

5.53
)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\psi\partial_{x}\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(
Propositions

5.18
and
5.21
)

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}(\partial_{x}\psi)(\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(Integration by parts,
Lemma

5.21
)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(\partial_{x}\psi)\left(-\frac{\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}}{\bm{u}(t)}\right)\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\overline{I}}(\partial_{x}\psi)v_{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}\,.

Corollary 6.9

(
P_{t}
is mass conserving)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
. Then for all
t>0
,
\int_{I}P_{t}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

Proof.

If
u
satisfies
Condition

6.7
, then this follows from

Proposition

6.8
by taking any constant test function
\psi
in

Definition

6.3
.

If
u\in H^{1}(I)
, then it is bounded, so let
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
v\vcentcolon=\alpha u+\beta\in H^{1}(I)
satisfies
Condition

6.7
. Then
u=\frac{1}{\alpha}v-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}
and, by
Proposition

5.63
and the above,

\int_{I}P_{t}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}P_{t}v-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{I}P_{t}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{I}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}v-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Finally, let
u\in\mathcal{U}
be arbitrary. Since
H^{1}(I)
is dense in
L^{2}(I)
, let
(u_{n})_{n}
be a
sequence in
H^{1}(I)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}(I)
. By the continuity of
P_{t}
from
L^{2}(I)

to
L^{2}(I)
, and using the above,

\int_{I}P_{t}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{I}P_{t}u_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{I}u_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

By passing to the limit
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u
in
L^{2}(I)
, we also conclude

Corollary 6.10

(
P_{\infty}
is mass conserving)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
. Then
\int_{I}P_{\infty}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.

We now upper bound the Wasserstein distance between
u\in\mathcal{U}
and its monotone equilibrium by
combining the Benamou-Brenier formula with the exponential decay of the directed Dirichlet energy,
at least as long as
u
is positive and bounded away from zero.

\thmundirectedtransportenergy

Proof.

Let
a\vcentcolon=\inf u
. By
Corollaries

5.56
and
6.9
,
P_{t}u

satisfies
Condition

6.7
with
P_{t}u\geq a
a. e. for all
t\geq 0
. For each

t\geq 0
, define the measure
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{t}\vcentcolon=(P_{t}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then
Proposition

6.8
applies to time interval
[\ell,\ell+1]
(we
refrain from introducing excessive notation for such relabeling of intervals) with initial/final
states
\varrho_{\ell}
and
\varrho_{\ell+1}
, respectively, so that
Proposition

6.6

gives

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\varrho_{\ell},\varrho_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\ell}^{\ell+1}\int_{I}\left(-\frac{\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}}{\bm{u}(t)}\right)^{2}\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\ell}^{\ell+1}\int_{I}\frac{(\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow})^{2}}{\bm{u}(t)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a}\int_{\ell}^{\ell+1}\int_{I}(\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\frac{2}{a}\int_{\ell}^{\ell+1}\mathcal{E}^{-}(P_{t}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{a}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\int_{\ell}^{\ell+1}e^{-Kt}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t<\frac{2}{a}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\int_{\ell}^{\infty}e^{-Kt}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=\frac{2e^{-K\ell}}{aK}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\,,

where the penultimate inequality and the constant
K
come from
Proposition

5.22
.
Now, by the triangle inequality (on the distance
W_{2}
, not the squared distance
W_{2}^{2}
), for
every
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have

\displaystyle W_{2}(\varrho_{0},\varrho_{\ell})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}W_{2}(\varrho_{j},\varrho_{j+1})\leq\left(\frac{2}{aK}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\right)^{1/2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}e^{-Kj/2}=\left(\frac{2}{aK}\mathcal{E}^{-}(u)\right)^{1/2}\frac{1}{1-e^{-K/2}}\,.

Therefore, extracting the appropriate constant
C
from the terms involving
K
, we obtain

W_{2}(\varrho_{0},\varrho_{\ell})\leq\frac{C}{a^{1/2}}(\mathcal{E}^{-}(u))^{1/2}\,.

Recall that
P_{t}u\rightharpoonup P_{\infty}u
weakly in
L^{2}(I)
by

Proposition

5.65
. Hence, defining the absolutely continuous measure

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varrho_{\infty}\vcentcolon=(P_{\infty}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, we conclude that
\varrho_{t}\rightharpoonup\varrho_{\infty}
as
t\to\infty
. Then by
Corollary

6.2
,

W_{2}(\varrho_{0},\varrho_{\infty})=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}W_{2}(\varrho_{0},\varrho_{\ell})\leq\frac{C}{a^{1/2}}(\mathcal{E}^{-}(u))^{1/2}\,.\qed

The definition of the velocity field
v_{t}
in
Proposition

6.8
, along with the fact
that
\partial_{x}\bm{u}(t){\downarrow}\leq 0
, gives
v_{t}\geq 0
, which intuitively says that
“particles only move to the right”​​. This suggests that the solution
\bm{u}(t)
can only lose
mass in any prefix of the interval
(0,1)
over time. The following items make this observation
rigorous.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\delta\in I
and let
J\vcentcolon=(0,\delta)
. Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
. Then for all
t>0
,

\int_{J}(P_{t}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{J}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

Suppose
u
satisfies
Condition

6.7
; the general case will then follow by the
same arguments as in the proof of
Corollary

6.9
. Let
J^{\prime}\vcentcolon=(-\infty,\delta)
, let
\chi_{J^{\prime}}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be the characteristic function for the set
J^{\prime}
, and
for each
\varepsilon>0
, let
\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}
be its mollification by the standard
mollifier. Note that each
\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}
is nonincreasing. Also note that
\chi_{J^{\prime}}=\chi_{J}
pointwise in
I
. Now, fix
T>0
. Since the family
(\varrho_{t},v_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}

given by
Proposition

6.8
solves the continuity equation, applying
(
24
) with test function
\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{I})

gives that
t\mapsto\int_{I}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
is AC and, for a. e.
t\in[0,T]
,

\partial_{t}\int_{I}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{I}\underbrace{(\partial_{x}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon})}_{\leq 0}\underbrace{v_{t}}_{\geq 0}\underbrace{\bm{u}(t)}_{\geq 0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq 0\,.

Hence
t\mapsto\int_{I}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\bm{u}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
is nonincreasing, and obtain

\left\langle\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon},u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}=\int_{I}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\bm{u}(0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{I}\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\bm{u}(T)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\left\langle\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon},P_{T}u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}\,.

Now, since
\chi_{J^{\prime}}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, we have that
\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\to\chi_{J^{\prime}}
in

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, so in particular
\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}\to\chi_{J^{\prime}}=\chi_{J}
in
L^{2}(I)
. Thus
we have

\int_{J}(P_{T}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\left\langle\chi_{J},P_{T}u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon},P_{T}u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\langle\chi_{J^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon},u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}=\left\langle\chi_{J},u\right\rangle_{L^{2}(I)}=\int_{J}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
\delta\in I
and let
J\vcentcolon=(0,\delta)
. Let
u\in L^{2}(I)
. Then

\int_{J}(P_{\infty}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{J}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

6.11
along with the convergence
P_{t}u\to P_{\infty}u
in

L^{2}(I)
.
∎

Definition 6.13
.

Let
\mu
and
\nu
be probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
. We say
\mu

dominates

\nu
, and
write
\mu\succeq\nu
, if for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\mu(-\infty,x)\geq\nu(-\infty,x)
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
satisfy
Condition

6.7
. Define the measures
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\vcentcolon=u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{\infty}\vcentcolon=(P_{\infty}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
. Then
\mu\succeq\mu_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We may view
\mu
and
\mu_{\infty}
as absolutely continuous measures on all of
\mathbb{R}
, taking
zero outside
[0,1]
. Now, for each
x\in[0,1]
,
Corollary

6.12
gives

\mu(-\infty,x)=\int_{(0,x)}u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\geq\int_{(0,x)}(P_{\infty}u)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\mu_{\infty}(-\infty,x)\,.

On the other hand, for
x<0
both sides are zero, and for
x>1
, both sides are
1
by

Corollary

6.10
.
∎

6.3
Directed optimal transport and Pythagorean composition

We now introduce directed versions of basic optimal transport concepts and theory, with the goal of
combining a directed version of weak Kantorovich duality with a multidimensional directed version of
the transport-energy inequality from
Section

1.2.3
to obtain our desired
directed Poincaré inequality via a perturbation argument. In the interest of space, we refrain
starting a systematic study of directed optimal transport, but rather limit ourselves to the results
we require.

Definition 6.15

(Quasimetric)
.

Let
X
be a set. An extended real-valued function
d:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
, not
necessarily symmetric, is called a
quasimetric
on
X
if it satisfies the following:

1.

For all
x,y\in X
,
d(x,y)\geq 0
with
d(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
.

2.

For all
x,y,z\in X
,
d(x,z)\leq d(x,y)+d(y,z)
.

Definition 6.16
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Borel set, and let
\mu
and
\nu
be probability measures on

\Omega
. We define the set
\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
of
directed couplings
, or
directed
transport plans
, from
\mu
to
\nu
as

\Pi(\mu\to\nu)\vcentcolon=\left\{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu):\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=0\right\}\,,

where the integral is well-defined because the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x\not\preceq y\}
is open and hence Borel measurable. Note that the condition could also be
written as
\gamma\left(\{x\not\preceq y\}\right)=0
, or
\gamma\left(\{x\preceq y\}\right)=1
.

Definition 6.17

(Directed Wasserstein distance)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set and let
p\in[1,\infty)
. Given two
probability distributions
\mu,\nu
over
\Omega
, we define the
directed
p
-Wasserstein
distance from
\mu
to
\nu
by

W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)}C_{p}(\gamma)\,,

and we write
W_{p}^{p}(\mu\to\nu)\vcentcolon=W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)^{p}
. Note that we may have
W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)=+\infty
, and that
W_{p}(\cdot\to\cdot)
is not symmetric in general.

Observation 6.18
.

For all measures
\mu
and
\nu
, we have
\Pi(\mu\to\nu)\subset\Pi(\mu,\nu)
and hence

W_{p}(\mu,\nu)\leq W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)
.

Lemma 6.19

(Gluing lemma; see e. g.
[
San15
, Lemma 5.5]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Borel set. Let
\mu,\varrho,\nu
be probability measures on

\Omega
, and let
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi(\mu,\varrho)
and
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi(\varrho,\nu)
. Then
there exists a probability measure
\sigma
on
\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega
such that

{(\pi_{x,y})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{+}
and
{(\pi_{y,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{-}
.

The proof of that
W_{p}(\cdot\to\cdot)
is a quasimetric follows the presentation of
[
San15
, Lemma
5.4]
, with a simple additional argument to handle
directed
couplings.

Lemma 6.20

(Composition of directed transport plans)
.

In
Lemma

6.19
, if
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi(\mu\to\varrho)
and
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi(\varrho\to\nu)
, then
{(\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\vcentcolon={(\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}
. First, since
{(\pi_{x})}_{\#}{\gamma}={(\pi_{x}\circ\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}={(\pi_{x}\circ\pi_{x,y})}_{\#}{\sigma}={(\pi_{x})}_{\#}{\gamma^{+}}=\mu
, and similarly
{(\pi_{z})}_{\#}{\gamma}=\nu
, we have

\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
. Moreover, by definition of pushforward measure we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq z\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq z\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)\leq\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}\left(\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}+\chi_{\{y\not\preceq z\}}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)+\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{y\not\preceq z\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}(x,y)+\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{y\not\preceq z\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}(y,z)=0\,,

the last equality since
\gamma^{+}
and
\gamma^{-}
are directed couplings. Hence
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
as claimed.
∎

Proposition 6.21
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set and
p\in[1,\infty)
. Then
W_{p}(\cdot\to\cdot)
is a quasimetric on
P(\Omega)
.

Proof.

It is clear that
W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)\geq 0
always. If
\mu=\nu
then the identity coupling
shows that
W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)=0
, and conversely if
W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)=0
then, by

Observation

6.18
,
W_{p}(\mu,\nu)=0
and hence
\mu=\nu
. It remains to show that
W_{p}(\cdot\to\cdot)
satisfies the triangle inequality.

Let
\mu,\varrho,\nu
be probability measures on
\Omega
. Let
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi(\mu\to\varrho)
and
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi(\varrho\to\nu)
. Then since we also have
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi(\mu,\varrho)
and
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi(\varrho,\nu)
, apply
Lemma

6.19
to obtain a
probability measure
\sigma
on
\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega
such that

{(\pi_{x,y})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{+}
and
{(\pi_{y,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{-}
. Let

\gamma\vcentcolon={(\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}
, so that
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
by

Lemma

6.20
. Then

\displaystyle W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}|x-z|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,z)\right)^{1/p}=\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}|x-z|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\||x-z|\|_{L^{p}(\sigma)}\leq\||x-y|+|y-z|\|_{L^{p}(\sigma)}\leq\||x-y|\|_{L^{p}(\sigma)}+\||y-z|\|_{L^{p}(\sigma)}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}|x-y|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)\right)^{1/p}+\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega\times\Omega}|y-z|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)\right)^{1/p}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}|x-y|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}(x,y)\right)^{1/p}+\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}|y-z|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}(y,z)\right)^{1/p}\,.

Since
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi(\mu\to\varrho),\gamma^{-}\in\Pi(\varrho\to\nu)
were arbitrary,

W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)\leq W_{p}(\mu\to\varrho)+W_{p}(\varrho\to\nu)
.
∎

The directed Wasserstein distance arises naturally in the one-dimensional case when one probability
measure dominates the other in the sense of
Definition

6.13
, as we now show.

Proposition 6.22

(Specialization of
[
San15
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty)
. Let
\mu
and
\nu
be two absolutely continuous probability measures
on
\overline{I}
with strictly positive densities. Then there exists a unique
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
attaining
C_{p}(\gamma)=W_{p}(\mu,\nu)
, and in fact
\gamma={(\mathsf{id},T_{\mathrm{mon}})}_{\#}{\mu}
where
T_{\mathrm{mon}}:\overline{I}\to\overline{I}
is a nondecreasing function that is
a. e. uniquely determined, and
\mathsf{id}
denotes the identity map.

Corollary 6.23
.

If in
Proposition

6.22
we have
\mu\succeq\nu
, then in fact
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
. As a consequence,
W_{p}(\mu\to\nu)=W_{p}(\mu,\nu)
.

Proof.

We claim that
T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)\geq x
for every
x\in I
. Indeed, suppose
T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)<x
for some
x\in I
. Then

\nu(0,T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x))=({T_{\mathrm{mon}}}_{\#}{\mu})(0,T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x))=\mu(T_{\mathrm{mon}}^{-1}(0,T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)))\geq\mu(0,x)\,,

the inequality because certainly
(0,x)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{mon}}^{-1}(0,T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x))
, but we do not rule
out at this point that
T
remains constant for a while after
x
. But since
T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)<x
and

\nu
has strictly positive density by assumption, we conclude that

\nu(0,x)>\nu(0,T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x))\geq\mu(0,x)\,,

contradicting the assumption that
\mu\succeq\nu
. Hence
T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)\geq x
for every
x\in I

as claimed. We now show that
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
. Let
S\vcentcolon=\{(x,y)\in\overline{I}\times\overline{I}:x\leq y\}
. Since
\gamma={(\mathsf{id},T_{\mathrm{mon}})}_{\#}{\mu}
,
we have

\gamma(S)=\mu((\mathsf{id},T_{\mathrm{mon}})^{-1}(S))=\mu(\{x\in\overline{I}:x\leq T_{\mathrm{mon}}(x)\})=\mu(\overline{I})=1\,,

so
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
as claimed.
∎

We now introduce the formal language for two related operations: constructing a probability measure
by its marginal and conditional distributions, and the inverse process of extracting marginal and
conditionals from a probability measure, which is also called
disintegration
. We refer the
reader to
[
AGS05
, Section 5.3]
for an overview of these ideas from a measure-theoretic
perspective.

We use the following definition from
[
AGS05
]
. For
X
and
Y
separable metric spaces and
x\in X\mapsto\mu_{x}\in P(Y)
a measure-valued map, we say
\mu_{x}
is a Borel map if
x\mapsto\mu_{x}(B)
is a Borel map for any Borel set
B\subset Y
, or equivalently if this holds for any open
set
A\subset Y
. In this case we also have that

x\mapsto\int_{Y}f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(y)

is Borel for any bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function
f:X\times Y\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 6.24

(Construction and disintegration)
.

Let
S
be a finite set and let
I\subset S
. Let
x_{S\setminus I}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}\mapsto{\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{I})
be a Borel map (the
conditionals
).
Then for any bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function
f:\mathbb{R}^{S}\to\mathbb{R}
, the
\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}\to\mathbb{R}
map

x_{S\setminus I}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{I}}f(x_{S\setminus I},x_{I})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}}(x_{I})

(25)

is Borel. Therefore, for any
{\mu}_{S\setminus I}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I})
(the

marginal
), we define
\mu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{S})
implicitly by

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{S\setminus I}(x_{S\setminus I})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{I}}f(x_{S\setminus I},x_{I})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}}(x_{I})\,,

(26)

and we formally write
\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}}{\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{S\setminus I}
.

Conversely, given
\mu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{S})
, we let
{\mu}_{S\setminus I}\vcentcolon={(\pi_{S\setminus I})}_{\#}{\mu}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I})
and write
({\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}})_{x_{S\setminus I}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}}\subset P(\mathbb{R}^{I})
for the

{\mu}_{S\setminus I}
-a. e. uniquely determined Borel family of probability measures
such that
\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S\setminus I}}{\mu}_{|x_{S\setminus I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{S\setminus I}
, and we call this decomposition a
disintegration
of

\mu
.

Examples and simplified notational conventions.

Since the index notation in the
definition above can be somewhat laborious to parse, let us briefly give two concrete settings we
will use below, and introduce simplified notational conventions for those settings. The
simplifications are supposed to be mnemonic for what we have already defined for projections.

1.

For a probability measure
\mu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and index
i\in[d]
, we may disintegrate

\mu
into a marginal on index set
[d]\setminus\{i\}
and conditionals supported along

i
-th coordinate, and write
\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{[d]\setminus\{i\}}}{\mu}_{|x_{[d]\setminus\{i\}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{[d]\setminus\{i\}}
. We simplify this notation by
writing
\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}
.

2.

For a transport plan
\gamma\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we write the index set as
[2d]=S=S_{x}\cup S_{y}
for
S_{x}\vcentcolon=[d]
and
S_{y}\vcentcolon=[2d]\setminus[d]
, so we write
each element in the support of
\gamma
as a tuple
(x,y)
with
x\in\mathbb{R}^{S_{x}}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{S_{y}}
. Then, given index set
I\subset[d]
, we may disintegrate
\gamma
into a
marginal on index set
S_{M}\vcentcolon=(S_{x}\setminus I_{x})\cup(S_{y}\setminus I_{y})
for
I_{x}\vcentcolon=I
and
I_{y}\vcentcolon=\{d+i:i\in I\}
, so that we think of the marginal as
determining all but the
I
-indexed coordinates of the points
x
and
y
; and we view the
conditionals as supported along the
I_{x}\cup I_{y}
directions. This disintegration is

\gamma=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{S_{M}}}{\gamma}_{|x_{S_{M}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{S_{M}}
. To be
clear, here we have
{\gamma}_{S_{M}}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{S_{M}})
and
{\gamma}_{|x_{S_{M}}}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{I_{x}\cup I_{y}})
for each
x_{S_{M}}\in\mathbb{R}^{S_{M}}
. We make the notation somewhat
more mnemonic by writing
\gamma=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}}{\gamma}_{|x_{-I},y_{-I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}
. When we are considering a singleton
I=\{i\}
, we further simplify the notation by directly writing
i
in the place of
I

in this formula.

For absolutely continuous probability measures on
[0,1]^{d}
, the following more familiar
characterization of disintegrations will be useful:

Proposition 6.25
.

Let
\mu
be an absolutely continuous probability measure whose density is Borel measurable and
supported in
[0,1]^{d}
. Namely, write
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=u\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
where
u:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
is a Borel map. Let
i\in[d]
. Then the disintegration
\mu=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}
is as follows:
{\mu}_{-i}
and each

{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}
are absolutely continuous probability measures with Borel densities
supported in
[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
and
[0,1]
respectively; and writing
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}={u}_{-i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}
, and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}={u}_{|x_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}
for each
x_{-i}
, where the (Borel) density functions are

{u}_{-i}:[0,1]^{d}_{-i}\to[0,+\infty)
and
{u}_{|x_{-i}}:[0,1]\to[0,+\infty)
for each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, we have (marginal density)

{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})=\int_{I}u(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

for each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, and (conditional density)

{u}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})=\frac{u(x)}{{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})}

for each
x\in[0,1]^{d}
where the denominator is nonzero, or
1
by convention otherwise.

Proof.

First, note that it is standard that
{u}_{-i}
is a Borel map since it is the integral
of the section
u(x_{-i},\cdot)
at each point
x_{-i}
. It is hence immediate that each

{u}_{|x_{-i}}
is also Borel. Thus
{\mu}_{-i}
and all
{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}
are
indeed absolutely continuous probability measures.

We now check the conditions of
Definition

6.24
. We claim that the map
x_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\mapsto{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}\in P(\mathbb{R})
is Borel. Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}
be a Borel set;
we may assume that
B\subset[0,1]
without loss of generality, since all measures assign zero
outside this interval. Then the map
x_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\mapsto{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}(B)
takes
value zero outside
[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, and for
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, it is

x_{-i}\mapsto\frac{1}{{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})}\int_{B}u(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,.

This is again, at each point, the integral of a section over the subspace
B
, so it is standard
that this map is Borel. Hence the claim holds.

It remains to verify (
26
). Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
be a
nonnegative Borel function (and the result for bounded
f
will also follow). By the definition
of the measures and Tonelli’s theorem, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{u}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\int_{I}f(x_{-i},x_{i}){u}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x){u}_{-i}(x_{-i}){u}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x)u(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

where the last equality holds because
u(x)={u}_{-i}(x_{-i}){u}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})

whenever
{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})>0
, and if
{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})=0
then
u(x_{-i},\cdot)=0
almost everywhere, so
\left\{x\in[0,1]^{d}:u(x)>0\text{ and }{u}_{-i}(x_{-i})=0\right\}
is Borel and has measure zero (by another application of
Tonelli’s theorem).
∎

Definition 6.26

(Aligned transport plans)
.

Let
I\subsetneq[d]
be nonempty and let
\mu,\nu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
and write its disintegration
\gamma=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}}{\gamma}_{|x_{-I},y_{-I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}
. We say
\gamma
is

I
-aligned
if its marginal
{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I})
satisfies the following:

{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}\left(\left\{(x_{-I},y_{-I})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}:x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\right\}\right)=0\,.

Note that this condition is well-defined because the set being measured is open and hence Borel.
By convention, we say that every
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is
[d]
-aligned. For any nonempty

I\subseteq[d]
, we denote the set of
I
-aligned transport plans by
\Pi_{I}(\mu,\nu)
. When
we have a singleton
I=\{i\}
, we write
i
directly in the place of
I
in this definition.

Definition 6.27
.

For nonempty
I\subseteq[d]
and
\mu,\nu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, write
\Pi_{I}(\mu\to\nu)\vcentcolon=\Pi(\mu\to\nu)\cap\Pi_{I}(\mu,\nu)
.

Lemma 6.28

(Alternative characterization of
I
-aligned plans)
.

Let
I\subsetneq[d]
be nonempty and let
\mu,\nu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is
I
-aligned if and only if

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=0\,.

Proof.

Writing the disintegration
\gamma=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}}{\gamma}_{|x_{-I},y_{-I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}(\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{I}\times\mathbb{R}^{I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-I},y_{-I}}(x_{I},y_{I})}_{=1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-I}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-I_{x}-I_{y}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{I}\times\mathbb{R}^{I}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-I},y_{-I}}(x_{I},y_{I})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma\,.\qed

While we defined
I
-aligned transport plans for probability measures on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we extend
the definition to measures on subsets
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
(e. g. on the cube) by canonically
extending any such measure to all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by assigning zero outside of
\Omega
.

Lemma 6.29

(Composition of aligned transport plans)
.

Let
I,J\subseteq[d]
be nonempty. Then in
Lemma

6.19
, if
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi_{I}(\mu,\varrho)
and
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi_{J}(\varrho,\nu)
, then
{(\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}\in\Pi_{I\cup J}(\mu,\nu)
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\vcentcolon={(\pi_{x,z})}_{\#}{\sigma}
and
K\vcentcolon=I\cup J
. As in the proof of

Lemma

6.20
, we do have
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
, so it remains to show
that
\gamma
is
K
-aligned (and we may assume that
K\subsetneq[n]
, otherwise there is
nothing to prove). We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-K}\neq z_{-K}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-K}\neq z_{-K}\}}(x,z)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

(Pushforward)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\chi_{\{x_{-K}\neq y_{-K}\}}(x,y)+\chi_{\{y_{-K}\neq z_{-K}\}}(y,z)\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-K}\neq y_{-K}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{y_{-K}\neq z_{-K}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-K}\neq y_{-K}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{y_{-K}\neq z_{-K}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}

(Pushforward)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{y_{-J}\neq z_{-J}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}

(
I,J\subseteq K
)

\displaystyle\qquad=0\,,

the last step since
\gamma^{+}
is
I
-aligned and
\gamma^{-}
is
J
-aligned and by

Lemma

6.28
; and again by the latter,
\gamma
is
K
-aligned.
∎

The following results let us find aligned transport plans by transporting mass only within

i
-aligned lines.

Lemma 6.30

(Measurable selection; specialization of
[
Vil09
, Corollary 5.22]
)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded set. Let
p\in[1,\infty)
. Let
A
be a measurable
space and let
a\mapsto(\mu_{a},\nu_{a})
be a measurable function
A\to P(\mathbb{R})\times P(\mathbb{R})

with each
\mu_{a}
and
\nu_{a}
having bounded support. Then there is a measurable choice
a\mapsto\gamma_{a}
such that, for each
a\in A
,
\gamma_{a}\in\Pi(\mu_{a},\nu_{a})
and

C_{p}(\gamma_{a})=W_{p}(\mu_{a},\nu_{a})
.

Lemma 6.31
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
,
i\in[d]
, and let
\mu,\nu\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be supported inside some
bounded set. Suppose
{\mu}_{-i}={\nu}_{-i}
. Then there exists
\gamma\in\Pi_{i}(\mu,\nu)
satisfying

C_{p}(\gamma)^{p}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}W_{p}^{p}({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

Proof.

Let
A\vcentcolon=\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}
be equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{[d]\setminus\{i\}}
. Then the
A\to P(\mathbb{R})\times P(\mathbb{R})
function
x_{-i}\mapsto({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})
is Borel measurable because, by disintegration, both
x_{-i}\mapsto{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}
and
x_{-i}\mapsto{\nu}_{|x_{-i}}
are Borel measurable. By

Lemma

6.30
, we obtain a Borel measurable map
x_{-i}\mapsto\gamma_{x_{-i}}
such that, for each
x_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}
,
\gamma_{x_{-i}}\in\Pi({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})
and
C_{p}(\gamma_{x_{-i}})^{p}=W_{p}^{p}({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})
.

Define
\gamma\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
by its disintegration
\gamma=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}
as
follows. First, for the marginal
{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i})
, for each Borel
Z\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}
we set

{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(Z)\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{Z}(x_{-i},x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,,

where the integral is well-defined because the set
\{x_{-i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}:(x_{-i},x_{-i})\in Z\}
is Borel by standard arguments. We remark that
{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}
is
indeed a probability measure because it is nonnegative, it assigns zero to the empty set, it is
additive over countable disjoint unions, and

{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}=1\,.

It follows that for every bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have
