\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}f(x_{-i},y_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}f(x_{-i},x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

(27)

Second, for the conditionals, we simply set
{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}\vcentcolon=\gamma_{x_{-i}}
for each
(x_{-i},y_{-i})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}
.

We claim that
\gamma\in\Pi_{i}(\mu,\nu)
. The
i
-aligned condition holds by construction, so
it remains to show that
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
. We first verify that

{(\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}=\mu
. It suffices to verify that these measures agree on each
rectangle
X\vcentcolon=X_{-i}\times X_{i}
of Borel sets
X_{-i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}
,
X_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}
. We have

\displaystyle({(\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma})(X)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{X}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(Pushforward)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{X}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

(Disintegration)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{X_{-i}}(x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{X_{i}}(x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma_{x_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

(Definition of
X,{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}
)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{X_{-i}}(x_{-i})({(\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma_{x_{-i}}})(X_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})

(Pushforward)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{X_{-i}}(x_{-i}){\mu}_{|x_{-i}}(X_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})

(
\gamma_{x_{-i}}\in\Pi({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})
)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{X_{-i}}(x_{-i}){\mu}_{|x_{-i}}(X_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})

(Application of (
27
))

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{X_{-i}}(x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{X_{i}}(x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{X}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})

(Definition of
X
)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{X}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\mu(X)

\displaystyle\text{(Disintegration)}\,,

as desired. Thanks to the hypothesis that
{\mu}_{-i}={\nu}_{-i}
, an
analogous calculation on the second variable yields that
{(\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}=\nu
and
hence
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
. Thus
\gamma\in\Pi_{i}(\mu,\nu)
as claimed.

Finally, we compute the cost of the plan
\gamma
. Again using its disintegration and
(
27
), we have

\displaystyle C_{p}(\gamma)^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|(x_{-i},x_{i})-(y_{-i},y_{i})|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|(x_{-i},x_{i})-(x_{-i},y_{i})|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},x_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{p}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma_{x_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})C_{p}(\gamma_{x_{-i}})^{p}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}W_{p}^{p}({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.\qed

We may combine the above to find
directed
transport plans by transporting mass within
axis-aligned lines, as long as the first probability distribution dominates the second in each such
line.

Lemma 6.32
.

Let
p\in(1,+\infty)
, and let
\mu,\nu\in P([0,1]^{d})
be absolutely continuous
probability measures with strictly positive densities. Let
i\in[d]
and suppose that
1)
{\mu}_{-i}={\nu}_{-i}
and 2)
{\mu}_{|x_{-i}}\succeq{\nu}_{|x_{-i}}
for each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
. Then there exists
\gamma\in\Pi_{i}(\mu\to\nu)
satisfying

C_{p}(\gamma)^{p}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{p}^{p}({\mu}_{|x_{-i}},{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

(28)

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\Pi_{i}(\mu,\nu)
be the plan obtained from
Lemma

6.31
, which
satisfies (
28
). After recalling the definition of
\gamma
in

Lemma

6.31
,
Corollary

6.23
implies that
{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}\in\Pi({\mu}_{|x_{-i}}\to{\nu}_{|x_{-i}})
for each
(x_{-i},y_{-i})\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}\times[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
. It remains to confirm that
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{\{x\not\preceq y\}}((x_{-i},x_{i}),(y_{-i},y_{i}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

(Disintegration)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{\{x_{-i}\not\preceq y_{-i}\}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}(x_{-i},y_{-i})\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\chi_{\{x_{i}>y_{i}\}}(x_{i},y_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}(x_{i},y_{i})}_{\text{$=0$
since ${\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}$ is a directed plan}}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\chi_{\{x_{-i}\not\preceq y_{-i}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{-i_{x}-i_{y}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\gamma}_{|x_{-i},y_{-i}}}_{=1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}\underbrace{\chi_{\{x_{-i}\not\preceq x_{-i}\}}}_{=0}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu}_{-i}(x_{-i})

(By (
27
))

\displaystyle\quad=0\,.

Now, we show that the directed, aligned
2
-Wasserstein distance enjoys a nice “Pythagorean”
composition property: composing
I
-aligned and
J
-aligned directed transport plans, for
I
and

J
disjoint, yields an
I\cup J
-aligned directed transport plan whose cost is obtained from the
costs of the two other plans via the Pythagorean theorem. Arguments of this nature are well-known in
the undirected case, e. g. a similar statement appears in
[
Vil09
, p. 572]
.

\lemmapythagoreancomposition

Proof.

Using
Lemma

6.19
, we obtain
\sigma\in P(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

{(\pi_{1,2})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{+}
and
{(\pi_{2,3})}_{\#}{\sigma}=\gamma^{-}
, and
by
Lemma

6.20
and
Lemma

6.29
, we have
\gamma\vcentcolon={(\pi_{1,3})}_{\#}{\sigma}\in\Pi_{I\cup J}(\mu\to\nu)
. To compute the cost of

\gamma
, we first claim that
\sigma
assigns zero measure to points
x,y,z
such that

x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}
or
y_{-J}\neq z_{-J}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1-\chi_{\{x_{-I}=y_{-I}\text{ and }y_{-J}=z_{-J}\}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{y_{-J}\neq z_{-J}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}\neq y_{-I}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{y_{-J}\neq z_{-J}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}(y,z)=0\,,

the last step by
Lemma

6.28
since
\gamma^{+}
and
\gamma^{-}
are
I
- and

J
-aligned, respectively. Hence
\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int f\chi_{\{x_{-I}=y_{-I}\text{ and
}y_{-J}=z_{-J}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma
for any bounded or nonnegative Borel function
f
. Therefore,
using the assumption that
I
and
J
are disjoint to apply the Pythagorean theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{2}(\gamma)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}=y_{-I}\text{ and }y_{-J}=z_{-J}\}}|x-z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\chi_{\{x_{-I}=y_{-I}\text{ and }y_{-J}=z_{-J}\}}\left(|x-y|^{2}+|y-z|^{2}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(|x-y|^{2}+|y-z|^{2}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y-z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{+}(x,y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|y-z|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma^{-}(y,z)

\displaystyle=C_{2}(\gamma^{+})^{2}+C_{2}(\gamma^{-})^{2}\,.\qed

Lemma 6.33

(Induction over coordinates)
.

Let
(\mu^{(i)})_{i\in[d+1]}
be a family of absolutely continuous probability measures on

[0,1]^{d}
with strictly positive densities. Suppose that, for each
i\in[d]
, we have
1)
{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}={\mu^{(i+1)}}_{-i}
and 2)
{\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}\succeq{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}}
for each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
. Then

W_{2}^{2}(\mu^{(1)}\to\mu^{(d+1)})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

Proof.

For each
k\in[d+1]
, let
A(k)
be the following proposition: there exists
\gamma\in\Pi_{[k]}(\mu^{(1)}\to\mu^{(k+1)})
satisfying

C_{2}(\gamma)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

Note that
A(d)
implies the result we want to prove, while
A(1)
holds by

Lemma

6.32
. Now let
2\leq k\leq d
. Suppose
A(k-1)
holds, and
thus let
\gamma^{+}\in\Pi_{[k-1]}(\mu^{(1)}\to\mu^{(k)})
satisfy

C_{2}(\gamma^{+})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,.

Apply
Lemma

6.32
with
i=k
to obtain a plan
\gamma^{-}\in\Pi_{k}(\mu^{(k)}\to\mu^{(k+1)})
satisfying

C_{2}(\gamma^{-})^{2}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-k}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(k)}}_{|x_{-k}},{\mu^{(k+1)}}_{|x_{-k}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(k)}}_{-k}(x_{-k})\,.

Then, use
Section

1.2.4
with
I=[k-1]
and
J=\{k\}
to obtain

\gamma\in\Pi_{[k]}(\mu^{(1)}\to\mu^{(k+1)})
satisfying

C_{2}(\gamma)^{2}=C_{2}(\gamma^{+})^{2}+C_{2}(\gamma^{-})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})\,,

which implies that
A(k)
holds. Thus
A(d)
follows by induction.
∎

6.4
From one-dimensional PDE to optimal transport in the cube

We now tie most of the foregoing theory together by showing how to inductively transform a function
on the unit cube into a monotone function, one coordinate at a time via the directed heat semigroup
machinery, keeping track of the cost using the directed optimal transport machinery.

For this part of the proof, it is convenient to restrict our attention to Lipschitz functions. As we
will see, the Lipschitz property of
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is preserved under taking the
monotone equilibrium along each axis-aligned direction, which enables an inductive argument over the
coordinates.

Denote the set of Lipschitz functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\Lip
(the dimension
d
will
always be clear from context).

Observation 6.34
.

A function
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz if and only if its restriction to every
axis-aligned line is uniformly Lipschitz, i. e. there exists some
M>0
such that, for every
i\in[d]
and
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
,
f(x_{-i},\cdot):[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is

M
-Lipschitz.

Definition 6.35

(
i
- and
I
-monotonicity)
.

Let
i\in[d]
. We say
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
i
-monotone
if its every
restriction along direction
i
is nondecreasing, i. e. if for every
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
,
f(x_{-i},\cdot)
is nondecreasing. For
I\subseteq[d]
, we say
f
is

I
-monotone
if it is
i
-monotone for every
i\in I
. We write
\Mon_{I}
for the set of

I
-monotone functions.

Observation 6.36
.

\Mon_{[d]}
is simply the set of monotone functions on
[0,1]^{d}
.

The following useful characterization of
i
-monotone functions in terms of
k
-aligned lines
follows by definition:

Observation 6.37
.

Let
i,k\in[d]
be distinct. Then for all
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
f
is
i
-monotone if
and only if the following holds: for every
z_{-i-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}
, and for all
x_{i}\leq y_{i}
in
[0,1]
,
f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)\leq f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)
.

Definition 6.38

(Monotone equilibrium operator)
.

Let
k\in[d]
. We define the operator
\mathcal{M}_{k}:\Lip\to([0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R})
as follows:
for each function
f\in\Lip
,
x_{-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-k}
and
x_{k}\in[0,1]
, the function

\mathcal{M}_{k}f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-k},x_{k})\vcentcolon=(P_{\infty}f(x_{-k},\cdot))(x_{k})\,,

where the application of
P_{\infty}
is well-defined because each
f(x_{-k},\cdot)
is Lipschitz
and hence in
L^{2}(I)
.

The following lemma shows that
\mathcal{M}_{k}
preserves Lipschitzness, confirming that
\mathcal{M}_{k}f
is
well-defined pointwise as a real-valued function, as opposed to only defined up to sets of measure
zero.

Lemma 6.39
.

Let
k\in[d]
. Let
f\in\Lip
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{k}f\in\Lip
.

Proof.

Let
M>0
be such that every axis-aligned line restriction of
f
is
M
-Lipschitz. We claim
that every axis-aligned line restriction of
\mathcal{M}_{k}f
is
M
-Lipschitz. We first check the

k
-aligned lines. For any
x_{-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-k}

Proposition

5.72
implies that

\psi((\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-k},\cdot))=\psi(P_{\infty}f(x_{-k},\cdot))\leq\psi(f(x_{-k},\cdot))\leq M\,,

the last inequality and the conclusion that
(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-k},\cdot)
is
M
-Lipschitz by

Fact

5.40
.

It remains to check the other line restrictions. Let
i\in[d]\setminus\{k\}
and let

z_{-i-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}
. Let
x_{i}\neq y_{i}\in[0,1]
. Since the axis-aligned line
restrictions of
f
(in particular, in direction
i
) are
M
-Lipschitz, we have

\sup_{w_{k}\in[0,1]}\left\lvert f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},w_{k})-f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},w_{k})\right\rvert\leq M|x_{i}-y_{i}|\,.

Proposition

5.74
implies that

\left\lvert P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)-P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)\right\rvert\leq\esssup_{w_{k}\in[0,1]}\left\lvert f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},w_{k})-f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},w_{k})\right\rvert\leq M|x_{i}-y_{i}|

(29)

a. e. in
I
, and since
P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)
and
P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)
are (Lipschitz) continuous as observed above, we conclude that (
29
)
holds pointwise. Since (
29
) holds for every
i\in[d]\setminus\{k\}
and

z_{-i-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}
, and since

\left\lvert(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)-(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)\right\rvert=\left\lvert P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)-P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)\right\rvert

pointwise, we get that every axis-aligned line restriction of
\mathcal{M}_{k}f
is
M
-Lipschitz, and

\mathcal{M}_{k}f\in\Lip
.
∎

Lemma 6.40

(
\mathcal{M}_{k}
application makes progress)
.

Let
k\in[d]
, and let
I\subseteq[d]\setminus\{k\}
. Let
f\in\Lip\cap\Mon_{I}
. Then

\mathcal{M}_{k}f\in\Lip\cap\Mon_{I\cup\{k\}}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{M}_{k}f\in\Lip
is given by
Lemma

6.39
, and the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{k}f

is
k
-monotone follows from the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
and the fact that the monotone equilibrium

P_{\infty}f(x_{-k},\cdot)
is nondecreasing. Now, let
i\in I
, so that
f
is
i
-monotone.
By
Observation

6.37
, we have that for every
z_{-i-k}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}
, and for all
x_{i}\leq y_{i}
in
[0,1]
,
f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)\leq f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)
. By

Corollary

5.68
, we obtain that
P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)\leq P_{\infty}f(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)
a. e. in
I
, and since these two functions are (Lipschitz)
continuous, this inequality holds pointwise. Thus
(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(z_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot)\leq(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(z_{-i-k},y_{i},\cdot)
, and again by
Observation

6.37
,
\mathcal{M}_{k}f
is
i
-monotone. This
concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.41

(
\mathcal{M}_{k}
preserves bounds)
.

Let
f\in\Lip
. Let
a\leq b
be real numbers and suppose
a\leq f\leq b
. Then
a\leq\mathcal{M}_{k}f\leq b
.

Proof.

This follows by the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
together with

Corollaries

5.68
and
5.70
.
∎

Lemma 6.42

(
\mathcal{M}_{k}
is nonexpansive in
L^{2}
)
.

Let
f,g\in\Lip
. Then

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\mathcal{M}_{k}f-\mathcal{M}_{k}g)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(f-g)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

5.69
and Tonelli’s theorem, as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\mathcal{M}_{k}f-\mathcal{M}_{k}g)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-k}\int_{I}\left[(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-k},x_{k})-(\mathcal{M}_{k}g)(x_{-k},x_{k})\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-k}\int_{I}\left[(P_{\infty}f(x_{-k},\cdot))(x_{k})-(P_{\infty}g(x_{-k},\cdot))(x_{k})\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-k}\int_{I}\left[f(x_{-k},x_{k})-g(x_{-k},x_{k})\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(f-g)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Similarly, combining
Corollary

6.10
with Fubini’s theorem yields

Lemma 6.43
.

Let
f\in\Lip
. Then

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

The following result is essentially an
L^{2}
version of
[
Fer23
, Proposition 3.14]
.

Lemma 6.44

(Effect of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
on the directed Dirichlet energy)
.

Let
i,k\in[d]
be distinct and let
f\in\Lip
. Then

\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}((\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\leq\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\,.

Proof.

For any
g\in\Lip
,
i\in[d]
and
h\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, define the
Q_{g,i,h}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by

Q_{g,i,h}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\frac{g(x_{-i},x_{i}+h)-g(x_{-i},x_{i})}{h}&\text{if $x_{i}+h\in(0,1)$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(30)

By Rademacher’s theorem on the open domain
(0,1)^{d}
,
g
is differentiable almost everywhere.
In particular, the function
D_{g,i}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

D_{g,i}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\lim_{h\to 0}Q_{g,i,h}(x)&\text{if the limit exists}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

gives the partial derivative of
g
in direction
i
for a. e.
x\in[0,1]^{d}
, and is
measurable as the limit of the measurable functions
Q_{g,i,h}
. Moreover, letting
M>0
be
such that the axis-aligned line restrictions of
g
are
M
-Lipschitz, we have
\left\lvert Q_{g,i,h}\right\rvert\leq M
pointwise. Finally, fixing any
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
,
g(x_{-i},\cdot)
is
(Lipschitz and hence) in
H^{1}(I)
, and the partial derivative
D_{g,i}(x_{-i},\cdot)
agrees
with any weak derivative
\partial_{x_{i}}g(x_{-i},\cdot)
a. e. in
[0,1]
. In particular, all
of these considerations apply to the functions
f
and
\mathcal{M}_{k}f
by
Lemma

6.39
. We
conclude that, for
g=f
or
g=\mathcal{M}_{k}f
and each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{-}(g(x_{-i},\cdot))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\left[\partial_{x_{i}}^{-}g(x_{-i},x_{i})\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\left[D_{g,i}(x_{-i},x_{i})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\lim_{h\to 0}\left[Q_{g,i,h}(x_{-i},x_{i})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\,,

where for simplicity we write the limit in the last expression with the understanding that it is
only defined almost everywhere in
[0,1]
. Note that the measurability of the last integrand
over
[0,1]^{d}
justifies writing the integrals in the statement of the lemma via Tonelli’s
theorem (as the computation below shows). Define for each
h\in\mathbb{R}
the set

I_{h}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}(0,1-h)&\text{if }h\geq 0\\
(-h,1)&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

so that
Q_{g,i,h}(x)
is defined by the first case of (
30
) if and only if
x_{i}\in I_{h}
. Then, by repeated applications of Tonelli’s theorem and the dominated convergence theorem,
we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}((\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\int_{I}\lim_{h\to 0}\left[Q_{\mathcal{M}_{k}f,i,h}(x_{-i},x_{i})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\lim_{h\to 0}\underbrace{\left[Q_{\mathcal{M}_{k}f,i,h}(x)^{-}\right]^{2}}_{\leq M^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left[Q_{\mathcal{M}_{k}f,i,h}(x)^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i-k}\int_{I}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int_{I}\left[Q_{\mathcal{M}_{k}f,i,h}(x_{-i-k},x_{i},x_{k})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i-k}\int_{I_{h}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int_{I}\left[\left(\frac{(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-i-k},x_{i}+h,x_{k})-(\mathcal{M}_{k}f)(x_{-i-k},x_{i},x_{k})}{h}\right)^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i-k}\int_{I_{h}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int_{I}\left[\left(\frac{(P_{\infty}f(x_{-i-k},x_{i}+h,\cdot))(x_{k})-(P_{\infty}f(x_{-i-k},x_{i},\cdot))(x_{k})}{h}\right)^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\,\leq\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i-k}\int_{I_{h}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int_{I}\left[\left(\frac{f(x_{-i-k},x_{i}+h,x_{k})-f(x_{-i-k},x_{i},x_{k})}{h}\right)^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i-k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i-k}\int_{I}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int_{I}\left[Q_{f,i,h}(x_{-i-k},x_{i},x_{k})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{k}

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{h\to 0}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\underbrace{\left[Q_{f,i,h}(x)^{-}\right]^{2}}_{\leq M^{2}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\lim_{h\to 0}\left[Q_{f,i,h}(x)^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\,=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\int_{I}\lim_{h\to 0}\left[Q_{f,i,h}(x_{-i},x_{i})^{-}\right]^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\,,

the inequality by
Proposition

5.67
via the identity
\left[(a-b)^{+}\right]^{2}=\left[(b-a)^{-}\right]^{2}
.
∎

Definition 6.45

(Coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium)
.

For each
f\in\Lip
, define
f^{*}\in\Lip
, the
coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium
of

f
, by

f^{*}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{M}_{d}\mathcal{M}_{d-1}\dotsm\mathcal{M}_{1}f\,.

Note that indeed
f^{*}
is indeed Lipschitz by
Lemma

6.39
, and it is monotone:

Proposition 6.46
.

Let
f\in\Lip
. Then
f^{*}
is monotone.

Proof.

This follows by repeatedly applying
Lemma

6.40
, and recalling that
\Mon_{[d]}
is
the set of monotone functions on
[0,1]^{d}
.
∎

By
Proposition

5.73
and the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{k}
, we also observe that the
coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium behaves nicely with respect to certain affine transformations:

Observation 6.47
.

Let
f\in\Lip
. Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
(\alpha f+\beta)^{*}=\alpha f^{*}+\beta
.

Similarly,
Lemma

6.42
implies that taking the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium
is a nonexpansive operation:

Observation 6.48
.

Let
f,g\in\Lip
. Then
\|f^{*}-g^{*}\|_{L^{2}((0,1)^{d})}\leq\|f-g\|_{L^{2}((0,1)^{d})}
. Since

0^{*}=0
by
Lemma

5.70
(where we write
0
for the constant zero
function), we conclude in particular that
\|f^{*}\|_{L^{2}((0,1)^{d})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{2}((0,1)^{d})}
.

We are now ready to combine the ingredients from the previous sections and establish the
transport-energy inequality. Note that this inequality is most effective when the function
f
is
pointwise bounded close to
1
.

\thmtransportenergy

Proof.

Define the family
(f^{(i)})_{i\in[d+1]}\subset\Lip
by
f^{(1)}\vcentcolon=f
and
f^{(i+1)}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{M}_{i}f^{(i)}
for each
i\in[d]
, so that
f^{(d+1)}=f^{*}
. Note that we have
1-a\leq f^{(i)}\leq 1+a
and
\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f^{(i)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=1
for each
i\in[d+1]
by

Lemmas

6.41
and
6.43
. Then, define the family
(\mu^{(i)})_{i\in[d+1]}
of probability measures on
[0,1]^{d}
by
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu^{(i)}\vcentcolon=f^{(i)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
,
so that
\mu^{(1)}=\mu
and
\mu^{(d+1)}=\mu^{*}
.

We claim
(\mu^{(i)})_{i\in[d+1]}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

6.33
. First,
they have strictly positive densities because
1-a\leq f^{(i)}\leq 1+a
for each
i\in[d+1]

as observed above. They are also absolutely continuous with Borel measurable density because
each
f^{(i)}
is continuous and hence Borel.

Recall that, by
Proposition

6.25
, for each
i,j\in[d+1]
we
may characterize the disintegration
\mu^{(j)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{-i}}{\mu^{(j)}}_{|x_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(j)}}_{-i}
as follows: the marginal and all conditional measures are both
absolutely continuous supported in
[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
and
[0,1]
respectively, and we have

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(j)}}_{-i}={f^{(j)}}_{-i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}
where

{f^{(j)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})=\int_{I}f^{(j)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

(31)

for each
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(j)}}_{|x_{-i}}={f^{(j)}}_{|x_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}
where

{f^{(j)}}_{|x_{-i}}(x_{i})=\frac{f^{(j)}(x)}{{f^{(j)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})}

(32)

for each
x\in[0,1]^{d}
(note that the denominator is nonzero since
f^{(j)}\geq 1-a
).

Now, fix any
i\in[d]
. Then the condition
{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}={\mu^{(i+1)}}_{-i}
is implied by the pointwise condition

\int_{I}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\int_{I}f^{(i+1)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}\qquad\forall x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}\,,

(33)

which in turn holds by the definitions of
f^{(i+1)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, and

Corollary

6.10
. Finally, fixing
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
, we verify
the condition
{\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}\succeq{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}}
. By

Corollary

6.14
, it suffices to show that

{f^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{=}}P_{\infty}{f^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}\,.

(34)

By (
31
) and (
32
), this is equivalent to

\frac{1}{\int_{I}f^{(i+1)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}}f^{(i+1)}(x_{-i},\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{=}}P_{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\int_{I}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot)\right)\,.

Since
f^{(i+1)}(x_{-i},\cdot)=P_{\infty}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot)
, and applying

Proposition

5.73
, the above is equivalent to

\frac{1}{\int_{I}f^{(i+1)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}}P_{\infty}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\int_{I}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},x_{i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}}P_{\infty}f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot)\,,

which is true again by (
33
), so indeed
{\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}\succeq{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}}
. Thus, by
Lemma

6.33
, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}}){f^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+a)\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\,,

the last inequality because
1-a\leq f^{(i)}\leq 1+a
also implies that
1-a\leq{f^{(i)}}_{-i}\leq 1+a
. Now, applying
Section

1.2.3
via
(
34
), we have

W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\leq\frac{C}{\inf{f^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}}\mathcal{E}^{-}({f^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}})

for each
i\in[d]
and
x_{-i}\in[0,1]^{d}_{-i}
. Note that
{f^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}}\geq\frac{1-a}{1+a}
by the pointwise bounds on
f^{(i)}
and
{f^{(i)}}_{-i}
, and that

\mathcal{E}^{-}(\alpha g)=\alpha^{2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(g)
for any
\alpha\geq 0
and (say) Lipschitz
g
by the
definition of
\mathcal{E}^{-}
. Therefore, applying (
32
), we obtain

W_{2}^{2}({\mu^{(i)}}_{|x_{-i}},{\mu^{(i+1)}}_{|x_{-i}})\leq\frac{C(1+a)}{1-a}\left(\frac{1}{{f^{(i)}}_{-i}(x_{-i})}\right)^{2}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot))\leq\frac{C(1+a)}{(1-a)^{3}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot))\,.

Hence we have

W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})\leq\frac{C(1+a)^{2}}{(1-a)^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f^{(i)}(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\,.

Recall that
f^{(1)}=f
while
f^{(i)}=\mathcal{M}_{i-1}\dotsm\mathcal{M}_{1}f
for each
i=2,\dotsc,d+1
.
Hence, by inductively applying
Lemma

6.44
for each
i\geq 2
, and by Tonelli’s
theorem and the definition of
\mathcal{E}^{-}
, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C(1+a)^{2}}{(1-a)^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(f(x_{-i},\cdot))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(1+a)^{2}}{2(1-a)^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{[0,1]^{d}_{-i}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{-i}\int_{I}\left(\partial_{x_{i}}^{-}f(x_{-i},x_{i})\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{C(1+a)^{2}}{2(1-a)^{3}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\partial_{i}^{-}f\right)^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{C(1+a)^{2}}{2(1-a)^{3}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\nabla^{-}f\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

7
Directed Poincaré from Wasserstein and Kantorovich

In this section, we establish our directed Poincaré inequality
(
Theorem

1.1
) by combining the transport-energy inequality from

Section

1.2.4
with a directed version of (weak) Kantorovich duality via a perturbation
argument, thereby implementing the ideas from
Section

1.2.5
of the proof overview.
The general theme of going between transport inequalities and Poincaré inequalities, as well as the
spirit of the technical arguments presented here, are well-known in the undirected case; we refer
the reader to
[
Vil09
, Chapters 7 and 22]
for a comprehensive presentation and primary
references, and to
[
Liu20
]
for an undirected version of the precise reduction we instantiate
here (that is, from a transport-energy inequality to a Poincaré inequality).

For simplicity, we do not attempt to initiate a systematic study of duality in directed optimal
transport, or to state results in the broadest possible generality, but rather limit ourselves to
what is required for the aforementioned goal. In
Section

7.1
, we introduce the notion of
directed weak Kantorovich duality and establish some of its properties. The main results are

Proposition

7.4
, which gives a
lower bound
on the directed Wasserstein distance between
two measures in terms the so-called (directed)
Hamilton-Jacobi operator

\vec{H}
(recall that
the previous section gave an
upper bound
on this distance via the transport-energy
inequality), and
Proposition

7.6
, which relates the action
\vec{H}_{t}h(x)
of this operator
back to the directed gradient
\nabla^{+}h(x)
(which, recall, we wish to have in the right-hand side
of our Poincaré inequality). Then, in
Section

7.2
, we apply these tools via a
careful choice of dual function (a “perturbation argument”) to conclude the directed Poincaré
inequality, first for continuously differentiable functions (
Theorem

7.8
) and then, via a
standard approximation argument, for all
H^{1}
functions (
Theorem

1.1
).

7.1
Directed weak Kantorovich duality and Hamilton-Jacobi operator

Kantorovich duality plays a fundamental role in the study of Wasserstein distance. The weak part of
this duality allows one to lower bound the Wasserstein distance between two distributions by means
of “test functions” satisfying a certain constraint. Here, we translate a small part of this rich
theory to the directed setting.

Lemma 7.1

(Weak duality)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set and let
\mu,\nu\in P(\Omega)
. Suppose

\phi\in L^{1}(\nu)
and
\psi\in L^{1}(\mu)
satisfy

\phi(y)-\psi(x)\leq|x-y|^{2}\qquad\forall x\preceq y\text{ in }\Omega\,.

Then

W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\nu)\geq\int_{\Omega}\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\Omega}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\,.

Proof.

Suppose
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
. Then

\displaystyle C_{2}(\gamma)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}|x-y|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\preceq y\}}|x-y|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(Since
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{\{x\preceq y\}}(\phi(y)-\psi(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(Hypothesis)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}(\phi(y)-\psi(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)

(Since
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\phi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\Omega}\psi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle\text{(Since ${(\pi_{1})}_{\#}{\gamma}=\mu,{(\pi_{2})}_{\#}{\gamma}=\nu$)}\,.

Since this holds for every
\gamma\in\Pi(\mu\to\nu)
, the result follows.
∎

This duality results motivates the following definition. Let
C_{b}(\Omega)
denote the set of bounded
continuous functions
\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 7.2

(Directed Hamilton-Jacobi operator)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For each
t\geq 0
, define the
directed Hamilton-Jacobi
operator

\vec{H}_{t}:C_{b}(\Omega)\to(\Omega\to\mathbb{R})
as follows: for each
h\in C_{b}(\Omega)

and
x\in\Omega
, we set

(\vec{H}_{t}h)(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}h(x)&\text{if $t=0$}\\
\sup_{y\succeq x}\left\{h(y)-\frac{1}{2t}|x-y|^{2}\right\}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(35)

Remark 7.3
.

The definition above is a directed analogue of the so-called (backward)

Hamilton-Jacobi-Hopf-Lax-Oleinik semigroup
. We do not claim that
\vec{H}_{t}
forms a
semigroup.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded Borel set, and let
\mu,\nu\in P(\Omega)
. Let
h\in C_{b}(\Omega)
. Then

\tfrac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\nu)\geq\int_{\Omega}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu-\int_{\Omega}(\vec{H}_{1}h)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\,.

Proof.

Let
\phi\vcentcolon=2h
and
\psi\vcentcolon=2\vec{H}_{1}h
. Note that
\phi\in L^{1}(\nu)
since
h
is
bounded. The fact that
h
is bounded also implies that
\vec{H}_{1}h
is bounded, as can be
verified from its definition, and hence
\psi\in L^{1}(\mu)
. Finally, for each
x\preceq y
in

\Omega
we have

\displaystyle\phi(y)-\psi(x)

\displaystyle=2h(y)-2\sup_{y^{\prime}\succeq x}\left\{h(y^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}|x-y^{\prime}|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{2}+\underbrace{2\left[\left(h(y)-\frac{1}{2}|x-y|^{2}\right)-\sup_{y^{\prime}\succeq x}\left\{h(y^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}|x-y^{\prime}|^{2}\right\}\right]}_{\leq 0}\leq|x-y|^{2}\,.

Thus
Lemma

7.1
gives the conclusion.
∎

The following simple lemma plays a key role in the perturbation argument.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
t>0
and let
h\in C_{b}(\Omega)
. Then
\vec{H}_{1}(th)=t\vec{H}_{t}h
.

Proof.

Indeed, for each
x\in\Omega
we have

(\vec{H}_{1}(th))(x)=\sup_{y\succeq x}\left\{th(y)-\frac{1}{2}|x-y|^{2}\right\}=t\sup_{y\succeq x}\left\{h(y)-\frac{1}{2t}|x-y|^{2}\right\}=t((\vec{H}_{t}h)(x))\,.\qed

Notation.

For
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
an open set and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
,
write
C^{k}(\overline{\Omega})
for the set of restrictions to
\overline{\Omega}
of functions in

C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

For
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, point
x\in\Omega
, and
r>0
, let
B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)\vcentcolon=B^{\circ}(x,r)\cap\Omega
, where
B^{\circ}(x,r)\vcentcolon=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:0<|x-y|<r\}
is the
open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
with
x
itself excluded, and let
B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)\vcentcolon=B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:y\succeq x\}
.

The following result gives a directed analogue to some of the properties of the Hamilton-Jacobi
semigroup, as presented in
[
Vil09
, Theorem 22.16]
.
10
10

10

Note that our use of the notation

\nabla^{+}h=0\lor\nabla h
,
\nabla^{-}h=0\land\nabla h
is unrelated to the use of similar
notation in
[
Vil09
]
, where it denotes a notion of norm of the gradient for functions in more
general spaces where the usual derivative may not be defined. In particular, their definition agrees
with the norm of the gradient for differentiable functions, while the point is the our definition
(the directed gradient) does not.

Proposition 7.6

(Some properties of the directed Hamilton-Jacobi operator)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. Let
h\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
.

(a)

Let
C\vcentcolon=\sup h-\inf h
, which is well-defined because
h
is continuous over
the compact set
\overline{\Omega}
and thus bounded. Then for each
t>0
and
x\in\Omega
, the supremum in (
35
) may be taken over the set

B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})
.

(b)

For each
x\in\Omega
,

\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}\leq\frac{|\nabla^{+}h(x)|^{2}}{2}\,.

(c)

The quotient

\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}

is nonnegative and bounded, uniformly in
t>0
and
x\in\Omega
.

Proof.

Let us first show
(a)
. For any
x\preceq y
in
\Omega
and
t>0
, if
|x-y|\geq\sqrt{2Ct}
then

h(y)-\frac{1}{2t}|x-y|^{2}\leq h(x)+C-\frac{1}{2t}2Ct=h(x)\,,

so
y
is irrelevant to the supremum in (
35
). Moreover, by the continuity
of
h
, we may drop
x
itself from the supremum. Thus
y
is irrelevant to the supremum
whenever
y\not\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})
, as claimed.

We now show
(b)
using
(a)
. Let
x\in\Omega
and
t>0
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left\{h(y)-\frac{1}{2t}|x-y|^{2}\right\}-h(x)}{t}

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left\{\frac{h(y)-h(x)}{t}-\frac{1}{2t^{2}}|x-y|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left\{\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{t}-\frac{1}{2t^{2}}|x-y|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left\{\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\frac{|x-y|}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|x-y|}{t}\right)^{2}\right\}\,.

Using the inequality
\alpha\beta-\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}
, we obtain

\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left(\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\right)^{2}\,.

(36)

Using
Lemma

7.7
and the fact that the term inside the supremum is
nonnegative, we conclude that

\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\right)^{2}=\frac{|\nabla^{+}h(x)|^{2}}{2}\,,

as desired. We may also obtain
(c)
from (
36
) as follows.
First note that
\vec{H}_{t}h(x)\geq h(x)
for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in\Omega
. It is standard
that
C^{1}
functions are Lipschitz on compact sets, so
h
is Lipschitz on
\overline{\Omega}
.
Let
M>0
be its Lipschitz constant. Then from (
36
) we conclude that,
for each
x\in\Omega
and
t>0
,

0\leq\frac{\vec{H}_{t}h(x)-h(x)}{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,\sqrt{2Ct})}\left(\frac{M|x-y|}{|x-y|}\right)^{2}=\frac{M^{2}}{2}\,,

which establishes
(c)
.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set. Let
h\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then for each
x\in\Omega
,

|\nabla h(x)|=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}

and

|\nabla^{+}h(x)|=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,.

Proof.

The first part of the statement is known, but let us give a proof for completeness. Let
x\in\Omega
. We have

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{h(y)-h(x)}{|x-y|}=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{h(y)-h(x)-\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)+\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)}{|x-y|}\,.

By definition of derivative, we have

\lim_{y\to x}\frac{\left\lvert h(y)-h(x)-\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)\right\rvert}{|x-y|}=0\,,

and hence, by
\ell^{2}
norm duality,

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{h(y)-h(x)}{|x-y|}=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)}{|x-y|}=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|v|=1}\nabla h(x)^{\top}v=|\nabla h(x)|\,.

Similarly, we also have

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\left\lvert h(y)-h(x)\right\rvert}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\left\lvert h(y)-h(x)-\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)+\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)\right\rvert}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\left\lvert h(y)-h(x)-\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)\right\rvert}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\left\lvert\nabla h(x)^{\top}(y-x)\right\rvert}{|x-y|}=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|v|=1}\left\lvert\nabla h(x)^{\top}v\right\rvert=|\nabla h(x)|\,.

Since
h(y)-h(x)\leq(h(y)-h(x))^{+}\leq\left\lvert h(y)-h(x)\right\rvert
, the first part of the statement
follows.

We now establish the second part. Write the partition
[d]=P\cup N
for

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{i\in[d]:\partial_{i}h(x)>0\}\,,

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{i\in[d]:\partial_{i}h(x)\leq 0\}\,.

Assume for a moment that
P\neq\emptyset
11
11

11

We track the edge case
P=\emptyset
as we
go along the proof; the main idea is the same.
​​. Define
\Omega^{P}\subset\mathbb{R}^{P}
by

\Omega^{P}\vcentcolon=\{y_{P}:y\in\Omega\text{ satisfies }\supp(y-x)\subseteq P\}\,,

and let
h^{P}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega^{P}})
be given by
h^{P}(y_{P})\vcentcolon=h(y_{P},x_{-P})
for
each
y_{P}\in\Omega_{P}
, i. e.
h^{P}
is the restriction of
h
to the space obtained by fixing the

N
-coordinates of inputs to those of
x
. Note that
\partial_{i}h(x)=\partial_{i}h^{P}(x_{P})
for
each
i\in P
, and hence
|\nabla^{+}h(x)|=|\nabla h^{P}(x_{P})|
. Applying the first part of the
statement to
\Omega^{P}
,
h^{P}
and
x_{P}
gives

|\nabla^{+}h(x)|=|\nabla h^{P}(x_{P})|=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y_{P}\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega^{P}}(x_{P},r)}\frac{(h^{P}(y_{P})-h^{P}(x_{P}))^{+}}{|x_{P}-y_{P}|}

(37)

We claim that

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y_{P}\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega^{P}}(x_{P},r)}\frac{(h^{P}(y_{P})-h^{P}(x_{P}))^{+}}{|x_{P}-y_{P}|}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{=}}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,,

(38)

with the LHS replaced by
0
if
P=\emptyset
, which will conclude the proof.

For each set
A\subseteq[d]
, let
X_{A}\vcentcolon=\{y\in\Omega:y_{A}\succeq x_{A}\}
and
Y_{A}\vcentcolon=\{y\in\Omega:y_{A}\preceq x_{A}\}
. Note that for all
r>0
,
B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)=B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}\cap X_{N}
and moreover, defining
B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\vcentcolon=B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{N}\cap Y_{N}
, when
P\neq\emptyset
we have

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y_{P}\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega^{P}}(x_{P},r)}\frac{(h^{P}(y_{P})-h^{P}(x_{P}))^{+}}{|x_{P}-y_{P}|}=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}

(39)

by definition of the objects involved.

Note that, when
P\neq\emptyset
,
\min_{i\in P}\partial_{i}h(x)>0
by the definition of

P
. Since
h
is continuously differentiable, it follows that there exists
r_{0}>0
such that,
for all
y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r_{0})
and every
i\in P
, we have
\partial_{i}h(y)>0
. Since

\Omega
is open, we can also let
r_{0}
be small enough so that
B(x,r_{0})\subset\Omega
.

Now, suppose
y\in B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r_{0})
and
i\in P
are such that
y_{i}<x_{i}
. Then define

z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
z_{i}\vcentcolon=y_{i}+2(x_{i}-y_{i})>x_{i}
, and
z_{j}\vcentcolon=y_{j}
for
j\in[d]\setminus\{i\}
. Then
|x-z|=|x-y|
, and in particular
z\in B^{\circ}(x,r_{0})\subset\Omega
.
Also, since
i\in P
and
z
agrees with
y
on all
j\neq i
, we have that
y\in X_{N}
(resp.

Y_{N}
) if and only if
z\in X_{N}
(resp.
Y_{N}
). Moreover, since
\partial_{i}h(w)>0
for all

w
in the line segment connecting
y
and
z
(which is contained in
B^{\circ}_{\Omega}(x,r_{0})\cup\{x\}
), the fundamental theorem of calculus implies that
h(z)>h(y)
.

Repeating this argument inductively for each index
i\in P
for which
y_{i}<x_{i}
, we conclude
that for all
r\in(0,r_{0})
and
y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)
, there exists
z\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}
such that
|x-z|=|x-y|
and
h(z)>h(y)
. It follows that, when
P\neq\emptyset
,

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,.

(40)

Combining (
38
), (
39
) and (
40
), we see that it
remains to show that

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\mbox{\tiny?}}}}{{=}}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,,

(41)

again with the LHS replaced by
0
if
P=\emptyset
. Note that for each
r>0
it holds by
definition that
B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}\subseteq B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)
, namely
B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}=B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap Y_{N}
. Thus we already have

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\leq\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,,

which also holds when the LHS is replaced by
0
, and it remains to prove the reverse
inequality. Let
\varepsilon>0
. As before, recalling that
\partial_{i}h(x)\leq 0
for every
i\in N
and using the fact that
h
is continuously differentiable, we can let
r_{0}>0
be small
enough so that for all
y\in B^{\circ}(x,r_{0})
and every
i\in N
, we have
\partial_{i}h(y)\leq\varepsilon
. Again since
\Omega
is open, we may also let
r_{0}
be small enough so that
B(x,r_{0})\subset\Omega
.

Now, let
r\in(0,r_{0})
and
y\in B_{\Omega}^{+}(x,r)
. Define
y^{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as follows:
y^{P}_{i}\vcentcolon=y_{i}
for each
i\in P
, and
y^{P}_{j}\vcentcolon=x_{j}
for each
j\in N
. Then
\supp(y^{P}-x)\subseteq P
by construction, so
y^{P}\in X_{N}\cap Y_{N}
. We also have
y^{P}\succeq x
and
|x-y^{P}|\leq|x-y|
, and hence
y^{P}\in(B^{+}(x,r)\cap Y_{N})\cup\{x\}=(B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P})\cup\{x\}
.

By a standard multivariate version of the mean value theorem, there exists a point
z
in the
line segment connecting
y^{P}
and
y
such that

h(y)-h(y^{P})=\nabla h(z)^{\top}(y-y^{P})\,.

By construction, we have
\supp(y-y^{P})\subseteq N
. Moreover, since
y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)

and hence
y\succeq x
, while
y^{P}_{j}=x_{j}
for each
j\in N
, we have that
y-y^{P}\succeq\vec{0}
. Finally, it is clear from the construction of
y^{P}
that
|y-y^{P}|\leq|x-y|
. We
conclude that

\displaystyle h(y)-h(y^{P})

\displaystyle=\nabla h(z)^{\top}(y-y^{P})

\displaystyle\leq\nabla^{+}h(z)^{\top}(y-y^{P})

(Since
y-y^{P}\succeq\vec{0}
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in N}(\partial_{i}^{+}h(z))(y_{i}-y^{P}_{i})

(Since
\supp(y-y^{P})\subseteq N
)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\in N}(\partial_{i}^{+}h(z))^{2}\right)^{1/2}|y-y^{P}|

(Cauchy-Schwarz inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\in N}\varepsilon^{2}\right)^{1/2}|x-y|

(Choice of
r_{0}
and last observation)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{d}|x-y|\,.

Therefore, when
P\neq\emptyset
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)}\frac{\left(h(y^{P})-h(x)+\varepsilon\sqrt{d}|x-y|\right)^{+}}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{d}+\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\sup_{y\in B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P}}\frac{(h(y)-h(x))^{+}}{|x-y|}\,,

the second inequality since
|x-y^{P}|\leq|x-y|
and since we showed that, for all
r<r_{0}
and

y\in B^{+}_{\Omega}(x,r)
, we have
y^{P}\in(B^{\prime}_{\Omega}(x,r)\cap X_{P})\cup\{x\}
, and moreover

(h(y^{P})-h(x))^{+}=0
when
y^{P}=x
. Similarly, when
P=\emptyset
we have
y^{P}=x

always, so in this case we obtain the upper bound
\varepsilon\sqrt{d}
, i. e. the last limit
superior may indeed be replaced by
0
. Since this inequality holds for every
\varepsilon>0
,
(
41
) follows, and this concludes the proof.
∎

7.2
Perturbation argument

We can now obtain the main result, first for
C^{1}([0,1]^{d})
functions and then for all of

H^{1}((0,1)^{d})
by an approximation argument. The key idea of the perturbation argument is to use the
same (mean zero) function
h
in two different roles: 1) to construct absolutely continuous
probability measures from
h
and its coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium, namely
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}=(1+th^{*})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, for small
t>0
; and 2) in the “test
function”
-th
for weak duality on
W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})
via the directed Hamilton-Jacobi
operator.

Theorem 7.8
.

There exists a universal constant
C>0
such that the following holds. Define
\Omega\vcentcolon=(0,1)^{d}
, and let
h\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then

\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(h)^{2}\leq\int_{\Omega}(h-h^{*})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}h|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

where
h^{*}
is the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium of
h
.

Proof.

Note that
h^{*}
is well-defined because
C^{1}
functions are Lipschitz on compact sets. We first
show that we may assume without loss of generality that
h
is bounded and has mean zero. For
any
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
\alpha g+\beta
is monotone if and only if

g
is monotone, so it follows that

\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(\alpha h+\beta)^{2}=\alpha^{2}\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(h)^{2}\,.

Moreover,
Observation

6.47
gives that
(\alpha h+\beta)^{*}=\alpha h^{*}+\beta
, and clearly
|\nabla^{-}(\alpha h+\beta)|^{2}=\alpha^{2}|\nabla^{-}h|^{2}
pointwise. Thus as
long as we show the result for
h
satisfying
\int_{\Omega}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
and (say)
-0.1\leq h\leq 0.1
, then we may write any other function in
C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
as
\alpha h+\beta

for some
h
satisfying these conditions, and conclude the result. Hence assume that

\int_{\Omega}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
and that
-0.1\leq h\leq 0.1
pointwise.

Let
t\in(0,1)
, and define
f\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
by
f\vcentcolon=1+th
. Note that

f
is Lipschitz and bounded between
0.9
and
1.1
, and it satisfies
\int_{\Omega}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=1
. Define the absolutely continuous probability measures
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\vcentcolon=f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and

\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}\vcentcolon=f^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
.
Section

1.2.4
then implies that, for some
universal constant
C>0
,

W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})\leq C\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{-}f\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=Ct^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{-}h\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=Ct^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

the first equality by the definition of
f
and the fact that
t>0
. On the other hand, noting
that
-th\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
and hence
-th\in C_{b}(\Omega)
as well, we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}(-th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu^{*}-\int_{\Omega}(\vec{H}_{1}(-th))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu

(
Proposition

7.4
)

\displaystyle=-t\int_{\Omega}h(1+th)^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}(\vec{H}_{1}(-th))(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(Definition of
\mu,\mu^{*}
).

Recalling that
\int_{\Omega}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0
and that
(1+th)^{*}=1+th^{*}
by

Observation

6.47
, the first term in the last line above is

-t\int_{\Omega}h(1+th)^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-t\int_{\Omega}h(1+th^{*})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=-t^{2}\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Recalling that
1+th
is bounded between
0.9
and
1.1
, the second term is

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}(\vec{H}_{1}(-th))(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad=-\int_{\Omega}(t\vec{H}_{t}(-h))(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(
Lemma

7.5
)

\displaystyle\qquad=-t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)-h}{t}\right)(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad=-t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\underbrace{\left(\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\right)}_{\geq 0\text{ by \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{prop:properties-h}\ref*{item:h-c}}}\underbrace{(1+th)}_{\leq 2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+t\int_{\Omega}h(1+th)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-2t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+t\underbrace{\int_{\Omega}h\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}_{=0}+t^{2}\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad=-2t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+t^{2}\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Putting all of the above together, we conclude that

\tfrac{C}{2}t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\geq\tfrac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu\to\mu^{*})\geq-t^{2}\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-2t^{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+t^{2}\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

and hence, since
t>0
,

\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\tfrac{C}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{\Omega}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Since this holds for all sufficiently small
t>0
, we may pass the inequality to the limit
superior as
t\to 0^{+}
. Since
\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}
is uniformly bounded by

Proposition

7.6
(c)
, we may apply the reverse Fatou lemma and

Proposition

7.6
(b)
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{C}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+2\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\vec{H}_{t}(-h)-(-h)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{C}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+2\int_{\Omega}\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{(\vec{H}_{t}(-h))(x)-(-h)(x)}{t}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{C}{2}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{+}(-h)\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{+}(-h)(x)|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\left(1+\tfrac{C}{2}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{-}h\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

By
Observation

6.48
, we have that
\int_{\Omega}(h^{*})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
and hence, since
h^{*}
is monotone,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(h)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(h-h^{*})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}(h^{*})^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega}h^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}hh^{*}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(1+\tfrac{C}{2}\right)\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{-}h\right|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

The following denseness result is an immediate application of
[
Maz11
, Theorem 1, p. 10]
.

Fact 7.9
.

Define
\Omega\vcentcolon=(0,1)^{d}
. The space
C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
is dense in

H^{1}(\Omega)
.

Thus we may promote the main result to all of
H^{1}(\Omega)
by an approximation argument:

See
1.1

Proof.

By
Fact

7.9
, we may find a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
such that
f_{n}\to f
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Since each
f_{n}
belongs in
particular to
C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
,
Theorem

7.8
gives that, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|f_{n}-f_{n}^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Since
f_{n}\to f
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
implies in particular that
f_{n}\to f
in

L^{2}(\Omega)
, we have that, for all sufficiently large
n
,
\|f-f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon
and hence, by the triangle inequality,

\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(f)\leq\|f-f_{n}^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|f-f_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|f_{n}-f_{n}^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon+\sqrt{C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\,.

(42)

Now, since the norm in
H^{1}(\Omega)
is given by

\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{i}u|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,,

the fact that
f_{n}\to f
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
implies that
\|f-f_{n}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\to 0
and
hence

0=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{i}(f-f_{n})|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|(\partial_{i}f)-(\partial_{i}f_{n})|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Since
0\leq|(a\land 0)-(b\land 0)|\leq|a-b|
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we conclude that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|(\partial^{-}_{i}f)-(\partial^{-}_{i}f_{n})|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0\,.

(43)

Observing that
\partial^{-}_{i}f,\partial^{-}_{i}f_{n}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
for each
i\in[d]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
(because
\partial_{i}f,\partial_{i}f_{n}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
by the definition of

H^{1}(\Omega)
, and if
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
then
u\land 0\in L^{2}(\Omega)
),
(
43
) implies that
\partial^{-}_{i}f_{n}\to\partial^{-}_{i}f
in

L^{2}(\Omega)
for each
i\in[d]
, and hence

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|\partial^{-}_{i}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\Omega}|\partial^{-}_{i}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Thus, for all sufficiently large
n
we have

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\varepsilon+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Combining with (
42
) we obtain, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(f)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\varepsilon+\sqrt{C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x}\right)^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}+2C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f_{n}|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}+2C\left(\varepsilon+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)=2\varepsilon^{2}+2C\varepsilon+2C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.

Since this holds for all
\varepsilon>0
, we conclude that

\mathsf{dist}^{\mathsf{mono}}_{2}(f)^{2}\leq 2C\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{-}f|^{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,.\qed

Appendix A
Technical lemmas

Lemma A.1
.

Define
\Omega\vcentcolon=(0,1)^{d}
. Suppose
f_{n}\rightharpoonup f
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and each
f_{n}

is monotone nondecreasing (resp. monotone nonincreasing). Then
f
is monotone nondecreasing
(resp. monotone nonincreasing).

Proof.

Recall that for each
\varepsilon>0
and
g\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
g^{\varepsilon}=\eta_{\varepsilon}*g
is
the mollification of
g
, defined on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon)^{d}
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/3)
. We claim that
f_{n}^{\varepsilon}\to f^{\varepsilon}
pointwise in

\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed, fix any
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and define
\eta_{\varepsilon,x}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
\eta_{\varepsilon,x}(y)\vcentcolon=\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)
. Since
f_{n}\rightharpoonup f

weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, we obtain

f^{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\Omega}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)f(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\left\langle\eta_{\varepsilon,x},f\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\langle\eta_{\varepsilon,x},f_{n}\right\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\eta_{\varepsilon}(x-y)f_{n}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}^{\varepsilon}(x)\,.

Now, suppose without loss of generality that each
f_{n}
is monotone nondecreasing. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/3)
. It is immediate that each
f_{n}^{\varepsilon}
is also monotone nondecreasing (in

\Omega_{\varepsilon}
). Since
f_{n}^{\varepsilon}\to f^{\varepsilon}
pointwise in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, we
conclude that
f^{\varepsilon}
is monotone nondecreasing as well.

The conclusion follows the fact that
f^{\varepsilon}\to f
almost everywhere as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

The following lemma is essentially standard, and below we present the proof sketched in

[
Fen
]
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open set, let
u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

be a bounded sequence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. Then
u_{n}\rightharpoonup u
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that every subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
has a subsequence that weakly
converges to
u
. Fix any subsequence
(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. First, since
u_{n_{k}}\to u
in

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, we have that
\left\langle u_{n_{k}},\phi\right\rangle\to\left\langle u,\phi\right\rangle
for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
. Moreover, since
L^{2}(\Omega)
is a Hilbert space and
(u_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded, let
(u_{n_{k_{\ell}}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
be a subsequence such that

u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup w
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
for some
w\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Now, let
v\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Since
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, let
(\phi_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

be a sequence in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
with
\phi_{m}\to v
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. Then

\displaystyle\left\langle u,v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\left\langle u,\phi_{m}\right\rangle

(Since
\phi_{m}\to v
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{k\to\infty}\left\langle u_{n_{k}},\phi_{m}\right\rangle

(As observed above)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left\langle u_{n_{k_{\ell}}},\phi_{m}\right\rangle

(Taking subsequence preserves the limit)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to\infty}\left\langle w,\phi_{m}\right\rangle

(Since
u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup w
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
)

\displaystyle=\left\langle w,v\right\rangle

(Since
\phi_{m}\to v
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\left\langle u_{n_{k_{\ell}}},v\right\rangle

(Since
u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup w
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
),

so
u_{n_{k_{\ell}}}\rightharpoonup u
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega)
as needed.
∎

Lemma A.3

(From “almost Lipschitz” to Lipschitz)
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and let
N\subset I
be a measure zero set. Suppose
f:I\to\mathbb{R}

satisfies
\left\lvert f(x)-f(y)\right\rvert\leq M|x-y|
for all
x,y\in I\setminus N
. Then there exists a

M
-Lipschitz function
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f=g
in
I\setminus N
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{f}:I\to(-\infty,+\infty]
be given by
\overline{f}\vcentcolon=f
in
I\setminus N
and
\overline{f}\vcentcolon=+\infty
in
N
. Define
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
by

g(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}f(x)&\text{if }x\in I\setminus N\\
\liminf_{z\to x}\overline{f}(z)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
g
is real-valued because, when
x\in N
, we may find a sequence
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset I\setminus N
such that
z_{n}\to x
and then, by the definition of
\liminf
by
subsequential limits and the assumption on
f
,

g(x)=\liminf_{z\to x}\overline{f}(z)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\overline{f}(z_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(z_{n})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\big[f(z_{1})+M|z_{n}-z_{1}|\big]=f(z_{1})+M|x-z_{1}|<+\infty\,.

Clearly
g=f
in
I\setminus N
. We claim that
g
is
M
-Lipschitz. By the assumption on

f
, we do have
\left\lvert g(x)-g(y)\right\rvert\leq M|x-y|
for all
x,y\in I\setminus N
. Now, let
x\in N
and
y\in I\setminus N
. By the definition of
g
, fix a sequence
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset I\setminus N
such that
z_{n}\to x
and moreover
\lim_{n\to\infty}f(z_{n})=g(x)
.
Then

\left\lvert g(x)-g(y)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\lim_{n\to\infty}f(z_{n})-f(y)\right\rvert=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\lvert f(z_{n})-f(y)\right\rvert\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}M|z_{n}-y|=M|x-y|\,,

as desired. The case when
x,y\in N
follows by the triangle inequality: suppose
x<y

without loss of generality, choose any
z\in(x,y)\setminus N
, which is possible since
N

has measure zero, and use the above to conclude that

\left\lvert g(x)-g(y)\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert g(x)-g(z)\right\rvert+\left\lvert g(z)-g(y)\right\rvert\leq M|x-z|+M|z-y|=M|x-y|\,.\qed
